How the real exam works

Everything in this section comes from the course page of AY 2025-26 and from the three official exam texts of 15 July 2024, 10 June 2025 and 22 June 2026. What they do not say, this section does not say.

Two ways to pass

  • Way 1 — a single live written exam: 33 points at most, 1 h 30 min.
  • Way 2 — a project-based evaluation, valid only for students registered in AY 2025-26: a midterm written exam worth 15 points and a project worth 18.

This page trains you for the written exam of Way 1.

The written exam

  • Closed books. No notes, slides, books or exercises, no laptop and no mobile phone.
  • One A4 page of your own, front side only, with formulas in 12-point type or larger — no copies of past exercises or of slides. The formula sheet of this notebook is the place to choose what goes on it.
  • Two parts. The practice part, 15 points: written exercises and design problems. The theory part, 18 points: open questions and multiple-choice quizzes.

The course page of AY 2025-26 lists three dates for the summer session 2026: 22 June, 17 July and 3 September.

How to practise

Practise the way the paper is taken: closed books, only your own A4 page, a pen and a clock. The exam is written, so write every answer out on paper before you open the model answer — recognising a good answer and producing one are two different skills.

  • Open — write the answer on paper. For an exercise, fill in the whole table or write the whole computation, every intermediate number included; for a theory question, a few short lines per sub-point. Then open "Show model answer" and compare, cell by cell or line by line.
  • Numeric — work it out on paper, type the result into the field under the question and press Check. 0.75, 0,75 and 3/4 are all accepted, with a small tolerance for rounding.
  • Multiple choice — the form of the quizzes. Decide on paper first: a click cannot be taken back until you reload the page. With one correct option the answer is checked as soon as you click; with several, tick every correct one and press Check. The check explains the options you picked and the correct ones; the model answer under the question covers the rest.

Keep an eye on the clock: the paper gives 1 h 30 min for 33 points, about 2.7 minutes a point — some 14 minutes for a 5-point exercise and 22 for an 8-point question.

A question that comes from a past paper, or follows the form of one, names the paper in its wording, as in "(Exercise 1, 15 July 2024)". Under every model answer a commentary says what a complete answer contains and where the usual traps are. When your answer does not match, follow "open in the lesson" above the box: the lesson explains the rule behind every cell, and the commentary sometimes refers to a table or a widget there.

For a mock paper, press "10 random questions" below and write all the answers before you open any model answer. Answers on this page are not saved: after a reload every question is clean again, ready for a second round a week later.

Questions

The questions follow below, module by module and in lesson order. A module opens with its button, or with its line in the list at the top of the page. Untick a question type or a module to leave it out. The button "10 random questions" assembles a mini exam from what is still ticked; every new press first takes questions that have not been in a selection yet.

Random selection

Module 1 · Pipelining and performance

19 questions · Notes, chapters 1 and 4 · the RISC-V pipeline, hazards, forwarding, the metrics every exercise ends with

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by a pipelined RISC-V processor:

  1. Pipelining of the instruction execution
  2. The five-stage organisation IF / ID / EX / MEM / WB
  3. The pipeline registers
  4. Separate instruction and data memories
Show model answer

1. Pipelining is an implementation technique that overlaps the execution of consecutive instructions: the execution is divided into stages and each stage works on a different instruction in the same clock cycle. The latency of one instruction does not change (k cycles), but once the pipeline is full one instruction completes per cycle, so the throughput grows by up to k times; n instructions take k + n − 1 cycles instead of nk, and the clock period becomes the time of one stage instead of a whole instruction. Costs: a fill of k − 1 cycles, pipeline-register overhead, and hazards (structural, data, control) that insert stalls.

2. The five stages correspond to five resources used once by every instruction: IF fetches from the instruction memory and computes PC + 4; ID decodes and reads the two source registers (two read ports); EX uses the ALU for the operation, the address or the branch comparison; MEM accesses the data memory (loads and stores only); WB writes the destination register (one write port). Because each stage uses a different resource, five instructions can be in flight without conflicts; every instruction traverses all five stages (idle stages are not skipped), so completion is in order and at most one instruction writes per cycle.

3. The pipeline registers IF/ID, ID/EX, EX/MEM and MEM/WB sit between the stages and are written on every clock edge. They hold everything an in-flight instruction still needs: the instruction and PC + 4 (IF/ID); the operand values, the immediate, the destination register number and the control signals for EX, MEM and WB (ID/EX); the ALU result or address, the store data, the branch target, rd and the MEM/WB control (EX/MEM); the loaded data or ALU result, rd and the WB control (MEM/WB). Benefits: they isolate the stages so that the clock period equals one stage; the control is decoded once and carried along; and they are the source of the values used by forwarding.

4. With a single memory, the instruction fetch of one instruction and the data access of another (IF and MEM of two different instructions in the same cycle) would compete for the memory port: a structural hazard with a stall every time. Separate instruction and data memories (in practice separate L1 caches) give the two stages their own port, so the hazard is designed away. It is a property of the implementation, not of the instruction set.

The 2026 paper asks its 8-point question in exactly this form: "Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques", with a few lines per sub-point. Fill them with the key words — overlap, throughput versus latency, k + n − 1, one resource per stage, the four register names with their contents, "structural hazard" for the memory — and with a cost next to each benefit. A sub-point that only says what the thing is called says nothing.

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the five-stage RISC-V pipeline (IF, ID, EX, MEM, WB) with one clock cycle per stage and no hazards (ideal case). Complete the pipeline diagram for one iteration of the following loop, then give the total number of clock cycles and the execution time with a 2 ns clock. How many cycles would the same five instructions take on a non-pipelined machine that executes the five stages one after another?

LOOP:  LD    F2, 0(R1)
       FADD  F4, F2, F0
       SD    F4, 0(R1)
       ADDUI R1, R1, 8
       BNE   R1, R2, LOOP
Show model answer
C1C2C3C4C5C6C7C8C9
I0 LD F2, 0(R1)IFIDEXMEMWB
I1 FADD F4, F2, F0IFIDEXMEMWB
I2 SD F4, 0(R1)IFIDEXMEMWB
I3 ADDUI R1, R1, 8IFIDEXMEMWB
I4 BNE R1, R2, LOOPIFIDEXMEMWB

Total clock cycles = k + n − 1 = 5 + 5 − 1 = 9 (equivalently IC + 4 = 5 + 4 with no stalls); execution time = 9 × 2 ns = 18 ns. Non-pipelined: 5 × 5 = 25 cycles = 50 ns. Speedup of the pipeline = 50 / 18 = 2.78.

Every row has all five stages, the idle ones included — a row that stops at EX for the FADD or at MEM for the SD is wrong. The diagonal must shift by exactly one column per instruction, and the cycle count must be read from the last WB (C9), not guessed as "5 instructions, 5 cycles".

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

An ideal five-stage pipeline with a 2 ns clock executes 100 independent instructions. How many nanoseconds does the execution take?

Show model answer
\[ T_{\text{pipe}} = (k + n - 1)\,T \] \[ = (5 + 100 - 1) \times 2 = 208 \text{ ns} \]

Against 1000 ns without the pipeline: a speedup of 1000 / 208 = 4.81.

The tempting wrong answer is 200 ns (one instruction per cycle, no fill). The four extra cycles are the fill of the five-stage pipeline, the "+4" of the course's cycle formula.

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

A non-pipelined processor executes one instruction in 10 ns. A designer splits that work evenly into 10 pipeline stages, but every stage adds 0.5 ns of pipeline-register overhead to the clock period. What is the maximum speedup over the non-pipelined processor, for a very long program of independent instructions? Give two decimals.

Show model answer

Stage time = 10 / 10 = 1 ns; clock period = 1 + 0.5 = 1.5 ns. For n → ∞ the pipeline completes one instruction per clock period, so the speedup tends to 10 / 1.5 = 6.67 — not 10.

This is the "why deeper is not always faster" question. The expected reasoning is: ceiling = time of one instruction / clock period, and the clock period = slowest stage + register overhead. A five-stage pipeline with the same overhead has a ceiling of 10 / 2.5 = 4, so the deeper pipeline is still better here — but by 1.67×, not 2×.

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about an ideal five-stage pipeline is correct? (Single answer)

  • Each instruction completes in one clock cycle. Each instruction still takes five cycles from fetch to write-back; only the rate of completion changes.
  • Once the pipeline is full, one instruction completes per cycle, but each instruction still takes five cycles. Throughput of one per cycle, latency of five cycles.
  • The latency of each instruction is reduced to one fifth. The latency is unchanged (slightly larger with the register overhead). Pipelining improves throughput, not latency.
  • Five instructions always take exactly five cycles. They take 5 + 5 − 1 = 9 cycles; the first instruction must traverse the whole pipeline before anything completes.
Show model answer

Answer 2. The pipeline overlaps instructions: the time of one instruction is still k cycles, but a new one completes every cycle once the pipeline is full, so n instructions take k + n − 1 cycles.

The quiz tests the latency/throughput distinction — the first bullet of the notes' p. 14. Options 1 and 3 are the same misconception in two forms.

Lesson 1 · The assembly line in silicon · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements are true for the basic five-stage RISC-V pipeline? (Multiple answers)

  • An ALU instruction passes through the MEM stage without using it. The stage is traversed idle, so that every instruction keeps a five-cycle latency.
  • A store skips the WB stage and completes one cycle earlier than a load. A store has nothing to write in WB but still spends the cycle there; no stage is skipped.
  • The destination register number travels in the pipeline registers together with the instruction. rd is copied from ID/EX to EX/MEM to MEM/WB, because the instruction word is no longer available when WB happens.
  • The branch updates the PC in the WB stage. The comparison is in EX and the PC is loaded in MEM (basic pipeline); optimised pipelines decide in ID.
  • The value loaded by a load instruction is available only at the end of its MEM stage. EX computes the address, MEM reads the memory; the value does not exist before the end of MEM.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 3 and 5.

Options 2 and 4 are the two claims of the notes corrected in this lesson (pp. 16–17). The 2026 paper uses "Multiple Choice – Multiple answer" quizzes of exactly this shape.

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used in a pipelined RISC-V processor:

  1. The split-cycle register file (write in the first half of the clock cycle, read in the second half).
  2. The forwarding paths EX/EX, MEM/EX and MEM/MEM and the forwarding unit.
  3. The load-use hazard and the hazard detection unit.
  4. Static versus dynamic solutions to RAW data hazards.
Show model answer

1. The register file is written in the first half of a clock cycle and read in the second half, so an instruction in WB and an instruction in ID exchange a value within the same cycle. Benefit: a consumer three instructions after its producer needs no stall and no extra datapath; without it a consumer at distance 1, 2 or 3 would pay one more stall. It is the "read and write at the same address in the same cycle" assumption of the exercises.

2. Forwarding (bypassing) feeds a result to a dependent instruction from the pipeline register where it already sits, instead of waiting for WB and a register-file read. EX/EX takes the ALU result from EX/MEM into the ALU input (ALU producer at distance 1); MEM/EX takes a value from MEM/WB into the ALU input (ALU producer at distance 2, or a loaded value); MEM/MEM takes a loaded value from MEM/WB into the data-memory write input (load followed by a store). The forwarding unit compares the destination register numbers in EX/MEM and MEM/WB with the source numbers in ID/EX, gives priority to the newest result and ignores R0. Benefit: all ALU-to-ALU stalls disappear; cost: multiplexers and comparators.

3. A load's value exists only at the end of its MEM stage, so an instruction that uses it immediately would need it in the same cycle: one stall is unavoidable even with forwarding, after which the MEM/EX path delivers the value. The hazard detection unit, in ID, detects the case (the instruction in EX is a load whose destination equals a source the instruction in ID needs in EX; the data register of a store is exempt, since the MEM/MEM path serves it in MEM) and inserts the bubble: it holds the PC and the IF/ID register and zeroes the control signals entering ID/EX. Benefit: correctness with the minimum delay; the compiler can fill the slot with an independent instruction.

4. Static solutions are applied by the compiler: nop insertion (simple, wastes the same cycles as a stall and inflates code and instruction count) and instruction scheduling (fills the gap with independent instructions, free when they exist). Dynamic solutions are applied by the hardware at run time: stalling through interlocks (bubbles, no code growth) and forwarding (the preferred one). Benefit of dynamic solutions: the code is independent of the pipeline depth; benefit of static ones: no detection hardware and the ability to fill slots forwarding cannot remove.

This is the shape of the 8-point question of the June 2026 paper: a few lines per sub-point, each with the concept, the mechanism and the benefit. The examiner is checking the vocabulary (pipeline registers, ID/EX, EX/MEM, MEM/WB, bypass, interlock, bubble), the cost next to the benefit, and the two facts that separate a good answer from a vague one: the forwarding unit compares register numbers, and load-use keeps one stall.

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the 5-stage RISC-V pipeline (IF, ID, EX, MEM, WB) with a register file that is written in the first half of the clock cycle and read in the second half. Consider the following code:

I0   LD   R2, 0(R1)
I1   ADD  R3, R2, R4
I2   SUB  R5, R3, R6
I3   SD   R5, 8(R1)
I4   OR   R7, R2, R5

(a) Complete the cycle-by-cycle table without forwarding. (b) Complete it with the EX/EX, MEM/EX and MEM/MEM forwarding paths. For each case give the stall cycles, the total clock cycles (IC + 4 + stalls) and the CPI. (c) Compute the speed-up of (b) over (a).

Show model answer

(a) Without forwarding. RAW on R2 (I0 to I1), R3 (I1 to I2) and R5 (I2 to I3), all at distance 1: 2 stalls each. I4 reads R2 and R5 in cycle 12, both already written: 0 stalls.

123456789101112131415
I0 LD R2, 0(R1)IFIDEXMEMWB
I1 ADD R3, R2, R4IFIDstallstallEXMEMWB
I2 SUB R5, R3, R6IFIFIFIDstallstallEXMEMWB
I3 SD R5, 8(R1)IFIFIFIDstallstallEXMEMWB
I4 OR R7, R2, R5IFIFIFIDEXMEMWB

Stalls = 6; clock cycles = 5 + 4 + 6 = 15 (the cycle of the last WB); CPI = 15 / 5 = 3.0.

(b) With forwarding. I0 to I1 is a load-use pair: 1 stall, then R2 through the MEM/EX path in cycle 5. I1 to I2: EX/EX path in cycle 6, 0 stalls. I2 to I3: R5 forwarded to the store through the EX/EX path in cycle 7, 0 stalls. I4: R5 through the MEM/EX path in cycle 8, R2 from the register file, 0 stalls.

12345678910
I0 LD R2, 0(R1)IFIDEXMEMWB
I1 ADD R3, R2, R4IFIDstallEXMEMWB
I2 SUB R5, R3, R6IFIFIDEXMEMWB
I3 SD R5, 8(R1)IFIDEXMEMWB
I4 OR R7, R2, R5IFIDEXMEMWB

Stalls = 1; clock cycles = 5 + 4 + 1 = 10; CPI = 10 / 5 = 2.0.

(c) Speed-up = 15 / 10 = 1.5 (the CPI drops from 3.0 to 2.0).

Not one of the official papers' exercises: the papers of 2024–2026 test this material through the dependence table of Exercise 1, the CPI line of every table and the quizzes. The form is that of the notes' Example 2 and Figure 9 and of the textbook. What is checked: the stalls per pair from the distance rule, the frozen IF cells of the instruction behind a stall, the one surviving load-use stall, and a total that equals the cycle of the last WB. Writing "S" (or the notes' "ID-Stallo" / "IF-Stallo") for a held cell gives the same cycle count.

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

The code SUB R2, R1, R3 / AND R12, R2, R5 / OR R13, R6, R2 / ADD R14, R2, R2 / SD R15, 100(R2) runs on the 5-stage pipeline without forwarding, with the split-cycle register file. How many clock cycles does it take, counting the pipeline fill?

Show model answer

Only AND stalls (distance 1 from SUB: 2 stalls); OR is held in IF behind it and reads R2 in cycle 6, after the write in cycle 5; ADD and SD are later still. Clock cycles = IC + 4 + stalls = 5 + 4 + 2 = 11.

The trap is to charge OR for its own distance-2 dependence (one stall by the rule) — but the rule assumes the consumer was not already delayed. The stall of AND has pushed OR past the write. Count on the table, not on the rule alone.

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

On a 5-stage pipeline with full forwarding (EX/EX, MEM/EX, MEM/MEM) and a split-cycle register file, how many stall cycles does LD R5, 0(R9) immediately followed by ADD R6, R5, R5 cause? (Single answer.)

  • 0 Forwarding cannot deliver a value that does not exist yet: the load's data appears at the end of its MEM stage, in the same cycle as the add's EX would run.
  • 1 One bubble, then the MEM/EX path hands the loaded value from MEM/WB to the ALU.
  • 2 Two stalls is the cost without forwarding; the MEM/EX path saves one of them.
  • 3 Three stalls would mean waiting for the register file without the split-cycle trick; both mechanisms exist here.
Show model answer

Answer 2: 1 stall. The loaded value exists only at the end of the load's MEM stage; the add's EX is delayed one cycle and then takes the value through the MEM/EX path.

A single-answer quiz in the form of the papers' quiz block (none of the 2024–2026 papers asks this exact pair, but it is the one fact of the chapter a quiz can test in one line). The distractor 0 is the notes' "forwarding eliminates stalls".

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which hazards can occur in the basic in-order 5-stage RISC-V pipeline with separate instruction and data memories? (Multiple answers.)

  • RAW data hazards A consumer can reach ID before its producer has reached WB.
  • WAR data hazards Every read happens in ID, three stages before any younger instruction's WB.
  • WAW data hazards All writes go through the single WB stage in program order.
  • Control hazards The next PC is not known when the instruction after a branch should be fetched.
  • Structural hazards on memory Separate instruction and data memories let IF and MEM of two instructions proceed in the same cycle.
Show model answer

Answers 1 and 4. Reads in ID precede any later write in WB and all writes are in order through one WB stage, so WAR and WAW are impossible; split memories remove the IF/MEM conflict; RAW and control hazards remain.

A multiple-answer quiz in the form of the June 2026 paper. The notes' "WAR … rare in RISC" is the distractor to resist.

Lesson 2 · When the line jams: hazards and forwarding · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

A compiler targets a 5-stage pipeline with no forwarding and no interlocks, but with the split-cycle register file. How many NOP instructions must it insert between SUB R2, R1, R3 and AND R12, R2, R5 for the AND to read the new R2? Motivate your answer. (Single answer.)

  • 0 Without forwarding the AND would read R2 in cycle 3 while SUB computes it; zero slots is the forwarding answer.
  • 1 The notes' Example 3; with one slot the AND reads in cycle 4 and SUB writes in cycle 5: stale value.
  • 2 With two slots the AND is the third instruction after SUB and reads R2 in cycle 5, the cycle of SUB's WB; the split-cycle file makes that read see the new value.
  • 3 Three slots would be needed only without the split-cycle register file, when the read must come the cycle after the write.
Show model answer

Answer 3: 2 NOPs. Motivation: SUB writes R2 in WB in cycle 5. The consumer at distance \(d\) reads in cycle \(d + 2\); the read must be in cycle 5 or later, so \(d = 3\): two instructions in between. Stalls = max(0, 3 − d) = 0.

The "motivate your answer" line is where the second point is: write the two cycle numbers (write in 5, read in d + 2), not only the count.

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

PERFORMANCE EVALUATION. Given CPU1 with clock frequency 500 MHz and CPU2 with clock frequency 200 MHz, let's consider the two CPUs execute a kernel of 100 instructions with the following frequencies of occurrence and clock cycles:

Instruction TypeInstruction FrequencyCPU1 Clock CyclesCPU2 Clock Cycles
ALU30%21
LOAD10%86
STORE20%23
JUMP30%22
BRANCH10%33

How much is the average CPI for each CPU? CPI1 = … CPI2 = … How much is the CPU time for each CPU? CPUtime1 = … CPUtime2 = … How much is CPU1 faster than CPU2?

Show model answer

CPI1 = 0.3 × 2 + 0.1 × 8 + 0.2 × 2 + 0.3 × 2 + 0.1 × 3 = 0.6 + 0.8 + 0.4 + 0.6 + 0.3 = 2.7

CPI2 = 0.3 × 1 + 0.1 × 6 + 0.2 × 3 + 0.3 × 2 + 0.1 × 3 = 0.3 + 0.6 + 0.6 + 0.6 + 0.3 = 2.4

TCLK1 = 1 / 500 MHz = 2 ns; TCLK2 = 1 / 200 MHz = 5 ns.

CPUtime1 = IC × CPI1 × TCLK1 = 100 × 2.7 × 2 ns = 540 ns; CPUtime2 = 100 × 2.4 × 5 ns = 1200 ns.

CPU1 is faster by the ratio of the execution times: 1200 / 540 = 2.22 times faster, i.e. 122% faster (Perf(CPU1) = (1 + 122/100) × Perf(CPU2)), although its CPI is higher: the clock factor 2.5 outweighs the CPI factor 2.7 / 2.4 = 1.125.

Three things earn the points: the two weighted sums written term by term, the conversion of each frequency to a cycle time in ns before the multiplication, and "faster" expressed as the ratio of the times (2.22×, 122%), not as a difference, not as the CPI ratio and not through MIPS. Part 2 of the same exercise, CPU3 with cache misses, needs the memory-stall formula of lesson 24.

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

A loop body of m = 6 instructions is executed n = 200 times on the 5-stage RISC pipeline at 2 GHz; each iteration suffers k = 2 stall cycles. Compute: (a) the clock cycles and the CPI of a single iteration; (b) the total clock cycles of the loop; (c) the CPI of the whole loop and the asymptotic CPI; (d) the asymptotic MIPS; (e) the CPU time of the loop.

Show model answer

(a) Single iteration: cycles = m + k + 4 = 6 + 2 + 4 = 12; CPIiter = 12 / 6 = 2.0.

(b) Whole loop: IC = m × n = 1200; clock cycles = n × (m + k) + 4 = 200 × 8 + 4 = 1604 (the fill is paid once).

(c) CPI = 1604 / 1200 = 1.337; CPIAS = (m + k) / m = 8 / 6 = 1.333.

(d) MIPSAS = fCLK / (CPIAS × 106) = 2000 MHz / 1.333 = 1500 MIPS.

(e) TCLK = 1 / 2 GHz = 0.5 ns; CPU time = 1604 × 0.5 ns = 802 ns.

The classic mistake is to add 4 to every iteration (200 × 12 = 2400 cycles) or to forget it for the single iteration (8 cycles, CPI 1.33). Note how close the exact CPI (1.337) already is to the asymptotic one (1.333) after 200 iterations.

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

An enhancement gives a speedup S = 10 to the part of the execution it affects. Which fraction F of the original execution time must be affected to obtain an overall speedup of 4? Give F as a decimal fraction (three digits).

Show model answer

Speedup = 1 / ((1 − F) + F / S) = 4 ⇒ (1 − F) + F / 10 = 0.25 ⇒ 1 − 0.9 F = 0.25 ⇒ F = 0.75 / 0.9 = 0.833 (83% of the execution time).

This is the 8 January 2026 quiz as the notes reproduce it (single answer, "motivate your answer"; its options were 55%, 83%, 45%, 40% and 25%). Check the answer the way the examiner would: 1 / (1/6 + 1/12) = 1 / (3/12) = 4. The tempting distractors are 40% (the "4/10" guess) and 25% (the reciprocal of the target, 1/4).

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following: 1. the CPU time equation; 2. the weighted average CPI of an instruction mix; 3. MIPS as a performance metric and its limits; 4. Amdahl's law; 5. the pipeline fill and the formula Clock cycles = IC + Stall cycles + 4; 6. the asymptotic CPI of a loop.

Show model answer

1. CPU time = IC × CPI × TCLK = IC × CPI / fCLK: the instructions executed, times the average clock cycles per instruction, times the cycle time. Each factor is set by a different level (compiler and ISA for IC; organisation for CPI; technology and pipeline depth for TCLK) and they interact (a deeper pipeline shortens the cycle but adds stalls). Benefit: it predicts the effect of a design change and makes execution time, the only reliable metric, computable from measurable factors.

2. Total cycles = Σ CPIi × Ii; dividing by IC gives CPI = Σ CPIi × Fi with Fi = Ii / IC the frequency of class i. Benefit: the CPI of a program on a machine from a mix table (e.g. 2.7 vs 2.4), without executing it; it shows which class dominates the time.

3. MIPS = fCLK / (CPI × 106) = IC / (CPU time × 106): millions of instructions per second. Limits: it depends on the instruction set and on the program, and it ignores the work per instruction, so it can vary inversely with performance (software FP: more instructions, higher MIPS, longer time). It is valid only to compare the same program with the same IC; otherwise compare CPU times.

4. If a fraction F of the original execution time is made S times faster, the new time is ((1 − F) + F / S) × old time and the speedup is 1 / ((1 − F) + F / S), bounded by 1 / (1 − F) as S → ∞. Benefit: it tells where to invest ("make the common case fast") and bounds the return of any single enhancement; e.g. F = 0.4, S = 10 gives 1.56, at most 1.67.

5. In a 5-stage pipeline the first instruction completes in cycle 5 and each following one a cycle later, so IC instructions complete in IC + 4 cycles (the 4 fill cycles are the depth minus one, paid once); each stall cycle delays everything behind it by one cycle: Clock cycles = IC + Stall cycles + 4, CPI = cycles / IC, MIPS = fCLK / (CPI × 106). It gives the throughput of pipelined code from a cycle table (e.g. 5 instructions, 2 stalls: 11 cycles, CPI 2.2).

6. For a loop of m instructions and k stall cycles per iteration run n times, cycles = n(m + k) + 4 and CPI(n) = 1 + k/m + 4/(mn) → CPIAS = (m + k)/m = 1 + k/m as n → ∞: the fill becomes negligible and the performance is governed by the stall cycles per instruction, so minimising k (or spreading it over a longer body) is what matters. The ideal CPI 1 is reached only with k = 0.

The paper's open question gives about five lines per sub-point: a definition, the formula with its symbols explained, the benefit, and one number as an example. A concept without its "why" (what the technique buys) loses the point.

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Program P runs in 10 s on machine A and in 15 s on machine B. Which statement is correct? (SINGLE ANSWER)

  • Answer 1: A is 33% faster than B 33% is the time A saves as a fraction of B's time, (15 − 10)/15; "faster" is defined on the ratio of the times.
  • Answer 2: A is 50% faster than B Time(B) = (1 + n/100) × Time(A): 15 = 1.5 × 10, so n = 50.
  • Answer 3: B is 50% faster than A B is the slower machine: it takes more time.
  • Answer 4: A is 1.33 times faster than B The ratio of the times is 15/10 = 1.5, not 1.33.
Show model answer

Answer 2: Perf(A)/Perf(B) = Time(B)/Time(A) = 15/10 = 1.5 = 1 + 50/100.

The notes' Example 2. The "33%" trap is the one distractor the examiner expects to catch people with.

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

On a 5-stage pipeline with no stalls, how many clock cycles do N instructions take? (SINGLE ANSWER)

  • Answer 1: 5N That is the unpipelined count: 5 cycles each, no overlap.
  • Answer 2: N + 4 The first instruction completes in cycle 5, the N-th in cycle N + 4: the fill of depth − 1 cycles is paid once.
  • Answer 3: N Only asymptotically: the fill never disappears, it is diluted.
  • Answer 4: N + 5 Off by one: the fill is depth − 1 = 4 cycles, because the cycle in which the first instruction completes is already one of the N.
Show model answer

Answer 2: Clock cycles = IC + Stall cycles + 4 with Stall cycles = 0.

Fig. 4: five instructions, cycles 1 to 9.

Lesson 3 · What “faster” means: the Iron Law, Amdahl and pipeline arithmetic · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements are correct? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • Answer 1: Pipelining increases the instruction throughput but does not reduce the execution time of a single instruction Every instruction still walks through all the stages; only the overlap changed (notes, p. 253).
  • Answer 2: The ideal CPI of a single-issue 5-stage pipeline is 1 One instruction completes per cycle once the pipeline is full; stalls and the fill push the real CPI above 1.
  • Answer 3: A higher clock frequency always gives a shorter CPU time CPU time = IC × CPI × TCLK: a faster clock can raise the CPI (loads cost more cycles), as in the June 2025 exercise.
  • Answer 4: A 4-issue processor can have a CPI below 1 Its ideal CPI is 1/4 = 0.25; "CPI is always greater than 1" holds for single issue only.
  • Answer 5: With a large enough S, Amdahl's overall speedup can exceed 1 / (1 − F) 1 / (1 − F) is the limit for S → ∞; no S reaches it, let alone exceeds it.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 4.

Two of the three distractors are the "always" statements of this lesson's red pens; the third is the frequency fallacy of the hook.

Module 2 · Branch prediction

20 questions · Notes, chapter 2 · control hazards, static schemes and the delay slot, history tables, correlating predictors

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques for handling branches in a pipelined RISC-V processor: 1. Branch always not taken; 2. Branch always taken with a Branch Target Buffer; 3. Backward taken, forward not taken; 4. Delayed branch with delay-slot scheduling.

Show model answer

1. Branch always not taken. Fetch continues at PC + 4 as if the branch were an ordinary instruction; no hardware and no target address are needed. When the branch is not taken the cost is zero; when it is taken the one instruction fetched after the branch is flushed (turned into a NOP) and fetch restarts at the target: a 1-cycle penalty with the branch solved in ID. Good for if-then-else with a rare else path, bad for loop-closing branches, which are taken almost always.

2. Branch always taken with a BTB. Fetch continues at the target. The target is needed while the branch is still in IF, before decoding, so a Branch Target Buffer — a cache indexed by the address of the branch instruction whose entry holds the predicted target address — supplies it; on a hit the target is fetched in the next cycle. When the branch is not taken the instruction fetched from the target is flushed and fetch resumes at PC + 4 (1 cycle). On a BTB miss the processor falls back to PC + 4 or waits for the target. Good for backward loop branches; without a BTB the scheme has no benefit in a 5-stage pipeline because the target is not known before the outcome.

3. BTFNT. Use the direction of the branch, i.e. the sign of the offset: backward branches (loops) are predicted taken, forward branches (if-then-else, rarely entered paths) not taken. Needs only one bit of the instruction and no learning hardware; better accuracy than either uniform rule on mixed code. Not adaptive: a forward branch that is usually taken is mispredicted most of the time; and the direction is only known after decoding, so the guess is stored in the BTB entry to be usable in IF.

4. Delayed branch. The instruction in the slot after the branch is always executed, whatever the outcome; the branch takes effect after it. The compiler fills the slot with an instruction from before the branch (independent of it: always useful), from the target (useful when taken; must be harmless when not; usually duplicated at the label) or from the fall-through (useful when not taken; harmless when taken). A filled slot hides the 1-cycle bubble of the ID-resolved pipeline; a NOP in the slot is a stall. Benefit: no guessing and no flush logic; drawbacks: the slot ties the ISA to one pipeline depth, deeper or multiple-issue pipelines cannot be covered by one slot, and about half of the slots are not usefully filled — which is why RISC-V has no delay slot.

The 2026 paper gives five lines per sub-point: name the mechanism, say what it costs, what it buys, and when it fails. For point 2 the examiner looks for "indexed by the branch PC" and "target needed in IF"; for point 4 for "always executed" and the three sources with their conditions.

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the following loop executed on a 5-stage pipeline where the branch is solved in the ID stage (misprediction penalty 1 cycle):

INIT: ADDUI R1, R0, 0
      ADDUI R2, R0, 20
LOOP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
      FADD  F2, F0, F0
      SD    F2, 0(R1)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 4
      BNE   R1, R2, LOOP

For (a) branch always not taken, (b) branch always taken with a BTB that always hits, (c) BTFNT, complete the table (prediction, branch outcome, penalty for each iteration), then give the misprediction rate on the BNE and the total branch stall cycles.

Show model answer

The BNE executes with R1 = 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 against R2 = 20: five executions, outcomes T T T T NT.

Iteration1st2nd3rd4th5th
Branch OutcomeTTTTNT
(a) Prediction: always NTNTNTNTNTNT
(a) Penalty11110
(b) Prediction: always TTTTTT
(b) Penalty00001
(c) Prediction: BTFNT (backward → T)TTTTT
(c) Penalty00001

(a) 4 mispredictions of 5 = 80 %, 4 stall cycles. (b) 1 of 5 = 20 %, 1 stall cycle. (c) LOOP is above the BNE, the offset is negative, the branch is predicted taken: identical to (b), 20 %, 1 stall cycle.

The rate is only worth the point with the row of outcomes underneath it. The commonest slip is counting four executions instead of five: list the values of R1 at the branch before writing anything else.

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

In a program 20 % of the executed instructions are conditional branches and 60 % of the branches are taken. The pipeline solves branches in the ID stage and uses branch always not taken; branches are the only source of stalls and the pipeline fill is negligible. Compute the CPI.

Show model answer
\[ \text{CPI} = 1 + f_b \cdot \text{penalty} \cdot p_{\text{mis}} \] \[ = 1 + 0.2 \cdot 1 \cdot 0.6 = 1.12 \]

Always not taken mispredicts exactly the taken branches, so \(p_{\text{mis}} = 0.6\). For comparison: stalling gives 1.20, always taken with a perfect BTB 1.08; with the PC written in ME (penalty 3) the three become 1.60, 1.36, 1.24.

Write the formula first, then the numbers. The examiner checks that the penalty is 1 for an ID-resolved branch and that the misprediction probability of "not taken" is the taken fraction, not its complement.

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

The pipeline solves branches in ID and has one delay slot that is always executed. Fill the slot of the following loop with the "from target" strategy: write the scheduled code, give the cycles per iteration before and after, and state the conditions that make the transformation legal.

LOOP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 8
      FADD  F2, F2, F0
      FMUL  F4, F4, F0
      BNE   R1, R2, LOOP
      NOP              # delay slot
Show model answer 
      LD    F0, 0(R1)      # copy of the target, runs once
LOOP: ADDUI R1, R1, 8      # new target
      FADD  F2, F2, F0
      FMUL  F4, F4, F0
      BNE   R1, R2, LOOP
      LD    F0, 0(R1)      # delay slot: next iteration's load

Before: 5 instructions + 1 NOP = 6 cycles per iteration (m = 5, k = 1, CPI∞ = 1.2). After: 5 cycles per iteration (k = 0, CPI∞ = 1), because the slot now holds the next iteration's load. Legal because: the moved LD does not produce an operand of the branch (R1, R2); the slot LD writes F0, which the next FADD reads with the ADDUI in between, so no load-use stall appears; the ADDUI stays at least one instruction before the BNE that tests R1 (2-cycle ALU-to-branch latency); and on the exit path the extra load past the end of the array must be harmless — the address readable and F0 dead after the loop. The copy before the loop is the duplication cost of the strategy.

Four things earn the marks: the retargeted label, the copy before the loop, the cycle count before/after, and the exit-path condition. Forgetting the copy makes the first iteration read an F0 that was never loaded.

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

In a 5-stage pipeline where the branch outcome and the target address are computed in the ID stage, the penalty of a mispredicted branch under branch always not taken is:

  • 0 cycles Zero is the cost of a correct guess; the wrong one loses the fetch slot used while the branch was in ID.
  • 1 cycle The instruction fetched in the cycle the branch spends in ID is flushed; fetch restarts from the target in the next cycle.
  • 2 cycles Two would be the penalty of a branch resolved at the end of EX.
  • 3 cycles Three is the penalty of the basic pipeline, where the PC is written at the end of ME.
Show model answer

1 cycle: penalty = resolution stage − 1 = 2 − 1.

The quiz is matched against the course's pipeline, in which "branch solved in ID stage" is the standing assumption.

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

With the delayed branch technique, the instruction placed in the branch delay slot:

  • is executed only if the branch is taken That describes a cancelling ("branch likely") branch predicted taken, not the plain delayed branch of the course.
  • is executed only if the branch is not taken No: the slot does not depend on the outcome at all.
  • is always executed, whatever the outcome The branch takes effect after the slot; the compiler must choose an instruction that is correct on both paths.
  • is flushed whenever the prediction is wrong There is no prediction and no flush in a delayed branch — this is exactly the wording the notes get wrong on pp. 49 and 51.
Show model answer

It is always executed, regardless of the branch outcome.

Single answer. The distractor "flushed" is attractive because of the notes' wording; the rule of the ISA is the one on p. 46.

Lesson 4 · Why the pipeline hates branches: static prediction and the delay slot · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which statements about the Branch Target Buffer used with branch always taken are true? (Multiple answers.)

  • It is indexed by the address of the branch instruction At fetch time the PC is the only thing the hardware has; the instruction bits are not yet available.
  • It stores the predicted target address Key: branch PC; value: predicted target (the notes' 0x100 → 0x80).
  • It decides whether the branch is taken The direction comes from the static rule (here) or from a history table (lesson 5); the BTB only says where.
  • On a hit the target is fetched in the cycle after the branch's IF That is the whole point: the target is available during IF, before decoding.
  • It is required by branch always not taken too Not taken needs no target: PC + 4 is always known.
Show model answer

True: indexed by the branch PC; stores the predicted target; on a hit the target is fetched in the next cycle. False: it does not decide the direction, and always not taken does not need it.

The 2026 paper has "Multiple Choice – Multiple answer" quizzes; tick every true statement and no false one.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the Dynamic Branch Prediction techniques used in modern microprocessors. (a) What are the main benefits to introduce the dynamic branch prediction in a modern microprocessor? (b) Explain how works a Branch History Table.

Show model answer

(a) The hardware predicts each branch at run time from its past behaviour, so the pipeline keeps fetching instead of stalling. Benefits: fewer mispredictions and hence a lower CPI (in \(\text{CPI} = \text{CPI}_{\text{ideal}} + f_b (1 - a) P\) the predictor raises the accuracy \(a\), the penalty \(P\) is fixed by the pipeline); adaptation to the actual input data and program phases, which a static compile-time rule cannot see; it is essential in deep and wide pipelines, where every misprediction wastes depth × width instructions; it enables speculative execution past unresolved branches, with results committed only when the branch resolves; it needs no compiler support, delay slots or profiling, so existing binaries benefit.

(b) A Branch History Table is a table indexed by the low-order bits of the branch PC, without tags (so two branches may share an entry: aliasing). Each entry is a small finite-state machine: 1 bit storing the last outcome, or a 2-bit machine with the states Strongly Taken, Weakly Taken, Weakly Not Taken, Strongly Not Taken, predicting Taken in the first two. The table is read in IF with the fetch PC and the prediction decides whether to fetch PC + 4 or the target; when the branch resolves the entry is updated with the outcome. With 2 bits a single anomaly, such as a loop exit, moves a strong state to the weak state of the same direction without changing the prediction, so a loop branch is mispredicted once per execution of the loop instead of twice.

The first two of the five sub-points of Question 2 of the January 2026 paper; the June 2026 paper asks the same in one sub-point of the 8-point question. A complete answer has the mechanism (indexed by the PC, no tags, FSM per entry, read in IF, updated at resolution), the 1-bit vs 2-bit contrast with the loop-exit argument, and benefits stated as cause and effect, not as adjectives. Do not write "reduces the penalty": it reduces the rate.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main purpose to introduce the Branch Target Buffer.

Show model answer

The branch target address is computed in the ID stage, so even a correct Taken prediction in IF leaves the fetch unit without an address: one bubble on every taken branch. The Branch Target Buffer removes this control hazard. It is a small cache indexed by the branch PC, with tags, whose data is the predicted target address of taken branches (often together with the T/NT prediction bits). On a hit with a Taken prediction the next PC is set to the stored target in the same IF cycle, so a correctly predicted taken branch costs zero cycles. An entry is written when a taken branch resolves and its target is known; on a miss, fetch defaults to PC + 4 and the branch pays the normal penalty if taken. The tags are necessary because a false hit would redirect the fetch of a non-branch instruction — unlike the BHT, which only predicts the direction and can be untagged.

The third sub-point of Question 2 of the January 2026 paper. The examiner looks for "whether versus where", "in the same cycle", "indexed by the PC with tags", "stores taken branches, filled at resolution", and the three cases (predict NT; predict T and hit; predict T and miss). Do not write "eliminates the control hazard" without qualification: it eliminates it on a hit with a correct prediction.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following LOOP to be executed on a processor with a single-entry 2-bit Branch History Table initialized in the state Strongly Not-Taken:

INIT: ADDUI R1, R0, 0
      ADDUI R2, R0, 20
LOOP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
      FADD  F2, F0, F0
      SD    F2, 0(R1)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 4
      BNE   R1, R2, LOOP

After the last iteration, how much is the misprediction rate on the BNE instruction? Motivate your answer by completing the table (rows Prediction State / Prediction / Branch Outcome, one column per iteration; the first column is given: Strongly Not Taken, Not Taken, Taken).

  • 60 % Five executions (R1 = 4, 8, 12, 16, 20), misses at the 1st, 2nd and 5th: 3/5.
  • 90 % Impossible on five executions (it would be 4.5 misses); read as an accuracy, 90 % is the notes' ten-iteration steady state, not this cold start.
  • 99 % Impossible: after two Taken in a row any 2-bit counter predicts the third Taken correctly.
  • 100 % Impossible for the same reason: the 3rd and 4th executions are predicted right.
  • 80 % Four misses would need the warm-up to last four executions; from SNT it lasts two.
Show model answer

The BNE is executed with R1 = 4, 8, 12, 16, 20: five times, Taken four times and Not Taken at the exit.

Iteration1st2nd3rd4th5th
Prediction StateStrongly Not TakenWeakly Not TakenWeakly TakenStrongly TakenStrongly Taken
PredictionNot TakenNot TakenTakenTakenTaken
Branch OutcomeTakenTakenTakenTakenNot Taken

Mispredictions at the 1st, 2nd and 5th execution: misprediction rate = 3 / 5 = 60 % (Answer 1). With the FSM of the lecture figure the 3rd state is Strongly Taken (WNT → ST on a Taken); the predictions and the rate are the same.

The 10 June 2025 paper, Question 5, worth 1 point for the answer and 1 for the table. Count the executions from the register values, not from the word "iterations", and count the warm-up misses. Write the state names in full, as the paper does.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Same loop and same Branch History Table. After the last iteration, what is the state of the prediction left in the Branch History Table?

  • Strongly Taken That is the state before the 5th execution; the exit Not Taken moves it one step down.
  • Weakly Taken After four Taken the entry is in Strongly Taken; the final Not Taken moves it to Weakly Taken, in both FSM variants.
  • Strongly Not Taken The initial state; after four Taken the counter is at the other end.
  • Weakly Not Taken One Not Taken from Strongly Taken reaches only the weak Taken state; a second one would be needed.
Show model answer

Before the 5th execution the state is Strongly Taken (after four Taken). The outcome is Not Taken, so the state moves one step: Strongly Taken → Weakly Taken (Answer 2). The question asks for the state after the last update, one transition beyond the last column of the table.

Second part of the same quiz. The trap is to answer with the state used for the last prediction (Strongly Taken) instead of the state left by it.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a processor with a 1-entry 1-bit Branch History Table initialized as Taken, executing the following code (only the BEQ accesses the BHT):

      ADDUI R1, R0, 0
      ADDUI R2, R0, 100
LOOP: BEQ   R1, R2, DONE
      ADD   R5, R5, R4
      ADDUI R1, R1, 1
      J     LOOP
DONE:

How many mispredictions occur on the BEQ? (State also the number of BHT accesses.)

Show model answer

The BEQ is executed with R1 = 0, 1, …, 100: 101 accesses to the BHT. Outcomes: Not Taken 100 times, then Taken at R1 = 100. The entry starts as Taken: 1st access, predict Taken, outcome Not Taken → misprediction, bit ← NT; accesses 2 to 100 predict Not Taken correctly; 101st access, predict Not Taken, outcome Taken → misprediction. Total: 101 accesses, 2 mispredictions (rate 2/101 ≈ 2 %).

Quiz 5 of the May 2025 midterm as the notes report it (its options paired accesses and mispredictions; "101 and 2" is the right pair). A top-tested loop executes its exit branch N + 1 times; the first miss comes from the initialisation, the second from the exit — both are changes of direction, and a 1-bit entry pays one miss per change.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

An inner loop of 8 iterations, closed by a BNE (taken 7 times, then not taken), is executed 3 times by an outer loop. Only the inner BNE accesses a single-entry Branch History Table. (a) With a 1-bit BHT initialized Not Taken, give the number of BHT accesses, the number of mispredictions, the misprediction rate and the final state. (b) Same with a 2-bit BHT initialized Strongly Not Taken. (c) How many mispredictions per execution of the inner loop does each predictor make in steady state?

Show model answer

(a) 3 × 8 = 24 accesses; outcomes T T T T T T T NT, three times. The 1-bit entry mispredicts at access 1 (NT predicted, T), at 8 (exit), at 9 (re-entry: the entry holds NT), at 16, 17 and 24: 6 mispredictions, 6/24 = 25 %, final state Not Taken (the last outcome).

(b) From Strongly Not Taken: access 1 predicts NT (miss, → WNT), access 2 predicts NT (miss, → WT with a saturating counter, → ST with the lecture figure), accesses 3–7 predict T correctly (the state is ST from access 4 at the latest), access 8 predicts T but the branch exits (miss, ST → WT); access 9 predicts T from WT correctly (→ ST), accesses 10–15 hit, 16 misses (→ WT), 17 hits, 18–23 hit, 24 misses (→ WT). Mispredictions at 1, 2, 8, 16, 24: 5, rate 5/24 ≈ 21 %, final state Weakly Taken.

(c) Steady state: 2 per inner-loop execution with 1 bit (exit and re-entry), 1 with 2 bits (exit only). The 2-bit predictor also paid one extra warm-up miss from Strongly Not Taken.

An exercise in the style of the June 2025 quiz with the counting extended over several executions of the loop. The examiner checks the three sources of misses — initialisation, exit, re-entry — and that you know which one the second bit removes. Both 2-bit variants give the same numbers here.

Lesson 5 · Learning from the past: 1-bit and 2-bit history tables, and the BTB · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

During the execution of a program, the number of mispredictions depends on the initialization and the size of the Branch History Table (BHT). True or False? Motivate your answer.

  • True Initialisation sets the warm-up misses (a 2-bit entry started Strongly Not Taken misses the first two taken executions of a loop; started Strongly Taken it misses none); the size sets how many branches share an untagged entry, so a small table makes unrelated branches overwrite each other's state (aliasing) and mispredict.
  • False Both effects are real: the June 2025 quiz counts the two warm-up misses from Strongly Not Taken, and an untagged table of 2^k entries aliases every pair of branches with equal low-order PC bits.
Show model answer

True. (1) Initialization: the initial state of the entry decides whether the first executions of a branch are predicted correctly — a loop branch whose entry starts Strongly Not Taken costs two warm-up mispredictions, one that starts Weakly Not Taken costs one, one that starts Taken costs none. (2) Size: the BHT is indexed by k low-order bits of the PC without tags, so with 2^k entries all branches sharing those bits share the entry and interfere (aliasing); a larger table spreads the branches over more entries and reduces the interference. (The number of mispredictions also depends on the predictor's FSM, 1-bit or 2-bit, and on the program's branch patterns.)

Quiz 4 of the May 2025 midterm, a True/False with "motivate". The motivation earns the point: name both mechanisms and the word aliasing.

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts of the correlating branch prediction technique and its benefits with respect to a standard Branch History Table.

Show model answer

A standard BHT predicts each branch from its own past only. A correlating (two-level) predictor also uses the outcomes of the branches executed just before it: an m-bit Global History Register, a shift register holding the last m outcomes (1 = Taken), selects one of 2^m tables of n-bit entries (1-bit, or the 2-bit states Strongly/Weakly Taken, Weakly/Strongly Not Taken), and the k low-order bits of the branch PC select the entry inside the selected table. Equivalently the GHR concatenated with the PC bits forms one index of m + k bits into a single table; gshare XORs the history with the PC bits instead, to spread the branches over the whole table and reduce aliasing. After the branch resolves, the entry is updated and the outcome is shifted into the GHR. The benefit: each (branch, recent context) pair has its own counter, so a branch whose outcome depends on previous branches — or on its own recent pattern, such as a loop exit — is predicted correctly after warm-up, where a per-branch counter flip-flops; H&P show a (2,2) predictor of 8 Kbit mispredicting less than a 2-bit BHT with unlimited entries. The cost is one shift register, 2^m × n × 2^k bits of tables, a longer warm-up (every context must be trained) and aliasing in the pattern table.

The wording is the fourth sub-point of Q2 of the January 2026 paper in the notes (p. 498); in the June 2026 format the same material sits inside the sub-point "dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution" of the 8-point question — about five lines either way. The examiner looks for the three mechanisms (history register, table selection, PC indexing), the update rule, the "separate counter per context" argument and one concrete benefit. Naming gshare's XOR earns the aliasing point; the January 2026 solution in the notes mentions "concatenating or XORing", so both words belong in the answer.

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the code BNE R1, R0, L1; ADDUI R1, R0, 1; L1: SUBI R3, R1, 1; BNE R3, R0, L2, where the first branch (b1) is taken if d = R1 is not 0 and the second (b2) is taken if d is not 1. The code is executed with d = 2, 0, 2, 0. (a) Fill the prediction table for a 1-bit predictor per branch, both entries initialised Not Taken. (b) Fill it for a (1,1) correlating predictor whose entries (one per branch and per value of the 1-bit global history) are initialised Not Taken, with the global history initialised Not Taken. (c) Count the mispredictions of each and explain the difference.

Show model answer

(a) 1-bit per branch (prediction = bit before, bit after = outcome):

db1 pred.b1 outcomeb2 pred.b2 outcome
2NT (miss)TNT (miss)T
0T (miss)NTT (miss)NT
2NT (miss)TNT (miss)T
0T (miss)NTT (miss)NT

(b) (1,1) predictor, entries written "if last NT / if last T":

dGH before b1b1 entriesb1 pred.b1 outcomeGH before b2b2 entriesb2 pred.b2 outcome
2NTNT/NTNT (miss)TTNT/NTNT (miss)T
0TT/NTNTNTNTNT/TNTNT
2NTT/NTTTTNT/TTT
0TT/NTNTNTNTNT/TNTNT

(c) 8 of 8 mispredicted against 2 of 8. The 1-bit entry always holds the previous outcome, and the outcomes alternate, so it is always one step behind. In the (1,1) predictor the global history bit selects a different entry for "after a taken branch" and "after a not-taken branch": in this code b1 not taken implies b2 not taken and b1 taken implies b2 taken, so each context converges after one miss; b1 is predicted from the previous b2, whose outcome encodes the previous value of d. Only the two cold entries miss.

Marks go to the correct shift of the history (it holds the last outcome, whichever branch produced it), to the two cold-entry misses being the only ones, and to the explanation in (c). A frequent slip: predicting b2 from b2's own previous outcome instead of from the entry the history selects.

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

A (2,2) correlating predictor has 1024 entries per table. How many bits of prediction storage does it use?

Show model answer
\[ 2^m \cdot n \cdot 2^k = 4 \cdot 2 \cdot 1024 = 8192 \text{ bits} \]

that is 1 KB; a plain 2-bit BHT with the same budget would have 4096 entries.

The m history bits multiply the tables, they do not add bits to an entry: 2^m tables, each of 2^k entries of n bits.

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the following assembly code executed by a processor with a 1-entry 1-bit Branch History Table initialised as Taken: ADDI R1, R0, 0; ADDI R2, R0, 100; LOOP: BEQ R1, R2, DONE; ADD R5, R5, R4; ADDI R1, R1, 1; J LOOP; DONE:. Assuming only the branch instruction accesses the BHT, how many accesses to the Branch History Table and how many mispredictions? (Single answer)

  • Answer 1: 101 accesses, 2 mispredictions The BEQ runs for R1 = 0 to 100: 101 executions; the initial Taken misses on the first (Not Taken), the exit misses on the last (Taken).
  • Answer 2: 100 accesses, 1 misprediction 100 counts the iterations, not the executions of the branch: the exit test is the 101st, and the initialisation costs a miss of its own.
  • Answer 3: 99 accesses, 1 misprediction The branch is executed for R1 = 0 as well as for R1 = 100; neither can be dropped.
  • Answer 4: 201 accesses, 2 mispredictions The unconditional J does not access the BHT by assumption; only the BEQ does.
Show model answer

Answer 1. Executions of BEQ: R1 = 0, 1, …, 100 → 101 accesses. Predict Taken at R1 = 0: outcome Not Taken, miss, bit → NT. R1 = 1 … 99: predict NT, outcome NT, 99 hits. R1 = 100: predict NT, outcome Taken, miss. Two mispredictions.

The midterm's quiz, recomputed. The two misses are the two changes of direction the 1-bit entry sees — one artificial (initialisation), one real (exit).

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

During the execution of a program, the number of mispredictions depends on the initialization and the size of the Branch History Table (BHT). True or False? Motivate your answer.

  • True Initialisation sets the start-up misses; size sets how many branches share an untagged entry (aliasing).
  • False Both matter: a loop branch started Strongly Not Taken misses twice before predicting Taken, and a small untagged table lets unrelated branches overwrite each other's state.
Show model answer

True. (1) Initialisation: the initial state of the entries decides whether the first executions of each branch are predicted right — a 2-bit entry initialised Strongly Not Taken mispredicts the first two taken executions of a loop branch, initialised Strongly Taken it mispredicts none; these start-up misses count in the misprediction rate. (2) Size: the BHT is indexed by the low-order PC bits without tags, so with a small table different branches map to the same entry (aliasing) and interfere, each spoiling the other's state; a larger table reduces the collisions.

Two separate reasons, each with its mechanism, is what the extra "motivate" point pays for. Adding that the counter type (1 bit or 2) and the program's patterns also matter does not hurt.

Lesson 6 · Branches that talk to each other: correlating and two-level predictors · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

A loop of 10 iterations, whose body contains no other branch, is executed many times. Which predictor can reach 100 % accuracy on its loop branch after warm-up, exit included? (Single answer)

  • A 2-bit BHT with 4096 entries A counter holds a bias, not a pattern: it mispredicts the exit every time, 90 % at best.
  • A 2-bit BHT with unlimited entries Unlimited entries remove aliasing only; the exit is not an aliasing problem.
  • A two-level predictor with at least 9 bits of local history With h ≥ N − 1 = 9 every position in the pattern T×9 NT has its own history and its own counter; after warm-up all ten executions are predicted.
  • Always Taken Right nine times in ten, wrong at every exit: 90 %.
Show model answer

Answer 3. The exit is predictable because it comes at a fixed position; only a history long enough to distinguish "nine taken in a row" from the earlier positions can represent it, which is what a local (or, with no other branch in the body, global) history of at least 9 bits does. Counters of any size and static rules cannot.

The examiner is checking the difference between bias (what a counter stores) and pattern (what a history register stores).
