Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle

Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

(10 June 2025, EX1, first part.) Let's consider the following assembly code containing multiple types of intra-loop dependences. Complete the following table by inserting all types of true-data-dependences, anti-dependences and output dependences for each instruction (I0, I1 and I2: None).

I0  FOR: LD    F2, A(R1)
I1       LD    F4, B(R1)
I2       LD    F6, C(R1)
I3       FADD  F4, F2, F4
I4       FADD  F6, F4, F6
I5       SD    F6, A(R1)
I6       ADDUI R1, R1, 8
I7       BNE   R1, R2, FOR
Show model answer
I#True data dependence with I# for FxAnti-dependence with I# for FyOutput dependence with I# for Fz
I0, I1, I2NoneNoneNone
I3with I0 for F2; with I1 for F4Nonewith I1 for F4
I4with I3 for F4; with I2 for F6Nonewith I2 for F6
I5with I4 for F6NoneNone
I6Nonewith I0, I1, I2 and I5 for R1None
I7with I6 for R1NoneNone

Three rules fill the table: nearest earlier writer of each source; every earlier reader of the destination; every earlier writer of the destination. The marks usually lost: forgetting that the ADDUI collects all readers of R1, including the store; writing an anti-dependence for I3 on F4 (nobody reads F4 before I3 — I1 writes it, which is an output dependence); listing I0 as a producer for I4 (I4 reads F4 from I3, the nearest writer). The loop-carried dependence through R1 is not asked.

Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

(In the form of Question 1 of 22 June 2026.) Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): 1. Dynamic instruction scheduling; 2. Dynamic register renaming; 3. Out-of-order execution.

Show model answer

Dynamic instruction scheduling. The hardware, not the compiler, decides at run time when each instruction executes. Instructions are issued in program order to reservation stations and start executing as soon as their operands and a functional unit are available, so an instruction stalled on a long-latency producer (a divide, a cache miss) does not block the independent instructions behind it. Benefits: higher ILP and unit utilisation, latency hiding, handling of dependences unknown at compile time (memory, control flow), and binary compatibility — the same code runs efficiently on different pipelines; it is also the base for hardware speculation.

Dynamic register renaming. At issue, each destination register is mapped to a new name — a physical register, a reservation-station tag or a reorder-buffer entry — and later readers are redirected to it, so two instructions that re-use the same architectural register no longer conflict. Benefits: it eliminates WAR and WAW hazards (name dependences), leaving only true RAW dependences, which lets instructions execute out of order and loop iterations overlap without running out of registers.

Out-of-order execution. Instructions execute in the order in which their operands become ready rather than in program order, while issue and commit remain in program order; results are broadcast on the common data bus to the waiting consumers. Benefits: independent instructions fill the cycles that dependent ones would waste, long latencies are overlapped, more ILP is extracted from the instruction window. Cost: renaming, reservation stations and a reorder buffer to keep exceptions precise.

The question gives five lines per technique: write concept, mechanism, benefit and cost as short labelled statements, with "in-order issue, out-of-order execution, in-order commit" somewhere in the answer — that triad is what the examiner looks for. The other five items of the real question — 2 dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution, 3 multiple-issue pipeline, 4 reservation stations, 7 reorder buffer, 8 simultaneous multithreading — are answered in lessons 5–6, 8, 11, 17 and 29.

Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

A 5-stage pipeline is extended with multi-cycle floating-point units of different latencies (a pipelined adder and multiplier, an unpipelined divider), keeping in-order issue. Which new hazards and problems appear, and why? How does a single in-order commit point remove them, and at what cost?

Show model answer

New problems: (1) a structural hazard on the unpipelined divider; (2) several instructions may reach write-back in the same cycle, so the register-file write port must be arbitrated or reserved at issue; (3) WAW hazards, because a short operation issued after a long one to the same register completes first — WAR hazards cannot occur, since operands are still read in order in ID; (4) imprecise exceptions, because a younger instruction may have written its result before an older one faults; (5) more and longer RAW stalls, since forwarding cannot hide a multi-cycle computation.

A single commit point delays every result so that all operations reach the W stage after the same number of cycles: write-back is then in issue order, one per cycle (no WAW, no write-port conflict), and an exception is taken at commit, after all earlier instructions and before any later one (precise). Cost: independent fast instructions wait behind slow ones, and an unpipelined divide or a cache miss stalls the whole pipeline — low ILP, which is what motivates out-of-order execution with a reorder buffer that keeps commit in order while execution reorders.

The examiner checks that you separate the five effects and that you know which hazard cannot occur (WAR) and why. "Precise" must be defined, not just named: older ones done, younger ones without effect, restart at the faulting instruction.

Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

(15 July 2024, quiz 1, single answer.) Let's consider the following loop: for (i=1; i<=100; i++) { X[i] = X[i] + X[i-1]; /*S1*/ Z[i] = X[i] + Y[i-1] /*S2*/ }. How many loop-carried dependencies are in the code?

  • Answer 1: Only one in S2 because Z[i] depends on Y[i-1] Y is never written inside the loop, so Y[i-1] is an input, not a dependence between iterations.
  • Answer 2: Only one in S1 because X[i] depends on X[i-1] S1 reads X[i-1], which S1 wrote in the previous iteration: that is the one loop-carried dependence.
  • Answer 3: Both of them S2 depends on X[i] from the same iteration (intra-iteration) and on Y, which the loop does not write.
Show model answer

Answer 2. S1 reads X[i-1], produced by S1 in iteration i-1: loop-carried. S2's dependence on X[i] is within the iteration, and Y[i-1] is read only.

The exam's own distractor is the array index: "[i-1]" does not make a dependence unless the loop writes that array.

Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Multiple answers.) Which hazards can occur in the classic 5-stage in-order pipeline with a single-cycle EX stage?

  • RAW A consumer may need a value before the producer has computed it (a load followed by its user still stalls one cycle even with forwarding).
  • WAR Impossible: every read happens in ID and every write in WB, both in program order, so no younger instruction can write before an older one reads.
  • WAW Impossible: all writes happen in WB, in program order.
  • Structural With one memory for instructions and data, or one register-file port too few, two instructions want the same resource in the same cycle.
  • Control The branch outcome is known in ID or EX, after the next instructions have been fetched.
Show model answer

RAW, structural and control. WAR and WAW cannot occur: reads precede writes in order (no WAR) and all writes happen in WB in order (no WAW). Register renaming therefore buys nothing on this pipeline; it matters only once multi-cycle units (WAW) and out-of-order execution (WAR) appear.

A quiz in the 2026 "multiple answer" form. The trap is to tick WAR and WAW because they exist as dependences in the program; the question asks which can become hazards on this pipeline.

Lesson 7 · Three ways instructions collide, and pipelines where EX takes more than a cycle · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Single answer.) In a pipeline with in-order issue, functional units of different latencies and no reorder buffer, an FDIV reports a divide-by-zero at the end of its execution. Why is the exception imprecise?

  • Because the FDIV was issued out of order Issue is in order in this pipeline; the order that breaks is completion.
  • Because younger instructions have already written their registers when the fault is reported, so the state is not the sequential state at the FDIV The program cannot be restarted at the FDIV: part of what follows it has already happened.
  • Because the write port was busy when the FDIV wanted to write A write-port conflict delays a write; it does not change which instructions have taken effect.
  • Because the instructions that depend on the FDIV have been squashed Squashing the dependent instructions is harmless: they had not changed the state. The problem is the independent ones that did.
Show model answer

The second option. An exception is precise when all older instructions have completed and no younger one has changed the state; here younger, independent instructions completed and wrote their registers before the older FDIV faulted, so the state corresponds to no point of the sequential program. In-order commit (a reorder buffer) fixes it by holding their results until the FDIV has committed.

Watch for the word "issued": the examiner uses it to check that you know which stages stay in order.

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): 1. Dynamic instruction scheduling; 2. Dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution; 3. Multiple-issue pipeline; 4. Reservation stations; 5. Dynamic register renaming; 6. Out-of-order execution; 7. Reorder buffer; 8. Simultaneous multithreading. (Five lines each.)

Show model answer

1. Dynamic instruction scheduling. The hardware, not the compiler, decides at run time when each instruction executes: instructions are issued in program order to reservation stations and start as soon as their operands and a functional unit are available, so execution and completion are out of order while issue and commit stay in order. Benefits: an instruction stalled on a long-latency producer (FP divide, cache miss) no longer blocks the independent instructions behind it; higher ILP and unit utilisation; latency hiding; dependences unknown at compile time (memory, misses) are handled; the same binary runs efficiently on different pipelines; it is the base for hardware speculation.

2. Dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution. A branch history table (with a branch target buffer) predicts the outcome and the target at fetch time from the past behaviour of the branch; the processor fetches, issues and executes the predicted path before the branch is resolved, keeping the results in the reorder buffer, and squashes them if the prediction was wrong. Benefits: removes most control stalls, keeps the issue slots of a wide machine full and exposes ILP across branches; the cost is the flush on a misprediction, which grows with pipeline depth and issue width.

3. Multiple-issue pipeline. The front end fetches, decodes and issues up to w instructions per cycle (the issue width) into several functional units, so IPC can exceed 1 and the ideal CPI is 1/w. It needs wider fetch and decode, a multi-ported register file, more functional units and hazard checks across the instructions issued together. Benefit: throughput multiplied by the width when enough independent instructions are available; the ideal is a maximum, rarely sustained because of dependences, branches and misses.

4. Reservation stations. Buffers in front of each functional unit that hold the operation, the operand values already available (Vj, Vk) or the tags of the instructions that will produce them (Qj, Qk); an instruction waits in its station and fires when the last operand arrives on the common data bus. Benefits: hazard detection is distributed (no central bottleneck), operands are fetched once at issue, so WAR hazards vanish, the station's tag renames the destination, so WAW hazards vanish, and issue continues past a waiting instruction.

5. Dynamic register renaming. At issue, every destination register is mapped to a new name — a reservation-station tag, a ROB entry or a physical register — and later readers are redirected to that name, so two instructions that reuse the same architectural register no longer conflict. Benefits: WAR and WAW hazards, which are name dependences, are eliminated; only true RAW dependences remain; more instructions can be in flight and loop iterations overlap without unrolling.

6. Out-of-order execution. Instructions execute in the order in which their operands become ready rather than in program order; issue and commit remain in order and results are broadcast on the common data bus to the waiting consumers. Benefits: independent instructions fill the cycles that dependent ones would waste, long latencies (loads, FP operations) are overlapped and more ILP is extracted from the instruction window; the cost is renaming, reservation stations and a reorder buffer to keep exceptions precise.

7. Reorder buffer. A FIFO in program order that holds each result from write-result to commit; the entry at the head commits to the register file (or to memory, for a store) only when it is complete and no longer speculative. Benefits: precise exceptions and an architectural state updated in program order; speculation can be undone by discarding the entries after a mispredicted branch; the register file is written only at commit.

8. Simultaneous multithreading. Several hardware threads issue in the same cycle into the functional units of one out-of-order core, each with its own PC and rename table, sharing the units and the caches. Benefits: thread-level parallelism fills the issue slots that the ILP of one thread leaves empty (both horizontal and vertical waste), so throughput rises at a small hardware cost; the ideal CPI of the core stays 1/w and the ideal per-thread CPI is threads/w; threads contend for caches and units, so single-thread performance can drop.

Five lines per item is the paper's own allowance: name the mechanism in one sentence, then the benefit, then one cost or condition. The examiner looks for the key words of each row (in-order issue, out-of-order execution, tags, broadcast, in-order commit, precise exceptions, threads/w) and for the distinction between items 1 and 6 — dynamic scheduling is the policy, out-of-order execution is what it produces.

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Compare static (VLIW) and dynamic (superscalar) scheduling: who schedules the instructions and when, the main advantages and drawbacks of each approach, and their typical application domains.

Show model answer

Static (VLIW). The compiler, at compile time, packs independent operations into long instruction words, scheduling around the known latencies and inserting NOPs where no operation fits. Advantages: simple hardware (no dependence checks, no renaming, no out-of-order logic), predictable timing, low energy per instruction. Drawbacks: performance depends entirely on the compiler and on the visible ILP; the binary is tied to one machine configuration (units, latencies), so it must be recompiled for a new one; the schedule cannot adapt to run-time events such as cache misses or mispredictions; unused slots become NOPs, lowering the code efficiency IC/(cycles × issues). Domains: embedded, real-time, DSP and domain-specific processors.

Dynamic (superscalar). The hardware, at run time, detects the parallelism with reservation stations, register renaming and a reorder buffer, and issues whatever is ready. Advantages: adapts to unpredictable latencies, keeps binary portability (no recompilation), exploits the hidden parallelism of general-purpose code. Drawbacks: complexity, area and power; the scheduling logic becomes the bottleneck at wide issue widths; timing is hard to verify. Domains: general-purpose x86 and ARM cores. Both aim at IPC above 1; the trade-off between hardware complexity and compiler sophistication decides where each wins.

The two columns of Table 12 in sentences. Say explicitly who and when in the first line of each half; the binary-compatibility point and the NOP/code-efficiency point are the drawbacks the examiner expects to see named.

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a dual-issue SMT processor that can manage up to 4 hardware threads. What are the values of the ideal CPI and the ideal per-thread CPI? (Single answer.)

  • Ideal CPI = 1 & ideal per-thread CPI = 0.25 A dual-issue core has ideal CPI 1/2, not 1; and a thread with a quarter of the slots cannot have a CPI below the core's.
  • Ideal CPI = 0.25 & ideal per-thread CPI = 0.25 0.25 would be a quad-issue core; the per-thread CPI is always larger than the core's when several threads share it.
  • Ideal CPI = 0.5 & ideal per-thread CPI = 2 Core: 1/w = 1/2 = 0.5. The four threads share the two slots, so each ideally completes half an instruction per cycle: per-thread CPI = threads/w = 4/2 = 2.
  • Ideal CPI = 0.5 & ideal per-thread CPI = 1 The core is right; 1 per thread would need 2 threads on 2 slots (or 4 threads on 4 slots).
  • Ideal CPI = 0.25 & ideal per-thread CPI = 4 Both numbers belong to other machines: 0.25 is quad-issue, 4 would be 8 threads on 2 slots.
Show model answer

Answer 3: ideal CPI = 1/issue width = 1/2 = 0.5; ideal per-thread CPI = threads/issue width = 4/2 = 2.

The same question is in all three papers, always with five options built from the same numbers permuted: dual-issue with 4 threads in July 2024 and June 2026 (0.5 and 2), quad-issue with 8 threads in June 2025 (0.25 and 2). In 2026 a second point asks for a practical example of SMT scheduling with up to 4 threads on the dual-issue core. The two formulas are all you need here; the reasoning behind them and the scheduling example are lesson 29.

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Considering superscalar processors, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Multiple answers; motivate each.)

  • Doubling the number of issues reduces the CPI metric. True for the ideal CPI = 1/issue width (0.5 becomes 0.25); the achieved CPI improves only as far as the ILP, the dependences and the memory system allow.
  • Out-of-order execution can be generated by data cache misses. True: a missing load stalls its own chain while the dynamic scheduler keeps executing the independent instructions in the window, so later instructions finish earlier — the miss triggers it, the scheduler does it.
  • The introduction of dynamic branch predictors improves the CPI metric. True: fewer mispredictions mean fewer flushes; CPI = CPI_ideal + branch frequency × misprediction rate × penalty.
  • Doubling the issue width halves the execution time of any program. False: only the ideal CPI halves. The serial part of the program (Amdahl), the branches and the misses are untouched, and beyond 3–4 issue slots real programs rarely offer enough independent instructions.
Show model answer

Statements 1, 2 and 3 are true (1 only for the ideal CPI, 1/issue width); statement 4 is false, because dependences, branches and memory stalls do not shrink with the width and Amdahl's law bounds the gain at 1/f.

The first three statements are the True/False quiz of the 5 May 2025 midterm (notes pp. 540–541); the fourth is the standard distractor. The motivation earns the extra point: write "ideal", name the scheduler as the mechanism behind the miss, and write the CPI formula with its branch term.

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

A program spends 20 % of its execution time in a part that must run sequentially. The rest is accelerated 4 times by a wider, out-of-order core. What is the overall speedup?

Show model answer

Serial fraction \(f = 0.2\), \(p = 4\):

\[ \mathrm{Speedup} = \frac{1}{0.2 + \dfrac{0.8}{4}} = \frac{1}{0.4} = 2.5 \]

In the formula sheet's form, \(F = 0.8\) and \(S = 4\): \(1/(0.2 + 0.8/4) = 2.5\). The ceiling for this program is \(1/0.2 = 5\).

Read the fraction first: "20 % sequential" is the serial \(f\), so \(F = 0.8\). The typical slip is to put 0.2 into the sheet's \(F\) and get \(1/(0.8 + 0.05) = 1.18\).

Lesson 8 · Doing more per cycle: superscalar, static vs dynamic scheduling, the limits of ILP · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

A compiler schedules the 10 operations of one loop iteration on a 3-issue VLIW processor in 7 cycles (delay slot included). How much is the code efficiency for a single iteration, in percent?

Show model answer
\[ \text{code efficiency} = \frac{\mathrm{IC}}{\#\text{cycles} \times \#\text{issues}} \] \[ = \frac{10}{7 \times 3} = \frac{10}{21} = 47.6\,\% \]

The formula is on the example formula sheet; the exam's VLIW exercises ask it after the schedule, together with the critical path, the CPI and the FP operations per cycle (lesson 20). Eleven of the 21 slots are NOPs.

Module 4 · Dynamic scheduling: scoreboard, Tomasulo, reorder buffer

67 questions · Notes, chapter 3.6–3.9 and the past papers · the three algorithms the exam tables are built on

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of dynamic scheduling with the scoreboard: (i) the assumptions of the model; (ii) the four stages and the hazard checked in each; (iii) the three data structures and the role of Qj/Qk and Rj/Rk; (iv) the main limitations and how Tomasulo's algorithm overcomes them.

Show model answer

(i) Single-issue, in-order issue; out-of-order read of operands, execution and write-back; no forwarding (a result is visible only after it is written in the register file); several independent functional units ("multiple pipelined FUs" in the notes) with different latencies, each holding one instruction from issue to write result in the tables; no register renaming, so WAW and WAR hazards must be handled by stalls; exceptions are imprecise.

(ii) Issue (in order): structural hazard — the functional unit must be free — and WAW — no pending write to the destination in the register result status. Read Operands (out of order): RAW — both source operands written to the register file (and read ports available). Execute: no hazard, latency of the unit. Write Result (out of order): WAR — no earlier instruction still waiting to read the destination (and a write port free).

(iii) Instruction status: the stage reached by each instruction (the I / RR / E / WB table). Functional-unit status, one row per unit: Busy, Op, Fi (destination), Fj, Fk (sources), Qj, Qk (the units that will produce Fj, Fk; blank if the value is in the register file), Rj, Rk (source ready and not yet read; set to No when the operands are read). Register result status: for each register, the unit that will write it. Qj/Qk say who produces an operand, Rj/Rk say whether it may be read now; Rj = No after the read is what the WAR test of Write Result uses. Benefits: independent instructions overlap, long latencies are hidden, functional units are better used, no compiler support is needed.

(iv) Limits: the parallelism available in the window, the number of scoreboard entries, the number and types of functional units, WAR/WAW stalls because there is no renaming, one extra cycle per RAW dependence because there is no forwarding, centralized control, imprecise exceptions. Tomasulo adds register renaming through reservation stations (WAR and WAW disappear), the Common Data Bus (results forwarded to every waiting station, distributed hazard detection) and, with a reorder buffer, speculation and precise exceptions.

The examiner checks three things: that you put each hazard in its stage (WAW at Issue, RAW at Read Operands, WAR at Write Result — not "the scoreboard checks hazards"), that you say what Qj and Rj are each for, and that part (iv) names concrete limits and the concrete Tomasulo mechanism that removes each. Write short labelled statements, one per line, as the paper's blank lines invite.

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the following program on a processor with dynamic scheduling based on the SCOREBOARD BASIC SCHEME (check for WAW hazards in the ISSUE stage; no forwarding), with 1 Integer unit (loads, stores and integer operations, latency 1 cycle), 1 FP ADD unit (latency 2 cycles), 2 FP MULT units (latency 4 cycles). Fill the table with the cycles of I, RR, E and WB for each instruction, the type of hazard and the unit, and compute the CPI.

I0: LD    F0, 0(R1)
I1: LD    F2, 8(R1)
I2: FMUL  F4, F0, F2
I3: FADD  F2, F0, F6
I4: SD    F4, 16(R1)
I5: ADDUI R1, R1, 8
Show model answer
InstructionIRREWBHazards TypeUnit
I0: LD F0, 0(R1)1234noneInteger
I1: LD F2, 8(R1)5678Structural Integer (I0)Integer
I2: FMUL F4, F0, F2691314RAW F2 (I1)Mult1
I3: FADD F2, F0, F69101213WAW F2 (I1); WAR F2 with I2 costs nothing (I2 read at 9)Add
I4: SD F4, 16(R1)10151617RAW F4 (I2)Integer
I5: ADDUI R1, R1, 818192021Structural Integer (I4); WAR R1 with I4 costs nothing (I4 read at 15)Integer

I1 issues at 5 because the Integer unit is Busy until I0's write at 4. I2 reads at 9 = WB(I1) + 1. I3 cannot issue while the register result status says F2 = Integer (cycles 7–8); it issues at 9 and writes at 13, allowed because I2 has read F2 at 9. I4 issues at 10 and reads at 15 = WB(I2) + 1; it holds the Integer unit until its write at 17, so I5 issues at 18. Total 21 cycles; CPI = 21 / 6 = 3.5.

The marks hide in the Hazards column and in two conventions: a unit is free again the cycle after its WB (I1, I5), and a consumer reads the cycle after the producer's WB (I2, I4). Say explicitly that the WAR of I3 and I5 costs nothing and why; a bare "WAR" in that column reads as a stall you did not find.

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the fragment of this lesson (I0 LD F2, 0(R1), I1 FMUL F4, F2, F0, I2 FADD F6, F4, F8, I3 LD F8, 8(R1), I4 FMUL F8, F2, F0, I5 SD F6, 16(R1); Integer 1, Add 2, Mult 4; basic scheme), in which cycle does I3 write its result?

Show model answer

I3 completes execution at cycle 7 but I2 (FADD F6, F4, F8) still has to read F8; I2 reads only at cycle 11 (= WB of I1 + 1). WAR on F8: WB(I3) = max(7 + 1, 11 + 1) = 12.

The wrong answer 8 forgets the WAR test; the wrong answer 11 forgets that the write comes the cycle after the read.

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the basic scoreboard scheme a WAR hazard is resolved by: (single answer)

  • stalling the reader in the Read Operands stage That is what the scoreboard does for a RAW hazard; a WAR hazard is about a writer that is too early.
  • stalling the writer in the Write Result stage until every earlier reader has read the register The write is postponed while a unit row has the register as a source with Rj or Rk still Yes.
  • renaming the destination register The scoreboard has no renaming; that is Tomasulo's answer.
  • stalling the writer in the Issue stage Issue stalls on structural and WAW hazards, not on WAR.
Show model answer

Answer 2: the later writer waits at Write Result until the earlier reader has read its operands (Rj/Rk back to No).

Each hazard has its stage; the quiz checks that you do not put WAR at Issue because "it is checked early".

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the functional-unit status table, Rj = No means: (single answer)

  • the operand Fj is not ready yet That is one of the two cases, not the only one.
  • the operand Fj has already been read That is the other case, not the only one.
  • either "not ready yet" or "already read" — and both are safe for a later writer, because the basic scheme blocks WAW at Issue The flag means "ready and not yet read"; after the read it is reset to No, and the Write Result test only has to look for a Yes.
  • the unit is idle An idle unit has Busy = No; the flags of an idle row are blank.
Show model answer

Answer 3: Rj, Rk are "ready and not yet read"; No covers both "waiting for the producer" and "already read", and a writer may proceed in both cases.

With the notes' definition ("ready") you can explain why the write of ADDD in the notes' example waits (the Divide row says Rk = Yes for F6) but not why it is released at cycle 22: a flag that only meant "ready" would stay Yes for ever.

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in the scoreboard, a functional unit becomes free as soon as its instruction completes execution.

  • True The result may still be waiting to be written (a WAR hazard, or a busy write port), and the unit holds it meanwhile.
  • False The unit is Busy from issue through write result; the next instruction for it can issue the cycle after the write.
Show model answer

False: Busy is cleared at Write Result, not at the end of execution. Example: in the fragment of this lesson I0 completes execution at cycle 3 and writes at 4; I3, which needs the same Integer unit, issues at 5, not at 4.

"Motivate your answer" is worth the extra point: give a cycle from a table.

Lesson 9 · The chalkboard that beat the pipeline: the scoreboard · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Why does the basic scoreboard check WAW hazards at the Issue stage rather than at Write Result, and what must change in the hardware if the check is postponed to the write-back phase, as in the "optimised" scheme?

Show model answer

Without renaming the two writes to the same register must land in program order. Checking at Issue is the simplest guarantee: issue is in order, so the instruction being issued is always the later writer, and one lookup in the register result status (a non-blank entry for its destination) settles it. It also keeps the Write Result test simple: because two pending writes to one register can never coexist, a source flag Rj = No can only mean "already read" or "waiting for this very writer", so the WAR test may let the writer through on a No.

If the check is postponed to write-back, the later writer issues and executes early (it no longer blocks the instructions behind it), but the Write Result stage must additionally enforce WB(second) > WB(first) for every earlier pending writer of the same register — i.e. the hardware must track, per register, more than one pending writer and order the writes explicitly.

The second paragraph is what distinguishes a full answer: name the benefit (earlier issue, no in-order stall) and the new obligation (explicit ordering of the writes). Lesson 10 computes the gain on the January 2026 paper: 25 cycles become 21.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the following program on a CPU with dynamic scheduling based on the SCOREBOARD BASIC SCHEME (check for WAW hazards in the ISSUE stage; no forwarding), with 1 Integer unit for the loads (latency 1, all cache hits), 2 FP MULT units (latency 10), 1 FP ADD unit (latency 2) and 1 FP DIV unit (latency 40). (a) Fill the table with I, RR, E and WB for each instruction, the hazard type and the unit, and compute the CPI. (b) Which hazard delays the write of I5, and until which cycle? (c) Would a second FP ADD unit reduce the total number of cycles? Justify.

I0: LD   F6, 34(R2)
I1: LD   F2, 45(R3)
I2: FMUL F0, F2, F4
I3: FSUB F8, F6, F2
I4: FDIV F10, F0, F6
I5: FADD F6, F8, F2
Show model answer
InstructionIRREWBHazards TypeUnit
I0: LD F6, 34(R2)1234noneInteger
I1: LD F2, 45(R3)5678Structural Integer (I0)Integer
I2: FMUL F0, F2, F4691920RAW F2 (I1)Mult1
I3: FSUB F8, F6, F2791112RAW F2 (I1)Add
I4: FDIV F10, F0, F68216162RAW F0 (I2)Divide
I5: FADD F6, F8, F213141622Structural Add (I3); WAR F6 (I4)Add

(a) 62 cycles; CPI = 62 / 6 ≈ 10.3. I1 issues at 5 because the Integer unit is Busy through I0's WB at 4; I2 and I3 read at 9 = WB(I1) + 1; I4 reads at 21 = WB(I2) + 1; I5 issues at 13, the cycle after I3's WB.

(b) A WAR hazard on F6 with I4: I4 reads F6 only at 21, because it waits for F0 (written at 20); I5 completes at 16 and writes at RR(I4) + 1 = 22.

(c) No. With a second adder I5 issues at 9 and completes at 15, but it still cannot write before I4 has read F6, so WB(I5) = 22; the total is fixed by the chain LD F2 → FMUL → FDIV, which ends at 62 whatever happens to the adds.

Three conventions decide the numbers: the unit is Busy through its WB (I1 at 5, I5 at 13), the consumer reads at WB + 1 (I2, I3, I4), and the WAR delays only the write. Part (c) asks, in effect, whether you know what a critical path is: name the chain and say the add is not on it.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the program of the January 2026 paper (I0 LD F2, VB(R6); I1 FADD F4, F2, F6; I2 SD F4, VA(R7); I3 LD F4, VC(R6); I4 SD F4, VC(R7); I5 ADDUI R6, R6, 4; I6 ADDUI R7, R7, 4) on the same resources (2 LOAD/STORE units LDU1, LDU2 with latency 3; 1 FP unit FPU1 with latency 3; 1 ALU/BR unit ALU1 with latency 1), with FORWARDING but with the check for WAW hazards still in the ISSUE stage. Row I0 is given: 1, 2, 5, 6, LDU1. Fill the table, compute the CPI and the speedup with respect to the basic scheme (25 cycles).

Show model answer
InstructionIRREWBHazards TypeUnit
I0: LD F2, VB(R6)1256none (given)LDU1
I1: FADD F4, F2, F626910RAW F2 (I0), forwarded: RR = WB(I0)FPU1
I2: SD F4, VA(R7)3101314RAW F4 (I1), forwardedLDU2
I3: LD F4, VC(R6)11121516Structural LDU1/LDU2 (I0, I2), then WAW F4 (I1) at Issue until WB(I1) = 10LDU1
I4: SD F4, VC(R7)15161920Structural LDU1/LDU2 (I3 through 16, I2 through 14): LDU2 at 15; RAW F4 (I3) forwarded, RR = 16LDU2

23 cycles; CPI = 23 / 7 ≈ 3.29; speedup = 25 / 23 ≈ 1.09. Forwarding takes one cycle out of each RAW wait (I1, I2, I4); the WAW on F4 still holds I3 at Issue, so the queue behind it moves only with I1's earlier write.

What this variant tests is that forwarding changes the RR rule only (read in the producer's write cycle) and nothing else: I3 still waits at Issue for I1's WB, and the structural rules are untouched. A table that lets I3 issue at 7 has confused this variant with Part B.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Same program and resources as the January 2026 paper, with the check for WAW hazards postponed to the WRITE BACK phase but NO forwarding. How many clock cycles does the program take (the cycle of the last write-back)?

Show model answer

I1 2–7–10–11 and I2 3–12–15–16 as in the basic scheme (no forwarding). I3 issues at 7 (LDU1 free, no WAW check at Issue), reads at 8, completes at 11 — but may write only after I2 has read F4, at 12 (WAR), and after I1's write at 11 (WAW at write-back): WB = 13. I4 issues at 14 on LDU1, reads F4 at 15 = WB(I3) + 1, E 18, WB 19. I5 15–16–17–18. I6 issues at 19 (ALU1 free after 18), 20, 21, WB = 22. CPI = 22 / 7 ≈ 3.14.

The one row that decides is I3's WB: 12 forgets the WAR with I2, which still reads F4 at 12 because there is no forwarding. Postponing the WAW check never removes the WAR test.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

In a scoreboard with forwarding (columns I, RR, E, WB as in the papers), a consumer of a value produced by instruction P may read its operands at the earliest in cycle: (single answer)

  • E of P In its last execution cycle the producer is still computing; the value exists only at the end of that cycle.
  • WB of P, i.e. E of P + 1 The result leaves the unit at the end of P's last execution cycle and is bypassed around the register file: the consumer reads in the cycle P writes (the notes' solution of January 2026: I0 writes at 6, I1 reads at 6).
  • WB of P + 1 That is the rule without forwarding: the value must be in the register file first.
  • I of P + latency Issue is not where execution starts; the read cycle and any waits come between.
Show model answer

Answer 2: RR = WB of the producer (one cycle earlier than without forwarding).

Do not mix this with Tomasulo's convention of the same papers, where the dependent instruction starts executing the cycle after the producer's WRITE RESULT. Same idea, different column.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

On the program of the January 2026 paper, which single optimisation saves more cycles with respect to the basic scheme (25 cycles)? (single answer)

  • Forwarding (23 cycles) Forwarding saves one cycle per RAW wait — two chained waits here — but leaves I3 stuck at Issue behind the WAW on F4.
  • The WAW check postponed to write-back (22 cycles) I3 issues at 7 instead of 12, and I4, I5, I6 — stuck behind it in the in-order issue queue — move with it.
  • They save the same number of cycles 23 and 22 differ; and with both switched on the total is 21, not 25 − 2 − 3.
  • Neither: the structural hazards fix the total Each one alone saves cycles (23 and 22 instead of 25); the structural waits remain, but they are not the whole story.
Show model answer

Answer 2: postponing the WAW check (22) beats forwarding (23); together they give 21.

"Motivate your answer" is the extra point: say that the WAW stall blocks the in-order issue queue, so removing it moves four instructions at once.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in the basic scoreboard scheme a WAR hazard can delay the execution of the later instruction.

  • True The later instruction reads and executes as soon as its own sources are ready; only its write is held.
  • False WAR is checked at Write Result: the writer completes on time and keeps its result in the unit until every earlier reader has read.
Show model answer

False. In the notes' example FADD F6 reads at 14 and completes at 16 although FDIV, an earlier reader of F6, reads only at 21; the WAR delays FADD's write to 22, not its execution.

Give the cycle numbers: they are the motivation the quiz asks for.

Lesson 10 · 62 cycles, one at a time: the scoreboard example and the exam table · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques of a scoreboard-based CPU, saying for each which hazard it addresses and what it cannot fix: 1. splitting the decode stage into Issue and Read Operands; 2. checking WAW hazards at write-back instead of at issue; 3. forwarding; 4. in-order issue with out-of-order completion.

Show model answer

1. Issue decodes and checks the resources (a free unit, no pending write to the destination: structural and WAW hazards), in order; Read Operands waits for the sources to be in the register file (RAW), separately for every issued instruction. Benefit: an instruction waits in the row of its own unit while independent later instructions issue behind it and may read before it (in the notes' example FADD, issued at 13, reads at 14 while FDIV, issued at 8, reads at 21). Cannot fix: a stall at Issue still blocks every later instruction.

2. The register result table is not consulted at Issue, so a later writer of a busy register issues as soon as a unit is free; Write Result then enforces the order of the two writes (WB of the second after WB of the first) in addition to the WAR test. Benefit: the in-order issue queue is not blocked by an output dependence (January 2026: I3 issues at 7 instead of 12 and the three instructions behind it issue 3 cycles earlier: 25 → 22). Cannot fix: the dependence itself — the two writes are still serialised, and the WAR test can still delay the second write; only renaming removes it.

3. A result is bypassed from the unit to a waiting consumer in the cycle it is written instead of after the register-file write: each RAW wait is one cycle shorter (RR = WB of the producer). Benefit: one cycle per link of every true-dependence chain (January 2026: 25 → 23). Cannot fix: the latency itself, and nothing about structural, WAW or WAR waits.

4. Instructions enter in program order, which keeps the hardware simple (the instruction being issued is always the later writer, so one lookup settles a WAW), and leave as soon as they are done, which lets independent instructions overlap long latencies (in the notes' example the subtract and the add complete inside the load–multiply–divide chain, which alone sets the 62 cycles). Cannot fix: WAR and WAW stalls (no renaming), imprecise exceptions (no reorder buffer), and the stalls of the issue queue behind a blocked instruction.

Four short lines per technique are enough: what it is, which hazard it addresses, what it buys (with a number from an exercise if you have one) and what it does not fix. The last line is what separates a full answer from a definition.

Lesson 11 · Names, not values: Tomasulo’s architecture

Lesson 11 · Names, not values: Tomasulo’s architecture · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): 4. Reservation stations; 5. Dynamic register renaming; 6. Out-of-order execution. (Three of the eight items of Question 1, 22 June 2026 — five lines each.)

Show model answer

Reservation stations. Buffers in front of each functional unit. An issued instruction waits there with its operation, its operand values (Vj, Vk) or the tags of the producers it waits for (Qj, Qk), and a busy bit. They decouple issue from execution: an instruction waits locally for its operands instead of blocking the pipeline, the station’s name is the temporary name of its result (renaming), and the control is distributed — each unit schedules itself. Benefit: no central scoreboard, no stall of the instructions behind a waiting one, more instructions in flight than there are registers.

Dynamic register renaming. At issue the destination register’s status field Qi is set to the station’s tag, and every later consumer records that tag instead of the register name. Results are broadcast with the tag; a register is written only if its Qi still holds that tag (the newest writer owns the register). Benefit: WAR and WAW hazards — name dependences — can never stall or corrupt; only true RAW dependences remain; the renaming is done by hardware, invisible to the ISA, so the register count of the ISA no longer limits scheduling.

Out-of-order execution. An instruction leaves its station for the functional unit as soon as Qj = Qk = 0 and the unit is free, independently of program order; results return out of order over the common data bus. Benefit: independent instructions proceed while a long-latency one (a load, a divide) waits, so stalls are filled and the CPI falls. Cost: completion out of order gives imprecise exceptions unless a reorder buffer restores in-order commit.

The form of the real question is a table with five lines per item: write labelled statements, not an essay. Full marks need the concept (what the structure holds or does), the mechanism (tags, Qi, the CDB) and a benefit stated as a benefit; for item 6, naming the cost (imprecise exceptions) shows you know what the next item, the reorder buffer, is for.

Lesson 11 · Names, not values: Tomasulo’s architecture · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Why does the scoreboard stall on a WAR hazard while Tomasulo’s algorithm does not? Where in Tomasulo’s three stages is the WAR hazard resolved?

Show model answer

In the scoreboard an instruction reads its operands from the register file in the Read Operands stage. A later instruction that writes one of those registers must therefore wait at Write Result until the read has happened, otherwise the reader would get the new value: the WAR hazard costs a stall of the writer. In Tomasulo’s algorithm the reader takes a copy of the operand at Issue (into Vj/Vk) if it is available, or records the producer’s tag (Qj/Qk) and receives the value from the CDB; it never reads the register file later. The later writer cannot overwrite anything the reader still needs; it only sets the register’s Qi to its own station. The WAR hazard is dissolved at Issue — copy or tag — and no stage ever stalls for it.

Two sentences carry the marks: “the reader copies or tags at issue and never returns to the register” and “the writer renames the register to its own station”. The stage asked for is Issue.

The paper wants every pair named “with I# for Fx”. The ones students miss: the anti-dependences of the ADDUI on every earlier reader of R1 (two loads and the store), and the output dependence of I3 on I0 as well as on I1. In the Tomasulo table of part 2 (lesson 12) all the WAR and WAW entries are “solved by renaming” — but part 1 asks you to find them.

Lesson 11 · Names, not values: Tomasulo’s architecture · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

In Tomasulo’s algorithm, a reservation station is released: (single answer)

  • when the instruction is issued into it At issue the station is filled, not freed: it must hold the operands or tags until the instruction can execute.
  • when the instruction starts execution in the functional unit The station stays busy while the unit works; it still has to deliver the result with its tag.
  • when the instruction writes its result on the CDB Write result: broadcast the pair, fill every waiting field, write the register if Qi matches, free the station.
  • when the next instruction of the same type is issued A later instruction can take the station only because it is already free — from the cycle after the write result.
Show model answer

Answer 3: the station is busy from Issue through Write Result and reusable from the next cycle.

The notes themselves say “the RS is now free” during execution on page 155; that is the wrong answer here and the wrong ISSUE column in the exercise.

Lesson 11 · Names, not values: Tomasulo’s architecture · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which of the following can stall the ISSUE stage of Tomasulo’s algorithm? (multiple answers)

  • a RAW hazard on a source register No: the instruction is issued with the producer’s tag in Qj or Qk and waits inside its station.
  • no free reservation station of the required type The structural hazard — the most common “STRUCTURAL” entry in the exam tables.
  • a WAW hazard on the destination register No: the destination is renamed to the new station; the older writer’s claim is simply forgotten.
  • a full load/store buffer A load or store needs a free buffer of its own kind, exactly as an FP operation needs a free station.
  • an unresolved branch in a machine without speculation Without speculation the instructions after the branch cannot be issued until it resolves — dynamic scheduling stays inside the basic block.
Show model answer

Answers 2, 4 and 5. Data hazards never stall issue in Tomasulo’s algorithm: RAW waits in the station, WAR and WAW are dissolved by the tags.

The question tests the one thing the scoreboard and Tomasulo disagree about at Issue: WAW stalls the scoreboard there and never stalls Tomasulo.

Lesson 11 · Names, not values: Tomasulo’s architecture · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: in Tomasulo’s algorithm a store instruction writes its result on the common data bus.

  • True A store has no destination register; nothing can wait for its tag, so there is nothing to broadcast.
  • False A store waits for its address and its data (the data may arrive from the CDB), then writes memory. It listens on the bus and never speaks on it.
Show model answer

False: the store buffer monitors the CDB for its data and writes memory; it broadcasts nothing. In the exam table its WRITE RESULT entry is the cycle the memory write completes.

“Motivate your answer” is worth the extra point: say what the store waits for and where its result goes.

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): 4. Reservation stations; 5. Dynamic register renaming; 6. Out-of-order execution. (Five lines each.)

Show model answer

Reservation stations. Buffers in front of each functional unit, each holding one issued instruction: its operation, its operand values (Vj, Vk) if they were available at issue, otherwise the tags (Qj, Qk) of the stations that will produce them, and a busy bit; the station's name is the tag under which its result will be broadcast. Benefits: the instruction leaves the in-order front end at once (a RAW dependence never stalls issue), waits locally and fires as soon as both operands arrive (data-driven execution); hazard detection is distributed, there is no central scoreboard; the stations give pending results more names than the ISA has registers. Cost: when all stations of a class are busy, issue stalls (structural hazard).

Dynamic register renaming. At issue the destination register's status field Qi is set to the station's tag, so the register always names its youngest writer; a consumer copies the value or the producer's tag at issue and never reads the register later. A result is broadcast with its tag, captured by every waiting station, and written into the register only if Qi still holds that tag. Benefits: WAR and WAW hazards (name dependences created by register reuse in loops and by the compiler's allocation) can neither stall nor corrupt — only true RAW dependences remain; the ISA's register count no longer limits scheduling; it is invisible to the program.

Out-of-order execution. An instruction starts in its unit as soon as Qj = Qk = 0 and the unit is free, independently of program order; results return out of order on the common data bus, one per cycle. Benefits: independent instructions proceed while a long-latency one (a load miss, a divide) waits, so stalls are hidden and throughput rises (lower CPI); latencies unknown at compile time are tolerated. Cost: completion out of order makes exceptions imprecise — a reorder buffer with in-order commit restores them.

The paper gives five blank lines per item: write short labelled statements, mechanism first, then "benefit:", then "cost:" where there is one. A complete answer names the fields of a station, the Qi tag and the "write only if the tag still matches" rule, and the exact firing condition of execution.

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Exercise 1, 15 July 2024; registers written without $.) Let's consider the following assembly code containing multiple types of intra-loop dependences. I1 FADD F2, F2, F22710RAW with I0 for F2; WAW with I0 (renaming)RS3FPU1
I2 FADD F2, F2, F431114RAW with I1 for F2; WAR with I1, WAW with I0, I1 (renaming)RS4FPU1
I3 SD F2, A(R1)41519RAW with I2 for F2RS2LDU2
I4 ADDUI R1, R1, 8568WAR with I0, I3 for R1 (renaming)RS5ALU1
I5 BNE R1, R2, FOR6911RAW with I4 for R1RS6ALU1

Writes on the CDB at 6, 8, 10, 11, 14 and the memory write at 19: no tie. CPI = 19 / 6 ≈ 3.17.

Every instruction issues one cycle after the previous one: with only one store the two load/store stations suffice. The whole loop is one RAW chain through F2, LD → FADD → FADD → SD; the integer tail runs underneath it. If you name a WAR or a WAW in the Hazards column, add "solved by renaming", so that it cannot be read as a stall.

Two traps. The third load finds both load/store stations busy at cycles 3–6 and issues only at 7, and in-order issue holds every later instruction behind it although the FP and integer units are idle — two stations per class cannot hold three loads at once. And the single CDB produces two ties, at 12 and at 17: the older instruction writes, the younger slips. The second FP unit changes nothing here, because I4 depends on I3 anyway.

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the loop of Exercise 1 of 22 June 2026 (this lesson's loop) on the same CPU, except that the LOAD/STORE class has 3 reservation stations instead of 2 (still 2 load/store units). Calculate the CPI of one iteration.

Show model answer

The store now finds a free station at cycle 5 (ISSUE 5 instead of 7), ADDUI issues at 6 (6 / 7 / 9) and BNE at 7 (7 / 10 / 12, no tie on the CDB any more). But the store still needs F2 from I3, written at 14: START EXEC 15, WRITE RESULT 15 + 4 = 19. CPI = 19 / 7 ≈ 2.71, unchanged.

The extra station removes the structural stall and the CDB tie, but neither was on the critical path LD → FADD → FADD → SD. The typical wrong answer moves the store's WRITE RESULT two cycles earlier together with its ISSUE (17 / 7 ≈ 2.43).

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

In Tomasulo's algorithm a reservation station is released: (single answer)

  • when the instruction is issued into it Issue is when it is allocated; it then holds the instruction's operands and tags.
  • when the instruction starts execution in the functional unit The station stays busy during execution; the exam counts it busy through the write cycle.
  • when the instruction writes its result on the CDB Step 4 of Write Result frees the station; it can take a new instruction from the next cycle.
  • when the next instruction of the same type is issued If no station is free, the next instruction of that type waits; it never evicts.
Show model answer

Answer 3: a station is busy from ISSUE through WRITE RESULT and reusable from the cycle after the write.

The notes' page 155 suggests answer 2 (the red pen in the section on Execute); every solved table is built on answer 3 (the June 2026 store issues at 7, not at 5).

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following can stall the ISSUE stage of Tomasulo's algorithm? (multiple answers)

  • a RAW hazard on a source register No: the instruction is issued with a tag in Qj or Qk and waits inside its station.
  • no free reservation station of the required type The structural hazard of every exam table: the instruction and everything behind it wait.
  • a WAW hazard on the destination register No: the destination is simply renamed to the new station; the older writer's broadcast will be dropped at the register file.
  • a full load/store queue Memory operations need a buffer entry, exactly like a station.
  • an unresolved branch, in a machine without speculation Without speculation the instructions after the branch must wait for it (the notes list it among the issue stalls); without a prediction the next instruction is not even known.
Show model answer

Answers 2, 4 and 5. Data hazards never stall issue: RAW waits in the station, WAW and WAR are dissolved by renaming.

The quiz checks that you separate the issue stall of the scoreboard (a WAW, or a busy unit) from Tomasulo's: a missing station or buffer entry and, without speculation, a pending branch — never a data hazard.

Lesson 12 · Issue, execute, write result: the three stages and the exam table · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in Tomasulo's algorithm a store instruction writes its result on the Common Data Bus. Motivate your answer.

  • True A store produces no register value; nothing in the machine could be waiting for its tag.
  • False A store writes memory when its address and data are ready; the WRITE RESULT cell of the table is the end of that memory write, and it takes no CDB slot.
Show model answer

False. A store waits in its load/store station for the address and the data (the data may arrive by tag over the CDB), then writes memory; it broadcasts nothing because no later instruction reads a register from it. In the June 2026 table the store's WRITE RESULT = 15 + 4 = 19 is its memory write, and it never competes for the bus.

"Motivate your answer" carries its own point in the papers: name the mechanism (no register result, a memory write, no tag anyone waits for) and, if you can, the cycle from a table.

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): 4. Reservation stations; 5. Dynamic register renaming; 6. Out-of-order execution.

Show model answer

Reservation stations. Buffers in front of each functional unit. An issued instruction waits there with its opcode, its operand values (Vj, Vk) if they were available at issue, otherwise the tags (Qj, Qk) of the stations that will produce them; the station is itself the tag under which the result will be broadcast. Benefits: the instruction leaves the in-order front end at once (no issue stall on a RAW hazard), waits locally and fires as soon as both operands arrive, results reach every waiting station and the register file in one cycle through the common data bus (forwarding), and hazard detection is distributed instead of centralized in a scoreboard.

Dynamic register renaming. At issue the destination register's status field Qi is set to the station's tag, so the register names its youngest producer; consumers take a value or a tag at issue and never consult the register name again. Benefits: WAR hazards disappear (a reader owns its copy or its producer's tag), WAW hazards disappear (only the broadcast whose tag matches Qi is written; an older writer's result is dropped), so the false dependences created by register reuse no longer serialize execution; more instructions in flight; loop iterations overlap without unrolling.

Out-of-order execution. An instruction executes as soon as its operands and a unit are available, whatever older instructions are doing: issue stays in order, execution and completion do not. Benefits: a long-latency instruction (a cache miss, a 40-cycle divide) no longer blocks independent work behind it — in the classic example FADD, issued after FDIV, writes its result at cycle 13 while FDIV finishes at 59 — giving higher unit utilization and IPC. Cost: results written out of order make exceptions imprecise and speculation hard to undo; the reorder buffer restores in-order commit.

Five lines per item on the paper. The examiner looks for the mechanism (fields, tags, the broadcast, Qi = youngest writer) and for the benefit stated as a hazard class removed or a stall avoided — not for the word "faster". A concrete number from the example ("FADD writes at 13, before the older FDIV") shows you have run it.

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

The same six instructions run with latencies load 2, add/sub 2, mult 10, div 40, one adder, one multiplier-divider and two independent load units (the second load starts the cycle after its issue). In which cycle does FDIV write its result?

Show model answer

LD F2: issue 2, start 3, write 5. FMUL: start 6, write 16. FDIV: start 17 (F0 written at 16, the multiplier free), write 17 + 40 = 57. With one load unit held through its write cycle the chain starts two cycles later and the answer is 59.

The examiner checks that you propagated the two cycles along the chain and did not touch anything off it: FADD moves from 13 to 11 too, but it is not asked.

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

In Tomasulo's algorithm a result is broadcast on the CDB with the tag RS3. The register file writes it into F6 if and only if:

  • F6 is the destination of the broadcasting instruction Not enough: if a younger instruction has renamed F6 since, the write must be skipped.
  • Qi[F6] = RS3 The register file compares the tag with its Qi field: a match means RS3 is still the youngest writer of F6. This is how WAW is handled.
  • no reservation station is reading F6 Readers never block a write in Tomasulo: they hold a value or a tag of their own.
  • RS3 is the oldest busy station Age decides who gets the bus when two results are ready, not whether the register accepts the value.
Show model answer

Answer 2. If Qi[F6] = RS4, a younger writer owns the name and the broadcast is dropped by the register file (the stations waiting for RS3 still take it).

A quiz in the style of the 2026 block: single answer, one point.

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which hazards make a scoreboard stall but are eliminated by Tomasulo's algorithm?

  • RAW only RAW waits remain in both machines: a value that does not exist yet cannot be forwarded.
  • WAR and WAW Renaming through station tags: readers keep their copy or their producer's tag, the register file honours only the youngest tag.
  • structural hazards on the functional units Both machines wait for a busy unit; Tomasulo even adds a structural resource, the single CDB.
  • all data hazards Only the two name hazards; FMUL still waited four cycles in MUL1 for F2.
Show model answer

Answer 2. In the example the scoreboard held FADD's write from 16 to 22 for a WAR with FDIV; Tomasulo wrote it at 13.

The distractor "all data hazards" is the one students pick after reading "no hazards occurred" on page 182.

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which statements about the Common Data Bus are correct? (multiple answers)

  • It carries a value together with the tag of the producing reservation station The pair ⟨tag, value⟩ is what every listener matches against its own tags.
  • Every register of the register file is overwritten by each broadcast Only the register whose Qi equals the tag; the others ignore it.
  • At most one result can be broadcast per cycle on a single CDB The structural limit of the bus: the older instruction goes first, the younger slips.
  • A reservation station whose Qj equals the broadcast tag copies the value and clears Qj That is the capture: Vj takes the value, Qj becomes 0.
  • Stores write their data on the CDB A store writes memory; it never uses the bus, which is why it cannot collide with anybody on it.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 3 and 4.

The "multiple answer" form of the 2026 quizzes: tick every true statement, and nothing is said about penalties, so reason about each option on its own.

Lesson 13 · Tomasulo cycle by cycle, and what it fixes compared with the scoreboard · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Why can the original Tomasulo algorithm not provide precise exceptions, and what is added to fix it?

Show model answer

Results are written into the register file at write result, in completion order, which is not program order: in the classic example FADD writes F6 at cycle 13 while the older FDIV finishes at 59. If FDIV raised an exception, F6 would already hold a value produced by a younger instruction and no earlier instruction could be guaranteed complete — the architectural state is inconsistent and in-place writes cannot be undone. The fix is a reorder buffer: results go to the ROB at write result and are committed to the register file in program order, so at an exception everything older has committed and everything younger is discarded. The scoreboard has the same problem, since it too writes registers in place and out of order.

Three things earn the marks: "out of order" plus "in place", the definition of precise (all before complete, none after visible), and the ROB as an in-order commit point.

Lesson 14 · Tomasulo under pressure: structural squeezes and CDB fights

Lesson 14 · Tomasulo under pressure: structural squeezes and CDB fights · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by a Tomasulo-style processor: (1) reservation stations; (2) tags on the Common Data Bus; (3) in-order issue with out-of-order execution. For each, say what it costs, with an example from an exercise.

Show model answer

(1) A reservation station is a buffer in front of a class of functional units that holds an issued instruction together with its operand values (Vj, Vk) if they are already available, or the tags (Qj, Qk) of the stations that will produce them. Benefits: the instruction leaves the issue stage without waiting for its operands, so later independent instructions can issue; the station is also the renamed destination, which removes WAR and WAW stalls (the February 2025 ADDI overwrites R0 while two stores still hold their copies of the old R0). Cost: the number of stations bounds what can be issued; when every station of a class is busy, issue stalls (RS1 and RS2 busy at cycles 5–8 of the February paper).

(2) When a unit finishes, the result is broadcast on the Common Data Bus as the pair (tag, value), the tag being the station of the producer. Every station and register whose Qj, Qk or tag field matches captures the value in the same cycle, so a consumer can start the next cycle without a trip through the register file and without a stall on name dependences. Cost: one bus delivers one result per cycle; when two are ready the older goes first and the younger waits, keeping its station (midterm 2025, cycles 12–16: five results, I7 writes at 15 instead of 12).

(3) Instructions issue strictly in program order, one per cycle, but start executing as soon as their own operands and a unit are available, and finish out of order: long-latency instructions no longer block independent ones (I6 ADDI executes at 12 while the FP work is pending), the hardware tolerates unpredictable latencies such as cache misses, and the compiler needs no knowledge of the pipeline. Cost: in-order issue means one blocked instruction blocks everything behind it; out-of-order completion makes exceptions imprecise, and nothing can pass an unresolved branch until a reorder buffer (lesson 17) adds speculation.

Each sub-point is worth a short labelled block: concept, benefit, cost, example. A complete answer names the tag as the station, says that WAR/WAW cost nothing and why, and states the structural limits (stations, bus) next to the benefits.

The midterm 2025 code of this lesson runs on the same machine but with two FP units (FPU1, FPU2) of latency 4, still a single Common Data Bus. Complete the table and calculate the CPI.

I1 1 / 2 / 6 RS1 LDU1; I2 2 / 3 / 7 RS2 LDU2; I3 3 / 4 / 8 RS3 FPU1; I4 4 / 5 / 9 RS4 FPU2 (no unit wait); I5 7 / 8 / 12 RS1 LDU1 (structural: RS1, RS2 busy at 5–6); I6 8 / 9 / 13 RS2 LDU2; I7 9 / 10 / 14 RS5 ALU1 (ready at 12: CDB taken by I5 at 12 and I6 at 13); I8 10 / 11 / 15 RS6 ALU2 (ready at 13: behind I6 and I7); I9 15 / 16 / 18 RS5 ALU1 (RS5 free after 14; R2 arrives at 15); I10 16 / 19 / 21 RS6 ALU2 (R3 at 18). Lesson 14 · Tomasulo under pressure: structural squeezes and CDB fights · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the February 2025 exercise the FP latency becomes 4 instead of 2; everything else is unchanged. Give the last WRITE RESULT cycle and the CPI.
