I2 starts 10, writes 14; I3 starts 15, writes 19; I4 starts 15 (F4 at 14), writes 21; I6 11 / 12 / 13 and I7 14 / 15 / 16 unchanged; I5 starts 20 (F6 at 19), writes 26. Write cycles 8, 9, 13, 14, 16, 19, 21, 26: no bus collision. Last WRITE RESULT = 26, CPI = 26 / 8 = 3.25.

Only the two FP operations of the chain move, by two cycles each; the structural hole at 5–8 and the integer tail are untouched.

Lesson 14 · Tomasulo under pressure: structural squeezes and CDB fights · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the February 2025 exercise the BNE cannot issue at cycle 12 because: (single answer)

  • it has a RAW hazard on R0 It has, but a RAW hazard delays START EXEC, not ISSUE: the instruction would issue with a tag.
  • ALU1 is executing the ADDI The unit is checked at START EXEC; issue only needs a station.
  • the only integer reservation station, RS5, is occupied by the ADDI until its WRITE RESULT Correct: a station is busy from ISSUE through WRITE RESULT (13), so the BNE issues at 14.
  • the Common Data Bus is busy The bus is used at WRITE RESULT; it never blocks an issue directly.
Show model answer

Answer 3: STRUCTURAL on RS5, the single integer station, busy with I6 through cycle 13.

The notes argue on page 552 whether the label should name RS5 or ALU1: in the issue stage it is the station that is checked.

Lesson 14 · Tomasulo under pressure: structural squeezes and CDB fights · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the Tomasulo algorithm a reservation station is released: (single answer)

  • when the instruction issues At issue the station is just filled.
  • when the instruction starts executing The station keeps the operands and the tag while the unit works.
  • when the instruction writes its result Correct: the station is busy through WRITE RESULT and can be reused from the next cycle.
  • when the next instruction of the same class issues The next instruction of the class needs a free station; it cannot free one.
Show model answer

Answer 3. This is why a result that waits for the bus delays the issue of later instructions.

Calibrate on the given row: a load that writes at 6 lets the next load/store instruction take its station at 7.

Lesson 14 · Tomasulo under pressure: structural squeezes and CDB fights · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: "A single Common Data Bus can delay the issue of later instructions."

  • True Correct: a result that loses the bus keeps its station; an instruction that needs that station cannot issue (midterm: I7 writes at 15 instead of 12 and I9 issues at 16 instead of 13).
  • False The bus delays writes directly, but a delayed write holds a station, and a held station blocks an issue.
Show model answer

True. Motivation: stations are released at WRITE RESULT; a write delayed by bus arbitration keeps the station busy, and in-order issue stalls on the missing station.

The one-point motivation is the chain bus → station → issue; name the cycles of an exercise.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Two sub-points in the form of the June 2026 Question 1, which had eight of them for 8 points.) Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques, five lines each: (a) dynamic register renaming in a Tomasulo-style processor; (b) loop unrolling with static register renaming by the compiler. Say for each what it costs and which hazards it removes.

Show model answer

(a) WAR and WAW are name dependences, caused only by reusing the few ISA register names. At issue, the reservation station that will produce a result becomes its name (tag): the register status records the tag, consumers keep either the value (Vj, Vk) or the producer's tag (Qj, Qk), and results are broadcast on the CDB as (tag, value). Benefits: WAR/WAW never stall, more instructions in flight, loop iterations overlap on unchanged code. Costs: tag comparators in every station, CDB bandwidth, limited by the number of stations. RAW remains.

(b) The compiler copies the loop body u times, folds the addresses into the displacements, keeps one index update and one branch, and gives each copy fresh ISA registers ((u − 1)ρ more), so the copies share no names. Benefits: the overhead is paid once per u elements (w + o/u instructions per element instead of w + o) and a large block of independent instructions can be scheduled — loads first, FP operations next, stores last. Costs: code size, register pressure, a remainder loop when the trip count is not a multiple of u; the latencies must be known at compile time. RAW inside each copy remains.

The examiner checks that the two renamings are kept apart — tags invisible to the ISA versus real register names — and that each technique is credited with removing WAR/WAW only, never RAW. A benefit without its cost is half an answer.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Midterm of 5 May 2025, Question 3, as reproduced in the notes p. 534.) Let's consider the following loop code where registers R1 and R2 are initialized to 0 and R3 is initialized to 400:

LOOP: LD    F2, 0(R1)
      LD    F4, 0(R2)
      FADD  F6, F2, F4
      SD    F6, 0(R1)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 4
      ADDUI R2, R2, 4
      BNE   R1, R3, LOOP

How many iterations of the loop? How many instructions per iteration? How many instructions due to the loop overhead per iteration? How many instructions are executed globally? How many branch instructions are executed globally? Let's consider a loop unrolling version of the code with unrolling factor 2, and then with unrolling factor 4: answer the same questions, and in addition: how many more registers are needed due to register renaming? What are the registers that need to be renamed? How much is the global instruction count decrease? What is the best unrolling factor between 2 and 4? Explain why.

Show model answer

Original loop: R1 goes 0, 4, …, 396 before the branch falls through at 400, so 400/4 = 100 iterations; 7 instructions per iteration; 3 of them overhead (ADDUI R1, ADDUI R2, BNE), 4 useful; 700 instructions and 100 branches executed.

Unrolling factor 2 — the body with the second copy renamed:

LOOP: LD    F2, 0(R1)
      LD    F4, 0(R2)
      FADD  F6, F2, F4
      SD    F6, 0(R1)
      LD    F8, 4(R1)
      LD    F10, 4(R2)
      FADD  F12, F8, F10
      SD    F12, 4(R1)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 8
      ADDUI R2, R2, 8
      BNE   R1, R3, LOOP

50 iterations (100 is a multiple of 2, no remainder loop); 11 instructions per iteration; overhead still 3; 550 instructions and 50 branches executed. Registers: with the same names, the second copy could not write F2, F4, F6 before the first copy had read them (WAR/WAW), so the copies would stay serialised; F2, F4, F6 are renamed to F8, F10, F12: 3 more registers, (u − 1) × 3. Decrease: (700 − 550)/700 = 21.43 %.

Unrolling factor 4: copies 3 and 4 use F14/F16/F18 and F20/F22/F24 with displacements 8 and 12; ADDUI R1, R1, 16 and ADDUI R2, R2, 16. 25 iterations; 19 instructions per iteration; overhead 3; 475 instructions and 25 branches executed; (4 − 1) × 3 = 9 more registers, F8 … F24 — the same three registers F2, F4, F6 renamed three times. Decrease: (700 − 475)/700 = 32.14 %.

Best factor: 4. Iterations and branches halve again (50 → 25) and the overhead per useful add falls from 1.5 to 0.75 instructions. The price is 12 live FP registers instead of 6 and a 19-instruction body; factor 4 is preferable if the architectural register file holds the 12 live values without spilling and the code size is acceptable, otherwise factor 2 is the safer choice.

Write the unrolled code: the displacements (0, 4 / 0, 4, 8, 12) and the scaled increments (8 / 16) are where points are lost. State explicitly that 100 is a multiple of 2 and of 4, so no remainder loop is needed. The renaming here is the compiler's, with ISA registers — do not write that "the hardware" renames them.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

The loop of this lesson, I0 LD F0, 0(R1); I1 FMUL F4, F0, F2; I2 SD F4, 0(R1); I3 SUBI R1, R1, 8; I4 BNE R1, R2, LOOP, runs with R1 initialized to 80 and R2 to 0. (a) How many iterations, instructions per iteration, overhead instructions per iteration, instructions and branches executed globally? (b) Write the unrolled version of the loop with unrolling factor 2 by using register renaming. (c) For the unrolled version: iterations, instructions per iteration, overhead, global instructions and branches, how many more registers and which ones, and the decrease of the global instruction count.

Show model answer

(a) R1 takes the values 80, 72, …, 8 at the top of the body: 80/8 = 10 iterations; 5 instructions per iteration, 2 of them overhead (SUBI, BNE); 50 instructions and 10 branches executed.

(b)

LOOP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
      FMUL  F4, F0, F2
      SD    F4, 0(R1)
      LD    F6, -8(R1)
      FMUL  F8, F6, F2
      SD    F8, -8(R1)
      SUBI  R1, R1, 16
      BNE   R1, R2, LOOP

(c) 5 iterations; 8 instructions per iteration; overhead still 2; 40 instructions and 5 branches executed. 2 more registers, (2 − 1) × 2: F0 → F6 and F4 → F8 in the second copy; F2 is only read and is not renamed. Decrease: (50 − 40)/50 = 20 %.

Two traps: forgetting that F2 needs no fresh name (it is never written), and leaving the second copy's displacements at 0 instead of −8. The SUBI immediate doubles; the branch is unchanged. Part (b) is a real exam request: the paper of 11 February 2025 asked to "Write the unrolled version of the loop with unrolling factor 2 by using Register Renaming" (notes p. 568) before running it through the reorder buffer of lesson 18.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Same loop, R1 = 80 (10 iterations). It is unrolled by a factor of 4; since 10 is not a multiple of 4, the leftover elements are handled by a remainder loop of the original body. How many instructions are executed in total?

Show model answer

10 = 2 × 4 + 2: two iterations of the unrolled body (3 × 4 + 2 = 14 instructions each) and two iterations of the original body (5 each): 2 × 14 + 2 × 5 = 38 instructions, with 2 + 2 = 4 branches; the decrease is (50 − 38)/50 = 24 %, less than the 30 % the formula o(1 − 1/u)/(w + o) promises for u = 4, because the remainder pays full overhead.

The examiner wants the split 8 + 2 elements written down; an answer of 35 (10/4 × 14 with a fractional iteration) shows the remainder loop was not understood.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Single answer.) In the scheduled, unrolled-by-4 loop, SUBI R1, R1, 32 is moved above the store of the fourth element, originally SD F16, -24(R1). After the move the store must read:

  • SD F16, -24(R1) R1 has already been decreased by 32: this writes to R1 − 56 of the old base, an element of a later iteration.
  • SD F16, 8(R1) The old −24 relative to the old R1 is −24 + 32 = +8 relative to the new one: the same address.
  • SD F16, -56(R1) The correction goes the other way: the base got smaller, so the displacement must grow.
  • SD F16, 0(R1) 0(R1) after the update is the first element of the next block, not the fourth of this one.
Show model answer

SD F16, 8(R1): displacement = old displacement − (change of R1) = −24 − (−32) = 8.

This is the line of the notes' page 192 that the hook asked you to find. Whenever a memory access crosses the index update in a schedule, correct its displacement by the update's immediate.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(15 July 2024, quiz 1, single answer — also in lesson 7; it is the vocabulary of this lesson.) Let's consider the following loop: for (i=1; i<=100; i++) { X[i] = X[i] + X[i-1]; /*S1*/ Z[i] = X[i] + Y[i-1] /*S2*/ }. How many loop-carried dependencies are in the code?

  • Answer 1: Only one in S2 because Z[i] depends on Y[i-1] Y is never written in the loop, so Y[i-1] is an input, not a value produced by an earlier iteration.
  • Answer 2: Only one in S1 because X[i] depends on X[i-1] S1 reads X[i-1], which S1 itself wrote in the previous iteration: a true dependence carried from iteration i − 1 to iteration i.
  • Answer 3: Both of them S2's dependence on X[i] is on the same iteration's S1, not on a previous iteration.
Show model answer

Answer 2. The loop-carried dependence is S1 → S1 through X: iteration i reads X[i−1], produced by iteration i − 1. S2 depends on S1 within the iteration, and Y is read only.

Compare with the loop of this lesson: its only loop-carried true dependence is the index R1, which is why its iterations can be overlapped — a loop like S1 could not be, whatever the renaming.

Lesson 15 · Same names, no conflicts: unrolling, renaming and scheduling · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

(True/False, motivate your answer.) "Tomasulo's algorithm performs loop unrolling dynamically: the hardware replicates the loop body in the reservation stations so that several iterations execute together."

  • True Nothing is replicated: every instruction of every iteration is issued once, from the instruction stream, into a station of its own.
  • False Tomasulo overlaps iterations of the unchanged code: fresh stations and tags per instance give the effect of unrolling without copying a single instruction (Table 20).
Show model answer

False. Loop unrolling is a compiler transformation of the program text. Tomasulo issues the instructions of each iteration as they arrive, in order, each into its own reservation station with its own tag; because operands are tracked by tag and not by register name, the LD/FMUL/SD of iteration i + 1 can execute while those of iteration i are still waiting — the "dynamic loop unrolling effect" — but the code is never modified or replicated.

The motivation must name the mechanism — stations and tags per instance — and the word "effect": the examiner wants to see that you know what the hardware does and does not do.

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style), in about five lines each: (1) dynamic instruction scheduling; (4) reservation stations; (5) dynamic register renaming; (6) out-of-order execution. Use as example the loop LD F0, 0(R1); FMUL F4, F0, F2; SD F4, 0(R1); SUBI R1, R1, 8; BNE R1, R2, LOOP, not unrolled, whose first LD misses in the cache.

Show model answer

Dynamic instruction scheduling. The hardware decides at run time when each instruction executes, preserving the data flow: instructions issue in program order and wait in reservation stations until their operands are available, instead of stalling the pipeline behind the first one that must wait. Benefit: it adapts to latencies known only at run time, such as a cache miss. In the loop the second iteration's LD, FMUL and SD issue at cycles 6, 7 and 8 while the first LD is still missing; the same code runs well on different implementations. Cost: stations, tag-compare logic and a common data bus.

Reservation stations. Buffers in front of the functional units (Load1–3, Mult1–2, Store1–3 in the example) holding the operation, the operand values (Vj, Vk) or the tags of the stations that will produce them (Qj, Qk). An instruction enters a free station even when its operands are missing (the first FMUL at cycle 2 with Qj = Load1) and fires when they arrive on the CDB. Benefits: distributed hazard detection, operands read once, and the station names serve as tags for renaming. Limit: a station is held from issue to write result, so their number bounds the overlap; with two multiply stations the third FMUL cannot issue until cycle 15.

Dynamic register renaming. At issue, the station or buffer that will produce a result becomes the name of that result; the register result status maps each register to its youngest producer (F0: Load1, then Load2 from cycle 6; F4: Mult1, then Mult2). Readers record the tag, not the register, and the register file accepts a broadcast only from the youngest producer: at cycle 10 M[80] goes to Mult1 but not to F0. Benefit: WAR and WAW hazards disappear without stalls, so two iterations using the same F0 and F4 are in flight together (dynamic loop unrolling); only RAW dependences remain.

Out-of-order execution. Instructions execute and complete when their operands are ready, not in program order; issue stays in order. The second LD completes at cycle 10, before the first FMUL starts at 11. Benefit: long latencies (misses, FP operations) are overlapped with independent work, functional units stay busy and CPI drops. Requirements and limits: renaming against WAR and WAW; without a reorder buffer results reach the registers out of order, so exceptions are imprecise and nothing may execute past an unresolved branch.

Four of the eight sub-points of the June 2026 open question, one point each. Give a mechanism and a benefit in every item, with its cost or limit; the numbers of the loop are the evidence. Do not write "Tomasulo is speculative": speculation (item 2) and the reorder buffer (item 7) are the answers of lesson 17.

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following assembly code containing multiple types of intra-loop dependences. Complete the following table by inserting all types of true-data-dependences, anti-dependences and output dependences for each instruction (I0: None).

I0  LOOP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
I1        FMUL  F0, F0, F2
I2        SD    F0, 0(R1)
I3        SUBI  R1, R1, 8
I4        BNE   R1, R2, LOOP

Let's consider the previous assembly code to be executed on a CPU with dynamic scheduling based on TOMASULO algorithm with all cache HITS, a single Common Data Bus and: 2 RESERVATION STATIONS (RS1, RS2) with 2 LOAD/STORE units (LDU1, LDU2) with latency 4; 2 RESERVATION STATIONS (RS3, RS4) with 1 FP unit (FPU1) with latency 3; 2 RESERVATION STATIONS (RS5, RS6) with 1 INT_ALU/BR unit (ALU1) with latency 2. Please complete the table INSTRUCTION / ISSUE / START EXEC / WRITE RESULT / Hazards Type / RSi / UNIT (row I0 given: 1, 2, 6, None, RS1, LDU1). Calculate the CPI.

Show model answer

Dependences: I0 — None. I1 — true data dependence with I0 for F0; output dependence with I0 for F0. I2 — true data dependence with I1 for F0. I3 — anti-dependence with I0 and I2 for R1. I4 — true data dependence with I3 for R1.

INSTRUCTIONISSUESTART EXECWRITE RESULTHazards TypeRSiUNIT
I0 LD F0, 0(R1)126NoneRS1LDU1
I1 FMUL F0, F0, F22710RAW with I0 for F0; WAW with I0 for F0 (solved by renaming)RS3FPU1
I2 SD F0, 0(R1)31115RAW with I1 for F0RS2LDU2
I3 SUBI R1, R1, 8457WAR with I0, I2 for R1 (solved by renaming)RS5ALU1

The timing is cell for cell the table of section 5: reusing F0 as the destination of FMUL costs nothing, because the SD records the tag RS3, not the name F0. Check the dependence table first — the output dependence of I1 with I0 for F0 is the easiest line to forget — and then the CDB conflict at cycle 10. If you assume that a branch does not use the CDB, write 10 for I4 and say so; the CPI does not change.

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

The notes' loop example (first LD misses: 8 cycles; later LDs hit: 1 cycle; FMUL: 4 cycles; 3 load buffers, 3 store buffers, 2 multiply reservation stations; one memory unit serving one access at a time; a store executes in the cycle after its data arrives and writes memory in the next cycle) is redone with the convention of the exam papers: a reservation station becomes free only in the cycle after its WRITE RESULT. In which clock cycle does the store of the third iteration write to memory?

Show model answer

Iterations 1 and 2 do not change: no station is reused before cycle 15. Mult1 writes at 15 and is free at 16, so the third FMUL issues at 16 with both operands ready (F0 = M[64] since cycle 13), executes from 17 to 20 and writes at 21. The third SD issues at 17 into Store3, receives the product at 21, executes at 22 and writes memory at 23.

One convention moves two numbers: the notes refill Mult1 in the cycle it broadcasts (third FMUL at 15, third SD writing at 22). On the paper, use the papers' rule and state it in one line.

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

In the loop example (the first LD misses in the cache, 8 cycles), at cycle 10 the LD of the first iteration writes M[80] on the Common Data Bus. At that moment the register result status of F0 holds Load2. Which units store the value? (single answer)

  • Register F0 only F0's status names Load2, not Load1: the register file ignores a broadcast whose tag is not its youngest writer.
  • Reservation station Mult1 only Mult1's Qj holds Load1, so it copies the value into Vj; F0 waits for Load2.
  • Both F0 and Mult1 That would let an older value overwrite F0 after a younger load has claimed it — exactly the WAW hazard the tag compare prevents.
  • Store buffer Store1 Store1 waits for the tag Mult1, the producer of its data, not for Load1.
Show model answer

Answer 2. Only Mult1, whose Qj = Load1, captures M[80]; the register file writes F0 only if F0's status equals the broadcast tag, and since cycle 6 it equals Load2.

The distractors test the two halves of the rule: stations match on Q fields, the register file on its own status field.

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

In the same example, why can the FMUL of the third iteration not be issued at cycle 12? (single answer; motivate your answer)

  • RAW hazard on F0 A missing operand never stops issue: the FMUL would simply record the tag Load3 — and F0 is ready at 13 anyway.
  • No free multiply reservation station Mult1 and Mult2 are busy until their results are written at 15 and 16; without a station the instruction cannot issue.
  • The Common Data Bus is busy Nothing is written on the bus at cycle 12, and the bus never delays issue.
  • The branch has not been resolved yet The branch is predicted taken and issue proceeds; the LD of the same iteration issued at 11.
Show model answer

Answer 2: a structural hazard on the reservation stations. A station stays busy from issue to write result; Mult1 holds the first FMUL from cycle 2 to 15 because its load missed, Mult2 holds the second until 16. The third FMUL issues at 15, when Mult1 is freed, and in-order issue keeps the third SD behind it until 16.

The motivation point is for naming the station's lifetime — from ISSUE to WRITE RESULT, not to the end of execution — and the knock-on effect on the SD behind it.

Lesson 16 · Tomasulo meets a loop: eighteen cycles of renaming in action · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: in the loop example, the LD of the second iteration — a cache hit issued at cycle 6 — starts executing only at cycle 10 because two loads to the same address must be executed in program order.

  • True The two loads read different addresses (80 and 72), and two loads never need to be ordered: neither writes memory.
  • False It waits for the single memory unit, busy with the first load's miss until cycle 9 (a blocking cache); with a non-blocking cache it would execute at 7.
Show model answer

False. The addresses differ and loads do not conflict with loads; the delay is structural — one memory unit, occupied by the miss from cycle 2 to 9. Memory disambiguation concerns a load and an earlier store to the same address.

The notes give the wrong reason on pages 202 and 208; the statement is built from it.

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style), in about five lines each: (2) dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution; (5) dynamic register renaming; (6) out-of-order execution; (7) reorder buffer.

Show model answer

Dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution. The outcome and target of a branch are predicted at fetch from run-time history (BHT, BTB). The instructions after the branch are fetched, issued and executed before the branch resolves; their results are forwarded among them through the CDB and the ROB but kept out of the architectural state until the branch is resolved and the instruction commits. On a misprediction the younger entries of the ROB are flushed and fetch restarts at the correct target. Benefit: branches no longer drain the pipeline, and the window of exploitable ILP spans several basic blocks. Cost: wasted work and recovery time on mispredictions, so accuracy matters.

Dynamic register renaming. Each issued instruction that writes a register gets a fresh name for its result — a reservation station in classic Tomasulo, a ROB entry in the speculative version; the rename table maps the architectural register to its newest producer, and a consumer issuing later takes that producer's value or its tag. Benefit: WAR and WAW (name) hazards disappear, so instructions and loop iterations can be overlapped freely and only true (RAW) dependences remain. Cost: a rename table to keep, to restore after a misprediction, and the copy of each value into the architectural register at commit.

Out-of-order execution. Instructions wait in reservation stations and start as soon as their own operands are ready, independently of program order; results are broadcast on the CDB. Issue and commit remain in order. Benefit: a stalled instruction — a cache miss, a long-latency FP operation — does not block independent later ones; functional units stay busy and CPI drops. It requires renaming against WAR/WAW hazards and in-order commit to keep exceptions precise.

Reorder buffer. A circular FIFO: an entry is allocated at issue in program order, written out of order at Write Result (value, ready bit) and drained in order at Commit from the head (head, ready, not speculative). It supplies operands between completion and commit, provides the names for renaming (the ROB number is the tag) and replaces the store buffers, keeping a store's address and data until commit. Benefits: precise exceptions, since nothing younger than a faulting instruction has touched the state; cheap misprediction recovery by discarding the entries after the branch; speculation made safe. Cost: a finite window — a stalled head stops issue when the buffer fills.

Four of the eight sub-points of the June 2026 open question. The examiner looks for the "what it buys" line in each one and for the pairing cost / benefit; "the ROB makes the processor faster" alone earns nothing — it makes speculation safe, and the speed comes from executing past unresolved branches.

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

What is a precise exception (give the three conditions)? Explain how the reorder buffer guarantees precise exceptions in an out-of-order processor, and what happens if an instruction on a mispredicted path raises an exception.

Show model answer

An exception is precise if (1) the processor can stop at a well-defined point corresponding to a specific instruction, (2) all previous instructions are fully committed, and (3) no subsequent instruction has modified the architectural state. With a reorder buffer, results are written to the ROB entry at Write Result, never directly to the register file or memory; only the head commits, in program order. An exception is recorded in the entry of the faulting instruction and taken when that instruction reaches the head: at that moment every older instruction has committed and every younger one is still in the buffer, so the younger entries are discarded and fetch restarts at the handler — the three conditions hold by construction. An exception raised on a mispredicted path never reaches the head: when the branch resolves, the entries after it are flushed together with the recorded exception, so it is never taken.

Give the three conditions as a list and then map each one to a property of the buffer (results parked, in-order commit at the head, flush of the younger entries). The last sentence — a speculative exception is simply discarded — is the part most answers miss.

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Single answer.) In the speculative Tomasulo algorithm, when is the architectural register file updated?

  • At Write Result, when the CDB broadcasts the value At Write Result the value goes to the ROB entry and to the waiting reservation stations; the CDB no longer reaches the register file (Figure 27).
  • At Issue, when the destination register is mapped to a ROB entry Issue writes the rename table, not the register file: a name, not a value.
  • At Commit, when the instruction is at the head of the ROB with its result ready Only then is the instruction certainly part of the program's path and older than nothing unfinished.
  • As soon as the functional unit finishes That is completion; the result exists, but it is parked in the entry with Ready = Yes.
Show model answer

The third option: at Commit. The register file must reflect only instructions that are certainly part of the program's path; at Write Result the value goes to the ROB entry and to the waiting reservation stations.

Keep the contrast straight: in classic Tomasulo the register file is written at Write Result (Table 21's "commit on write-back"); with the ROB only at Commit. In neither design does the value wait inside the reservation station — the red pen on Table 21 above.

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Multiple answers.) Which of the following statements about the reorder buffer are true?

  • The tag broadcast on the CDB is the ROB entry number The ROB number is the unique name of the in-flight result, from issue to commit.
  • The reservation station is released at Commit The station is freed at Write Result; it is the ROB entry that is freed at Commit.
  • A store writes memory at Commit Its address and data wait in the entry; the ROB replaced the store buffer.
  • The tail pointer points to the newest issued instruction The tail points to the next free entry, one past the newest instruction.
  • When head = tail the ROB may be either full or empty The busy bit of the entry decides (p. 232).
  • A load may read memory only when no earlier store in the ROB has an unresolved or equal address Memory disambiguation: a RAW hazard through memory (p. 229, step 3).
Show model answer

True: 1, 3, 5, 6. False: 2 (the station is freed at Write Result, the ROB entry at Commit) and 4 (the tail points to the next free entry).

The two false statements are the two confusions the lesson warns against most: which structure is freed when, and what the tail points to.

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Single answer.) In the Speculative Tomasulo Architecture, what type of hardware block is used to undo speculative instructions in case of a mispredicted branch? Motivate your answer by explaining what happens in the case of a mispredicted branch.

  • Reorder Buffer Speculative results live only in ROB entries; discarding the entries younger than the branch undoes them.
  • Instruction Dispatcher Issue allocates entries; it has no record of results to undo.
  • Store Buffer The speculative design has no store buffer: stores wait in the ROB.
  • Reservation Stations They hold operands and tags, not the commit state; they are squashed as a consequence of the flush.
  • Load Buffers They queue loads before memory access; nothing architectural is kept there.
Show model answer

The Reorder Buffer. A speculative instruction's result is held only in its ROB entry (and in copies inside reservation stations), never in the register file or memory, and the entry is marked speculative. When the branch resolves as mispredicted: all ROB entries younger than the branch are invalidated and the tail is moved back to the entry after the branch; the reservation stations and load buffers working for them are squashed; the rename table is restored to the mapping valid at the branch, so that each register points to its older in-flight producer or to nothing; fetch is redirected to the correct target; the branch itself commits normally. Nothing has to be undone in the architectural state, because nothing speculative ever reached it.

The quiz of the February 2025 paper, as reproduced in the notes (p. 582). The motivation is worth the extra point: name the four actions (invalidate the younger entries, squash their stations, restore the rename table, redirect fetch) and the reason nothing in the registers needs undoing.

Lesson 17 · Guess now, pay later: speculation, precise exceptions and the reorder buffer · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider the following instruction sequence executed on a speculative Tomasulo architecture with a 4-entry ROB, 4 load buffers (Load1–Load4), 4 FP reservation stations (FP1–FP4) and 2 integer reservation stations (Int1–Int2). The BNE is predicted taken. Complete the ROB table and the Rename Table (a) when the ROB becomes full, (b) after In has committed, (c) after the BNE has been found mispredicted and the ROB has been flushed.

In:   LD   F0, 0(R1)
In+1: FADD F2, F0, F0
In+2: BNE  R1, R2, NEXT
In+3: FMUL F6, F2, F2
In+4: FADD F8, F6, F2
Show model answer

(a) The ROB is full after four issues: head = tail = ROB0 and the entry is busy. In+3 is speculative because the BNE in ROB2 is unresolved; the BNE itself is not.

(b) In completes (Ready = Yes, value 10 in ROB0, Load1 released) and commits from the head: F0 = 10 in the register file, ROB0 freed, head ROB1, tail ROB0, and the mapping of F0 cleared. In+1 has captured F0 from the CDB and is executing.

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): (a) dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution; (b) dynamic register renaming; (c) out-of-order execution; (d) reorder buffer. (Five lines each.)

Show model answer

(a) The outcome and target of a branch are predicted at fetch from run-time history (BHT, BTB); the instructions after the branch are fetched, issued and executed before the branch resolves, their results forwarded among themselves but kept out of the architectural state until the branch is validated; on a misprediction the younger instructions are flushed and fetch restarts. Benefit: branches no longer drain the pipeline, the window of exploitable ILP spans several basic blocks. Cost: wasted work and recovery time on a misprediction, so accuracy matters.

(b) Each issued instruction that writes a register gets a fresh name for its result — a reservation station in classic Tomasulo, a ROB entry in the speculative version; the rename (register status) table maps the architectural register to its newest producer, and consumers receive the value or its tag. Benefit: WAR and WAW (name) hazards disappear, instructions and loop iterations can be overlapped freely; only true RAW dependences remain.

(c) Instructions wait in reservation stations and start as soon as their own operands are ready, regardless of program order (data-flow execution), results being broadcast on the CDB. Benefit: a stalled instruction (cache miss, long FP latency) does not block independent later ones; functional units stay busy; CPI falls. It requires in-order commit to keep exceptions precise.

(d) A circular FIFO allocated at issue in program order, written out of order at write result (value, ready bit) and drained in order at commit from the head; it supplies operands between completion and commit, provides the names for renaming and replaces the store buffer (address and data kept until commit). Benefits: precise exceptions (nothing after the faulting instruction has touched the state), cheap misprediction recovery (discard the entries after the branch), safe speculation. Cost: a finite window — a blocked head stalls issue when the buffer fills.

These are sub-points 2, 5, 6 and 7 of the June 2026 question, which gives five blank lines per sub-point. Each line must pair a mechanism with its benefit: "renaming removes WAR/WAW", "the ROB commits in order, so exceptions are precise". Name the hardware (BHT/BTB, reservation station, CDB, head/tail) rather than describing the idea in prose: short labelled statements fit the lines, an essay does not.

How many instructions per iteration? How many control vs datapath instructions? Write the unrolled version of the loop with unrolling factor 2 by using register renaming, and answer the same three questions. Then execute the unrolled version on the Speculative Tomasulo architecture with a 10-entry ROB and 4 load buffers (Load1–Load4), 4 FP reservation stations (FP1–FP4), 2 integer reservation stations (Int1, Int2): complete the ROB and the Rename Table until the ROB becomes full while the first instruction is still in execution due to a cache miss (*).

L1: FADD F4, F2, F2
L2: SD   F4, 0(R1)
L3: ADDI R1, R1, 4
L4: BNE  R1, R2, L0
Show model answer

Iterations: 40/4 = 10. Instructions per iteration: 5. Control vs datapath: 2 (ADDI, BNE) vs 3 (LD, FADD, SD). Iterations 40/8 = 5; 8 instructions per iteration; 2 control vs 6 datapath.

The marks hide in five places: the corrected offset of L5 (4, not 0); "–" in Res. Alloc. for the stores and Int2 for the branch; "No, issued" everywhere behind the head; Spec = Yes only for ROB8–9; and the rename table pointing to the newest producers with the old ones struck through. The notes' solution (pages 568–573) agrees with this table cell for cell except the store offset.

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

The loop LD F0, 0(R1); FMUL F4, F0, F2; SD F4, 0(R1); SUBI R1, R1, 8; BNEZ R1, Loop runs on a speculative Tomasulo machine with an 8-entry ROB, 2 load buffers (Load1, Load2), 2 multiplier reservation stations (Mult1, Mult2) and 2 integer reservation stations (Int1, Int2). The first LD misses in the cache; BNEZ is predicted taken. (a) Show the ROB (with the allocated resources) and the Rename Table at the moment the ROB becomes full. (b) BNEZ then resolves as not taken. Show the ROB, head, tail and Rename Table after the recovery.

Show model answer

(a) ROB0 LD F0, 0(R1) · F0 · Load1 · No, exec.(*) · No; ROB1 FMUL F4, F0, F2 · F4 · Mult1 · No, issued · No; ROB2 SD F4, 0(R1) · Mem [0+R1] · – · No, issued · No; ROB3 SUBI R1, R1, 8 · R1 · Int1 · No, issued · No; ROB4 BNEZ R1, Loop · – · Int2 · No, issued · No; ROB5 LD F0, 0(R1) · F0 · Load2 · No, issued · Yes; ROB6 FMUL F4, F0, F2 · F4 · Mult2 · No, issued · Yes; ROB7 SD F4, 0(R1) · Mem [0+R1] · – · No, issued · Yes. Head = ROB0, tail = ROB0 (busy): full. Rename table: F0 ROB0 ROB5, F4 ROB1 ROB6, R1 ROB3.

(b) ROB5, ROB6, ROB7 are flushed (Busy = No); Load2 and Mult2 are released; head still ROB0 (the load has not committed), tail = ROB5; Rename table: F0 → ROB0, F4 → ROB1 (the older producers are still in flight), R1 → ROB3; fetch restarts at the instruction after BNEZ, which will take ROB5 with Spec = No; the BNEZ in ROB4 commits normally when it reaches the head.

Part (b) is where answers diverge: the tail goes back to the entry after the branch, not to 0; the rename table is restored, not emptied — had ROB0 already committed, F0 would read "–" instead. Say explicitly that nothing is undone in the registers or in memory.

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

The same five-instruction loop runs with R1 initialized at 0, R2 at 96 and the same step of 4. It is unrolled with unrolling factor 4 (register renaming, one index update, one branch). How many instructions are executed in total by the unrolled loop (dynamic instruction count)?

Show model answer

Original: 96/4 = 24 iterations of 5 instructions. Unrolled by 4: 24/4 = 6 iterations of 4 · 3 + 2 = 14 instructions. Dynamic count: 6 × 14 = 84 (down from 120).

The two usual slips: counting 4 · 5 = 20 instructions per unrolled iteration (the index update and the branch are not copied), and forgetting to divide the iteration count by the unrolling factor.

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the speculative Tomasulo algorithm, when is the architectural register file updated? (single answer)

  • At write result, when the CDB broadcasts the value The broadcast reaches the waiting stations and the ROB entry; the registers are not on the CDB any more.
  • At issue, when the destination is renamed Issue writes the rename table and the entry, not a value.
  • At commit, when the instruction is at the head of the ROB with its result ready Only the head writes the architectural state, in program order, and only when Ready and not speculative.
  • As soon as the functional unit finishes That is the moment of write result: the value goes to the entry's Value field.
Show model answer

Answer 3: at commit. The register file must reflect only instructions that are certainly part of the program's path; at write result the value lives in the ROB entry and in the stations that captured it.

Answer 1 is the classic Tomasulo rule (lesson 12) — the quiz checks that you know what the ROB changed.

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

In the Speculative Tomasulo architecture, what type of hardware block is used to undo speculative instructions in case of a mispredicted branch? (single answer; motivate your answer by explaining what happens in the case of a mispredicted branch)

  • Reorder Buffer The entries younger than the branch are discarded before they commit; the architectural state was never touched.
  • Instruction Dispatcher Issue logic allocates stations and entries; it keeps no record of what to undo.
  • Store Buffer The speculative design has no store buffer: the ROB holds the stores' address and data until commit.
  • Reservation Stations They hold operands and tags, not the commit state; they are squashed as a consequence of the flush, not as its mechanism.
  • Load Buffers Same as the stations: released by the flush, not the thing that performs it.
Show model answer

Answer 1, the Reorder Buffer. Every speculative result is held only in its ROB entry (and in station copies), marked speculative, never in the register file or memory. When the branch resolves wrong: the entries younger than the branch are invalidated and the tail goes back to the entry after the branch; their reservation stations and load buffers are squashed; the rename table is restored to the mapping valid at the branch; fetch is redirected to the correct path; the branch itself commits normally. Nothing has to be undone in the architectural state.

This is the February 2025 quiz. The extra point is the motivation: five concrete actions, and the sentence "nothing speculative ever reached the registers or memory".

Lesson 18 · The speculative Tomasulo algorithm, stage by stage — and the ROB exam table · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which statements about the reorder buffer are true? (multiple answers)

  • The tag broadcast on the CDB is the ROB entry number The entry is the unique name of the in-flight result from issue to commit.
  • The reservation station is released at commit The station is released at write result; the ROB entry is released at commit.
  • A store writes memory at commit Address and data wait in the entry; memory is written in program order at the head.
  • The tail pointer points to the newest issued instruction The tail points to the next free entry — the one the next issue will take.
  • When head = tail the ROB may be either full or empty The busy bit of that entry tells the two apart.
  • A load may read memory only when no earlier store in the ROB has an unresolved or equal address Memory disambiguation: a RAW hazard through memory, because stores write only at commit.
Show model answer

True: 1, 3, 5, 6. False: 2 (station freed at write result, entry at commit) and 4 (the tail is the next free entry).

Two of the six are about when something is freed; the 2026 paper's multiple-answer quizzes are built from exactly such timing pairs.

Module 5 · VLIW and static scheduling

Lesson 19 · The compiler takes the wheel: what a VLIW is

Lesson 19 · The compiler takes the wheel: what a VLIW is · question 1 of 5 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by VLIW processors:

  1. Static scheduling by the compiler
  2. Bundle with fixed slots
  3. NOP insertion
  4. Single program counter with at most one branch per bundle
  5. Shared multi-ported register file
  6. Fully pipelined functional units with exposed latencies
  7. Register renaming by the compiler
  8. Hardware support for dynamic events (data-cache misses, branch mispredictions)
Show model answer

1. The compiler analyses data dependences and resource constraints at compile time and decides which operations execute together and in which cycle; the hardware executes the schedule without checking it. Benefit: no dynamic scheduling hardware (no scoreboard, reservation stations or run-time dependence checks), hence less area and power; the compiler sees a much larger instruction window than any hardware. Cost: it must know all latencies and cannot see run-time events.

2. Independent operations are grouped into one very long instruction word; each slot corresponds to one functional unit (e.g. Memory, FP, Integer). Benefit: one fetch delivers k operations, decode is trivial (slot i goes to unit i, no arbitration), and scaling means adding slots.

3. When no independent operation is available for a slot, or a latency must elapse, the compiler fills the slot with a NOP. Benefit: the fixed format and the timing stay explicit, so no stall logic is needed. Cost: wasted slots and larger code; code efficiency = IC / (#cycles × #issues).

4. One PC fetches one bundle per cycle, and a bundle contains at most one branch. Benefit: a single next-PC decision per cycle keeps branch handling simple (in the exam's model the branch is solved in ID with one delay slot).

5. All the operations of a bundle read their operands in the same cycle, and every unit can write a result in every cycle: two read ports and one write port per operation, 8 + 4 for a 4-issue machine. Benefit: every unit gets its operands and writes its result without a bottleneck. Cost: area and power grow quickly with the number of ports, which leads to clustered VLIWs (several smaller register files, each serving a subset of the units).

6. Every unit accepts a new operation each cycle, and its result is available after a fixed latency (e.g. Memory 3, FP 3, Integer 1, 2 to a branch); the compiler places each consumer at least one latency after its producer and fills the gap with independent operations. Benefit: one operation per unit per cycle with no interlocks. Cost: the schedule, hence the binary, depends on the latencies of one implementation.

7. To remove WAR and WAW dependences the compiler writes results into different architectural registers. Benefit: more freedom to reorder and overlap operations, more ILP. Cost: register pressure — every result in flight needs an architectural register; when the register file saturates, values are spilled to memory.

8. The compiler cannot know whether a load will miss in the data cache or whether a static branch hint is wrong. On a miss the processor stalls; on a misprediction the hardware flushes the wrongly fetched bundles. Benefit: correct execution despite unpredictable events, while the regular flow stays free of checks. Cost: a stall freezes the whole schedule, which assumed the hit latency.

The June 2026 paper asks exactly this shape — eight techniques, five lines each, for the Tomasulo-style superscalar; this is its VLIW twin. Give each sub-point its concept in one sentence and its benefit in the next; for 3, 5, 6 and 7 add the cost too, since there the benefit only makes sense against it. Use the precise terms (bundle, slot, NOP, latency, WAR / WAW, renaming, spill), and never let the VLIW hardware "check" or "rename" anything.

Lesson 19 · The compiler takes the wheel: what a VLIW is · question 2 of 5 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following LOOP1 code, where Ri are integer registers and Fi are floating-point registers:

LOOP1: LD    F2, 0(R1)
       FADD  F6, F2, F2
       SD    F6, 0(R1)
       ADDUI R1, R1, 4
       BNE   R1, R3, LOOP1

Given a 3-issue VLIW machine with fully pipelined functional units: 1 Memory Unit with 3 cycles latency; 1 FP ALU with 4 cycles latency; 1 Integer ALU with 1 cycle latency to next Int/FP/L/S & 2 cycle latency to next Branch. The branch is completed with 1 cycle delay slot (branch solved in ID stage). No branch prediction. In the Register File, it is possible to read and write at the same address at the same clock cycle. Considering one iteration of the loop, complete the following table (Memory Unit | Floating Point Unit | Integer Unit, rows C1 … C15) by using the list-based scheduling (do NOT introduce any software pipelining, loop unrolling and modifications to loop indexes) on the 3-issue VLIW machine including the BRANCH DELAY SLOT. Please do not write in NOPs. (C1 is given: LD F2, 0(R1) in the Memory Unit.)

How long is the critical path for a single iteration? What CPI did you achieve? What performance did you achieve in FP ops per cycle? How much is the code efficiency?

Extra question of this lesson, not of the papers: the same loop compiled for a 3-cycle FP ALU (SD in C7) runs unchanged on this machine. Which instruction reads a wrong value?

Show model answer
Memory UnitFloating Point UnitInteger Unit
C1LD F2, 0(R1)
C2
C3
C4FADD F6, F2, F2
C5
C6
C7
C8SD F6, 0(R1)ADDUI R1, R1, 4
C9
C10BNE R1, R3, LOOP1
C11(delay slot)

FADD at C1 + 3 = C4 (F2 from the memory unit); SD at C4 + 4 = C8 (F6 from the FP ALU); ADDUI in C8 with the SD, its last reader of R1 (read and write in the same cycle); BNE at C8 + 2 = C10; delay slot C11.

Critical path = 11 cycles. CPI = 11 / 5 = 2.2. FP ops per cycle = 1 / 11 = 0.09. Code efficiency = 5 / (11 × 3) = 0.15.

Old binary: SD F6, 0(R1) in C7 reads F6 one cycle before the 4-cycle FADD (issued in C4) writes it, so it stores the F6 of the previous iteration. A VLIW has no hardware that detects the RAW hazard.

The table is the June 2026 EX2 (LOOP1) with one number changed, FP latency 4 instead of 3. Four placements decide it: the RAW distances (3 after the load, 4 after the FADD), the ADDUI in the same cycle as the SD, the 2-cycle distance to the branch and the delay slot counted in the 11. The extra question is this lesson's point: on a VLIW a latency change does not slow the old binary down, it makes it wrong.

Lesson 19 · The compiler takes the wheel: what a VLIW is · question 3 of 5 · open in the lesson

True or False: "A 4-issue VLIW processor requires 4 Program Counters to load the necessary instructions in the 4 parallel lanes."

  • True The four operations travel together in one bundle, fetched with one address; four PCs would mean four instruction streams, as in multithreading.
  • False A VLIW has a single program counter: each fetch brings one bundle, and a bundle holds at most one branch, so there is one next-PC decision per cycle.
Show model answer

False. One PC fetches one bundle per cycle; the bundle carries up to 4 operations in its 4 slots, one per functional unit, and at most one of them is a branch.

A True/False quiz of the 11 February 2025 paper, as the notes transcribe it (p. 582). The statement mixes up the issue width (operations per bundle) with the number of instruction streams. If a motivation line is asked, name the single PC and the one-branch-per-bundle rule.

Lesson 19 · The compiler takes the wheel: what a VLIW is · question 4 of 5 · open in the lesson

True or False: "In VLIW architectures, the compiler can detect parallelism only in basic blocks of the code."

  • True Inside a basic block the dependences are easy to see, but a block holds only about 4 to 7 instructions; a compiler limited to it would leave most VLIW slots empty.
  • False Loop unrolling, software pipelining and trace scheduling find and schedule parallelism across basic blocks and across loop iterations.
Show model answer

False. A basic block (straight-line code with one entry and one exit) holds only about 4 to 7 instructions, and its RAW chains limit the parallelism further. VLIW compilers therefore schedule larger regions: loop unrolling and software pipelining overlap loop iterations, trace scheduling schedules the most likely path through several basic blocks.

A True/False quiz of the 11 February 2025 paper, as the notes transcribe it (p. 581). The notes answer it themselves on p. 273: substantial speedups need "techniques that go beyond individual basic blocks". The three techniques are lesson 21.

Lesson 19 · The compiler takes the wheel: what a VLIW is · question 5 of 5 · open in the lesson

In a VLIW processor, which of the following are handled by the compiler at compile time? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • RAW hazards between operations The compiler places each consumer at least one latency after its producer and fills the gap with independent operations or NOPs.
  • WAR and WAW hazards By the order of the operations (a WAR writer may share the cycle of its reader) or by renaming into other registers.
  • Structural hazards on the functional units One operation per slot, one slot per unit: the bundle format itself prevents two operations on one unit.
  • Data-cache misses Whether an access misses depends on the run; the hardware stalls the processor when it happens.
  • Branch mispredictions The compiler may give a static hint, but detecting a wrong guess and flushing the pipeline is the hardware's job.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 3. Data hazards (RAW, WAR, WAW) and structural hazards are solved statically with known latencies and resources; cache misses and branch mispredictions are dynamic events handled by the hardware (stall, flush).

Draw the line between compile time and run time exactly: everything known in advance (latencies, units, register names) belongs to the compiler; everything that depends on the data or on the path taken belongs to the hardware.

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by a compiler to schedule code statically for a VLIW processor:

  1. Basic block
  2. Dependence graph
  3. Critical path
  4. ASAP scheduling
  5. List-based scheduling
  6. Priority based on the longest path to the sink
  7. Resource reservation table
  8. Local versus global scheduling
Show model answer
  1. A straight-line sequence of instructions with one entry and one exit (only the last instruction may branch). All its instructions execute together, so the compiler can reorder them freely as long as the data dependences hold. It is the unit of local scheduling; its limit is its size — a few instructions between branches.
  2. A DAG with one node per instruction and one edge per dependence: RAW (true), WAR and WAW (name dependences). RAW edges carry the producer's latency. It shows what may run in parallel (unconnected nodes) and what must wait, and it is the input of every scheduler.
  3. The longest latency-weighted path from a source to a sink, LP(i) = max LP(p) + Lat(i). It is the minimum execution time of the block even with unlimited resources: no schedule can beat it. In the exam's VLIW exercise, "critical path for a single iteration" is answered with the length of the scheduled iteration, delay slot included.
  4. Each operation is scheduled in the first cycle in which its operands are available, ignoring resources. It gives the lower bound (= the critical path) and the earliest cycle of every operation; it is not a valid schedule on a real machine, because it can use more units than exist.
  5. ASAP plus resource constraints: in every cycle the ready instructions (operands available) are sorted by priority and the highest ones take the free units. It is greedy and fast, close to optimal on small graphs, and it is what a VLIW compiler — and the exam table — uses.
  6. The priority of an instruction is the longest path from it to the end of the block, computed backwards from the sinks. Instructions with a long chain still ahead go first; instructions with slack fill the holes; ties are broken in program order.
  7. A table of cycles × functional units recording which unit is busy in which cycle. The scheduler checks it before placing an instruction, so no unit is oversubscribed (with fully pipelined units: one new instruction per unit per cycle). Read row by row, it gives the bundles.
  8. Local scheduling works inside one basic block (ASAP, list scheduling; unrolling and software pipelining reshape a loop body); it is simple but limited by the block. Global scheduling moves instructions across branches (trace and superblock scheduling): more ILP, at the cost of compensation code and a more complex compiler.

The 2026 paper asks eight sub-points of this kind with five lines each, on superscalar techniques; this one transposes the form to the static side. The question asks for "main concepts and benefits": give each item a definition plus one benefit or limit. Sub-point 3 is the delicate one: say that the critical path is a resource-independent lower bound, and if you mention the exam table, say that there the iteration length is meant.

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following LOOP code, where Ri are integer registers and Fi are floating-point registers.

LOOP: LD    F2, 0(R1)
      LD    F4, 0(R2)
      FADD  F4, F4, F4
      FADD  F6, F2, F2
      FADD  F8, F0, F0
      SD    F6, 0(R1)
      SD    F8, 0(R2)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 4
      ADDUI R2, R2, 4
      BNE   R1, R3, LOOP

Given a 3-issue VLIW machine with fully pipelined functional units: 1 Memory Unit with 3 cycles latency; 1 FP ALU with 3 cycles latency; 1 Integer ALU with 1 cycle latency to next Int/FP/L/S & 2 cycle latency to next Branch. The branch is completed with 1 cycle delay slot (branch solved in ID stage). No branch prediction. In the Register File, it is possible to read and write at the same address at the same clock cycle. Considering one iteration of the loop, complete the table (columns Memory Unit, Floating Point Unit, Integer Unit; rows C1 to C15; C1 is given: LD F2, 0(R1)) by using the list-based scheduling (do NOT introduce any software pipelining, loop unrolling and modifications to loop indexes) including the BRANCH DELAY SLOT. Please do not write in NOPs. How long is the critical path for a single iteration? How much is the code efficiency for a single iteration?

Show model answer

Dependences: LD F2 → FADD F6 (RAW F2, 3) → SD F6 (RAW F6, 3); LD F4 → FADD F4 (RAW F4, 3); FADD F8 → SD F8 (RAW F8, 3); ADDUI R1 after LD F2 and SD F6 (WAR R1, same cycle allowed); ADDUI R2 after LD F4 and SD F8 (WAR R2); BNE at least 2 cycles after ADDUI R1.

Memory UnitFloating Point UnitInteger Unit
C1LD F2, 0(R1)FADD F8, F0, F0
C2LD F4, 0(R2)
C3
C4SD F8, 0(R2)FADD F6, F2, F2ADDUI R2, R2, 4
C5FADD F4, F4, F4
C6
C7SD F6, 0(R1)ADDUI R1, R1, 4
C8
C9BNE R1, R3, LOOP
C10(delay slot)

Critical path for a single iteration: 10 cycles (LD F2 → FADD F6 → SD F6 / ADDUI R1 → BNE → delay slot). Code efficiency = 10 / (10 × 3) = 0.33. (CPI = 10/10 = 1; FP ops per cycle = 3/10 = 0.3.)

This schedule reaches the floor: with unlimited units the chain is also 10 cycles, so the single units cost nothing. Three traps. FADD F4, F4, F4 produces a value nobody uses in the iteration — schedule it anyway. ADDUI R2 cannot go before SD F8, which still reads R2. The printed text says "ADDDUI" and, in one sentence, "4-issue": the machine and the table are 3-issue, so divide by 3. Try it first in the trainer (preset July 2024).

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Given the following software pipelined loop:

SP_LOOP: SD    F6, 0(R1)
         SD    F8, 0(R2)
         SD    F10, 0(R3)
         FADD  F6, F0, F0
         FADD  F8, F2, F2
         FADD  F10, F4, F4
         LD    F0, 16(R1)
         LD    F2, 16(R2)
         LD    F4, 16(R3)
         ADDUI R1, R1, 8
         ADDUI R2, R2, 8
         ADDUI R3, R3, 8
         BNE   R1, R4, SP_LOOP

Consider a 4-issue VLIW machine with fully pipelined functional units: 2 Memory Units with 3 cycles latency; 1 FP ALU with 3 cycles latency; 1 Integer ALU with 1 cycle latency to next Int/FP & 2 cycle latency to next Branch. The branch is completed with 1 cycle delay slot (branch solved in ID stage). No branch prediction. In the Register File, it is possible to read and write at the same address at the same clock cycle. Considering one iteration of the SP_LOOP, complete the table (columns Memory Unit 1, Memory Unit 2, Floating Point Unit, Integer Unit; C1 is given: SD F6, 0(R1)) by using the list-based scheduling (do NOT introduce any loop transformation) including the BRANCH DELAY SLOT. Please do not write in NOPs. How long is the critical path for a single iteration? What performance did you achieve in CPI? What performance did you achieve in FP ops per cycles? How much is the code efficiency?

Show model answer

Inside the body there is no RAW chain except ADDUI R1 → BNE: each SD stores a value computed in the previous iteration, each FADD reads a value loaded in the previous iteration. Only WAR edges remain (SD F6 before FADD F6, FADD F6 before LD F0, the SD and LD of each pointer before its ADDUI), and the same cycle is allowed for all of them.

Memory Unit 1Memory Unit 2Floating Point UnitInteger Unit
C1SD F6, 0(R1)LD F0, 16(R1)FADD F6, F0, F0ADDUI R1, R1, 8
C2SD F8, 0(R2)LD F2, 16(R2)FADD F8, F2, F2ADDUI R2, R2, 8
C3SD F10, 0(R3)LD F4, 16(R3)FADD F10, F4, F4ADDUI R3, R3, 8
C4BNE R1, R4, SP_LOOP
C5(delay slot)

Critical path for a single iteration: 5 cycles. CPI = 5/13 ≈ 0.38. FP ops per cycle = 3/5 = 0.6. Code efficiency = 13 / (5 × 4) = 0.65.

The bottleneck is the single Integer ALU: three ADDUIs and the BNE, the BNE last, so the BNE cannot be before C4 — and it reaches C4 only if each ADDUI goes as early as possible, which means pairing each SD and LD with its own pointer, cycle by cycle. The longest-path priority of section 3 does not see this. After C1 every remaining instruction has priority 2, program order sends SD F8 and SD F10 together to the two Memory Units in C2, and LD F2 with ADDUI R2 slips to C3, ADDUI R3 to C4, the BNE to C5: 6 cycles (CPI 6/13 ≈ 0.46, efficiency 13/24 ≈ 0.54), a legal list schedule — and so is plain program order, which also ends at 6. Hand in the 5, with one line on the tie-break. Why the loop looks like this (iteration i is stored, i + 1 computed, i + 2 loaded, hence the offset 16) is lesson 21; here you only schedule it.

Let's consider the two following loops LOOP1 and LOOP2, where Ri are integer registers and Fi are floating-point registers:

LOOP1: LD    F2, 0(R1)

LOOP1 is one RAW chain, LD → FADD → SD, plus the branch: 10 cycles, all forced.
