The same five instructions, half the cycles — the before and after of software pipelining that lesson 21 builds. The paper prints "ADDDUI" for ADDUI.

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

The dependence graph of a basic block has a critical path of 7 cycles. Which statement is true for any list-based schedule of the block on a machine with one functional unit of each type? (SINGLE ANSWER)

  • Answer 1: it is always exactly 7 cycles long Only when the units never delay an instruction of the critical path; with the MUL latency at 1, the graph a–g of the notes needs 6 cycles on one ALU against a critical path of 5.
  • Answer 2: it is never shorter than 7 cycles The critical path is the length with unlimited resources: fewer resources can only add waiting.
  • Answer 3: it is never longer than 7 cycles Busy units can delay instructions of the critical path: the January 2026 loop has a floor of 13 and needs 14 on one Memory Unit.
  • Answer 4: its length does not depend on the priority function List scheduling is greedy: the order in which it serves the ready set can change the length. Two loads are ready in C1, one at the head of the critical chain, one whose value nobody waits for: serve the second first and the whole chain slips by a cycle.
Show model answer

Answer 2. The critical path is a resource-independent lower bound; resources and priorities can only make the schedule equal to it or longer.

A single-answer quiz in the style of the paper's block. The trap is Answer 1, which is what students conclude after seeing a few schedules that reach the floor.

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

The January 2026 loop needs 14 cycles per iteration on the 3-issue VLIW of the paper (1 Memory Unit, 1 FP ALU, 1 Integer ALU; latencies 3, 3, 1 and 2 to the branch). Which of the following single changes of the machine would shorten the schedule? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • Answer 1: a second Memory Unit LD F4, VC(R6) can then share C7 with SD F4, VA(R7): 13 cycles, the floor.
  • Answer 2: a second FP ALU The FP unit is never the bottleneck: FADD F8 already sits in C1 and FADD F4 waits for its operand, not for the unit. Still 14.
  • Answer 3: a second Integer ALU The two ADDUIs never wait for the unit, and the BNE waits for R7. Still 14.
  • Answer 4: a Memory Unit with 2 cycles latency Two load latencies lie on the critical chain (LD F2 → FADD, LD F4 → SD): 12 cycles.
  • Answer 5: an FP ALU with 2 cycles latency FADD F4 → SD F4 is on the chain: 13 cycles.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 4 and 5: they give 13, 12 and 13 cycles. Answers 2 and 3 leave 14: the units they add are never waited for.

The question tests whether you know where the critical path runs and which constraints are latencies (they move the floor) and which are resources (they can only close the gap to the floor). The trainer above lets you verify each case.

Lesson 20 · Graphs, critical paths and the exam table: list scheduling · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or False: the longest path of the dependence graph of a basic block, computed with the latencies of the machine, becomes shorter when a second memory unit is added to the machine.

  • True The longest path is computed with unlimited resources: the number of units does not appear in it. A second unit can shorten the schedule, never the critical path.
  • False Correct: only latencies and dependences enter the longest path. Units change how close a real schedule gets to it.
Show model answer

False. LP(i) = max LP(p) + Lat(i) contains latencies and edges only; the number of functional units changes the list schedule, not the critical path.

A true/false quiz, one point. Keep apart the two meanings of "critical path": the graph's longest path (machine-independent) and, in the exam's VLIW exercise, the length of your scheduled iteration.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following static scheduling techniques used by compilers for VLIW processors, and state the main drawback of each: (1) Loop unrolling; (2) Software pipelining; (3) Trace scheduling; (4) Superblock scheduling; (5) Predication (if-conversion).

Show model answer

(1) Loop unrolling replicates the loop body u times in one block, adjusts the displacements of the copies (0, 8, 16, …) and keeps a single index update and a single branch. The compiler renames the registers of each copy, removing the WAR and WAW dependences between copies, so the scheduler can interleave u independent chains and hide latencies. Benefits: fewer branches and index updates per element, fewer stalls, more operations per bundle (x[i] = x[i] + s: 10 cycles per element → 3.5 on a single-issue pipeline with a delay slot). Costs: code size, (u − 1) times the registers a copy writes, a remainder loop when the trip count is not a multiple of u.

(2) Software pipelining reorganises the loop so that each execution of the body contains stages of different iterations — store of i, compute of i + 1, load of i + 2 — in reverse order. The true dependences of an iteration no longer lie inside one body; only anti-dependences and the loop control do. A prologue fills and an epilogue drains the pipeline. Benefits: one iteration completes per execution of the body, with the body's original size and the same registers (June 2026: LOOP1 10 cycles, LOOP2 5 cycles, CPI speed-up 2). Costs: prologue and epilogue code, extra registers when a value must live longer than one pass, at least as many iterations as stages, compiler complexity.

(3) Trace scheduling is global scheduling across basic blocks: profiling selects the most likely path (the trace, acyclic), which is list-scheduled as one block; instructions move across branches (above them speculatively) and across joins. Compensation code — copies of moved instructions on the off-trace exit and entry paths — keeps the other paths correct. Benefits: fills wide instructions beyond the 4 to 7 instructions of a basic block and starts long-latency loads early. Costs: code growth from compensation code, slower off-trace paths, dependence on predictable branches, a complex compiler; stores and instructions that may raise exceptions cannot be speculated freely.

(4) Superblock scheduling restricts the region to a single entry and multiple exits, obtained by tail duplication: the part of the trace below a side entrance is copied for the entering path. Moves then need compensation only at the exits, outside the superblock, so speculation is simpler and safer. Cost: the duplicated code.

(5) Predication removes a branch instead of scheduling around it: a compare sets a predicate register and its complement, and the instructions of each arm are guarded by one of them — always issued, but writing their result only when their predicate is true. The control dependence becomes a data dependence on the predicate, so both arms form one larger basic block that the scheduler can fill, with nothing to mispredict and no compensation code. Cost: both arms take issue slots on every pass, so it pays only for short, balanced, hard-to-predict branches; it needs predicate registers in the ISA (IA-64).

The June 2026 paper asked eight such sub-points for 8 points, about five lines each. A complete sub-point has three things: the mechanism in one or two sentences, the benefit with a number from an exercise, the cost. The contrasts make the answer crisp — unrolling replicates while software pipelining interleaves; trace scheduling repairs at entries and exits, a superblock only at exits, predication nowhere — and so does one sentence saying that the compiler applies all five, the VLIW doing no dependence checking at run time.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Given the following software pipelined loop:

SP_LOOP: SD    F6, 0(R1)
         SD    F8, 0(R2)
         SD    F10, 0(R3)
         FADD  F6, F0, F0
         FADD  F8, F2, F2
         FADD  F10, F4, F4
         LD    F0, 16(R1)
         LD    F2, 16(R2)
         LD    F4, 16(R3)
         ADDUI R1, R1, 8
         ADDUI R2, R2, 8
         ADDUI R3, R3, 8
         BNE   R1, R4, SP_LOOP

Consider a 4-issue VLIW machine with fully pipelined functional units: 2 Memory Units with 3 cycles latency; 1 FP ALU with 3 cycles latency; 1 Integer ALU with 1 cycle latency to next Int/FP and 2 cycle latency to next Branch. The branch is completed with 1 cycle delay slot (branch solved in ID stage). No branch prediction. In the Register File, it is possible to read and write at the same address at the same clock cycle. Considering one iteration of the SP_LOOP, complete the table (Memory Unit 1, Memory Unit 2, Floating Point Unit, Integer Unit; C1 given: SD F6, 0(R1)) by using the list-based scheduling (do NOT introduce any loop transformation), including the branch delay slot. Then: how long is the critical path for a single iteration? What performance did you achieve in CPI and in FP ops per cycle? How much is the code efficiency?

Show model answer

There is no true dependence among the SDs, FADDs and LDs: each SD reads the value its FADD rewrites (WAR), each FADD reads the value its LD rewrites (WAR), and every ADDUI may share the cycle of the last instruction that reads its register. The integer unit has four instructions to issue (three ADDUI and BNE), so each stream's SD and LD are placed together to free its ADDUI as early as possible.

Memory Unit 1Memory Unit 2Floating Point UnitInteger Unit
C1SD F6, 0(R1)LD F0, 16(R1)FADD F6, F0, F0ADDUI R1, R1, 8
C2SD F8, 0(R2)LD F2, 16(R2)FADD F8, F2, F2ADDUI R2, R2, 8
C3SD F10, 0(R3)LD F4, 16(R3)FADD F10, F4, F4ADDUI R3, R3, 8
C4BNE R1, R4, SP_LOOP
C5(delay slot)

Critical path = 5 cycles. CPI = 5/13 ≈ 0.38. FP ops per cycle = 3/5 = 0.6. Code efficiency = 13/(5 × 4) = 0.65.

Five cycles is optimal: six memory operations need three cycles on two units, the ADDUI of the stream loaded last cannot precede C3, and the single integer unit needs a fourth cycle for BNE. A pure program-order tie-break (SD F6 and SD F8 together in C1) is also a list schedule, but it delays LD F2 and LD F4, pushes ADDUI R3 to C4 and BNE to C5: 6 cycles, CPI 0.46, 0.5 FP ops per cycle, efficiency 0.54. Write the reasoning about the integer unit next to the table: it is what justifies pairing each SD and LD in one cycle. Lesson 20 drills the same table as a list-scheduling exercise.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider the following software pipelined loop SP_LOOP and its start-up code; R1 points to the first element of an array of n doubles.

START_UP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
          FADD  F4, F0, F2
          LD    F0, 8(R1)
SP_LOOP:  SD    F4, 0(R1)
          FADD  F4, F0, F2
          LD    F0, 16(R1)
          ADDUI R1, R1, 8
          BNE   R1, R5, SP_LOOP

(a) Reconstruct the original (non-pipelined) loop. (b) For each of the first three instructions of SP_LOOP, state which iteration of the original loop it belongs to. (c) Write the finish-up code. (d) For n = 100, how many times is the body of SP_LOOP executed, and which value must R5 hold?

Show model answer

(a) With R3 = initial R1 + 8n:

LOOP: LD    F0, 0(R1)
      FADD  F4, F0, F2
      SD    F4, 0(R1)
      ADDUI R1, R1, 8
      BNE   R1, R3, LOOP

(b) SD F4, 0(R1): iteration i, whose sum was computed in the previous pass. FADD F4, F0, F2: iteration i + 1, whose operand was loaded in the previous pass. LD F0, 16(R1): iteration i + 2 — two strides ahead, 16 = 2 × 8.

(c) FINISH_UP: SD F4, 0(R1) (element n − 2); FADD F4, F0, F2 (element n − 1); SD F4, 8(R1) (element n − 1).

(d) The body handles i = 0 … n − 3: it runs n − 2 = 98 times. R5 = initial R1 + 8 × 98 = R1 + 784, the address of element 98.

The classic mistake is to read SD as "store the result of this pass" and put it first in the reconstructed loop. Follow one value through the passes instead. In (c), remember that the last ADDUI of the body has already moved R1.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the two following loops, where LOOP2 is the software-pipelined form of LOOP1 (its load fetches the element two iterations ahead):

LOOP1: LD    F2, 0(R1)
       FADD  F6, F2, F2
       SD    F6, 0(R1)
       ADDUI R1, R1, 4
       BNE   R1, R3, LOOP1
LOOP2: SD    F6, 0(R2)
       FADD  F6, F2, F2
       LD    F2, 8(R2)
       ADDUI R2, R2, 4
       BNE   R2, R3, LOOP2

Both are scheduled by list-based scheduling, including the branch delay slot, on a 4-issue VLIW machine with fully pipelined units: 2 Memory Units with 3 cycles latency, 1 FP ALU with 3 cycles latency, 1 Integer ALU with 1 cycle latency to next Int/FP and 2 cycle latency to next Branch; branch solved in ID with 1 delay slot; read and write at the same register-file address in the same clock cycle. How much is the speed up of LOOP2 over LOOP1 in terms of CPI?

Show model answer

LOOP1 does not change: its chain LD (C1) → FADD (C4) → SD with ADDUI (C7) → BNE (C9) → delay slot (C10) uses one memory unit at a time. 10 cycles, CPI = 10/5 = 2.

LOOP2: SD F6, 0(R2) and LD F2, 8(R2) both fit in C1 (two memory units, two different addresses), with FADD F6 (WAR on F6 and F2) and ADDUI R2 (WAR on R2) in the same cycle; BNE in C3, delay slot in C4. 4 cycles, CPI = 4/5 = 0.8.

\[ \text{speed-up} = \frac{2}{0.8} = 2.5 \]

The second memory unit helps only the software-pipelined loop: LOOP1 is bound by its true-dependence chain, LOOP2 by resources and the branch. The paper itself prints LOOP2's load as LD F2, 0(R2): then the store and the load of one pass touch the same address, the load must not move up beside the store that precedes it, it stays in C2, and LOOP2 takes 5 cycles — a speed-up of 2. With one memory unit, as in the paper, the question never arises.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following code: for (i=0; i<260; i++) Y[i] = X[i] + Y[i]; Which code transformation can be applied to be executed by the VMIPS processor with a Vector Register File composed of 16 Vector registers of 32 elements and 64 bits/element? Motivate your answer.

  • Trace scheduling A VLIW compiler technique for scheduling across branches; this loop has no branch inside and the problem is the vector length.
  • Software pipelining It overlaps iterations of a scalar loop on a statically scheduled machine; it does not make 260 elements fit 32-element registers.
  • Strip mining 260 > 32, so the loop is cut into strips of at most 32 elements, with the vector length register set for each strip.
  • Apply a mask register A mask handles conditional statements inside a vector loop; there is no condition here.
Show model answer

Strip mining. The loop has 260 elements and a vector register holds 32: 260 = 8 × 32 + 4, so a first strip of 4 elements and then eight strips of 32, setting VLR for each strip.

The distractors are the techniques of this lesson; strip mining itself is lesson 31. The motivation point is the arithmetic 260 = 8 × 32 + 4.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: in the steady state, a software-pipelined loop completes one iteration every clock cycle.

  • True That holds only if the whole body, branch included, fits one bundle; LOOP2 of June 2026 completes one iteration every 5 cycles.
  • False One iteration completes per execution of the body, every II cycles, where II is the length of the body's schedule.
Show model answer

False. Each execution of the body finishes one iteration, so the throughput is one iteration per initiation interval — 5 cycles for LOOP2 on the 3-issue VLIW of June 2026, 8 cycles for the midterm's SP_LOOP.

The notes repeat the wrong version five times (pp. 289–291); the CPI you compute in the VLIW exercise contradicts it.

Lesson 21 · Same loop, three shapes: unrolling, software pipelining and betting on the hot path · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about trace and superblock scheduling are true? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • An instruction moved from below a branch to above it needs no compensation code if its result is dead on the other path and it cannot store or raise an exception. Speculation is free exactly in this case: the off-trace path simply never uses the value.
  • An instruction moved above a side entrance into the trace needs a copy on the entering path. Otherwise the path that enters from the side would skip it.
  • A superblock may have several entries but only one exit. The other way round: one entry, any number of exits.
  • Tail duplication removes side entrances at the price of code size. The tail below the entrance is copied for the entering path.
  • A trace may contain a loop's back edge. Traces are acyclic; a back edge ends a trace, which is why loops are unrolled first.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 4.

The two directions of compensation are what the question checks: copies go on the exit path for moves below a branch and on the entering path for moves above a join.

To the filters ↑

Module 6 · Memory hierarchy

47 questions · Notes, chapter 6 and 4.4 · caches, the AMAT formulas of the formula sheet, nineteen optimisations, virtual memory

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following concepts of the memory hierarchy: 1. Memory hierarchy; 2. Temporal locality; 3. Spatial locality; 4. Cache block (cache line); 5. Hit time and miss penalty; 6. Average Memory Access Time (AMAT); 7. Separate instruction and data caches (Harvard architecture) versus a unified cache; 8. SRAM and DRAM technologies.

Show model answer

1. A layered memory system: small, fast, expensive levels close to the processor (registers; L1, L2, L3 caches in SRAM) and large, slow, cheap levels further away (main memory in DRAM, disk). Each upper level holds a subset of the level below. Benefit: the processor sees one large memory with an average access time close to that of the fastest level, at a cost per bit close to that of the cheapest (the illusion of a large, fast memory). It works only because programs have locality. Registers are managed by the compiler, caches by hardware, main memory and disk by the operating system.

2. A location accessed now is likely to be accessed again soon (an accumulator in a loop, the instructions of a loop body). Exploited by keeping recently used blocks in the cache and, when space is needed, replacing the least recently used one (LRU), so that the repeated accesses hit. Benefit: after the first access the reused word costs only the hit time; the cache needs enough capacity to keep the block until the reuse comes.

3. After a location, nearby locations are likely to be accessed soon (array elements a[i], a[i+1]; sequential instructions). Exploited by transferring whole multi-word blocks: one miss brings the neighbours, whose accesses then hit (8 doubles in a 64-byte block: 1 miss, 7 hits). Benefit: one miss penalty is shared by several accesses; if the neighbours are never used, a larger block only makes each miss longer.

4. The smallest unit transferred between memory and cache, typically 32 to 128 bytes; number of blocks = cache size / block size (64 KB / 16 B = 4096). Benefit: one transfer serves several accesses (spatial locality). Cost: a larger block makes each miss longer.

5. Hit time: time to access the cache, determine whether it is a hit and return the data (1 to 3 cycles for L1). Miss penalty: extra time to fetch the block from the lower level, write it into the cache and resume the CPU (tens to hundreds of cycles). On a miss the CPU stalls, the block is fetched and written, and the access is retried; miss time = hit time + miss penalty.

6. AMAT = Hit Rate × Hit Time + Miss Rate × (Hit Time + Miss Penalty) = Hit Time + Miss Rate × Miss Penalty, because Hit Rate + Miss Rate = 1: every access pays the hit time, the misses add the penalty. The miss rate is a fraction (5% = 0.05): 1 + 0.05 × 100 = 6 cycles. Benefit: one number that combines the three parameters and shows the three ways to improve a cache: reduce the hit time, the miss rate or the miss penalty.

7. Split L1: separate I-cache and D-cache, so the pipeline can fetch an instruction and access data in the same cycle (no structural hazard). AMAT = %Instr × (HT + MRI × MP) + %Data × (HT + MRD × MP), with %Instr = 1/(1 + d) and %Data = d/(1 + d), d = loads/stores per instruction. Unified: one cache shared flexibly between code and data, usually a lower miss rate for the same total size, but with one port every data access stalls one cycle. Example: unified 1.99% against split 2.10% miss rate, yet AMAT 2.245 against 2.05 cycles: the split cache is better there because of the port, and the comparison must be made on AMAT.

8. SRAM: six transistors per bit, fast but large and expensive per bit, used for the caches. DRAM: one transistor and a capacitor per bit, dense and cheap but slower, and it must be refreshed; used for main memory (SDRAM and DDR are DRAM). No single technology is both fast and big, which is why the hierarchy combines them.

The 2026 paper leaves five lines per sub-point: room for a definition, the mechanism, the benefit, the cost and a number. In sub-point 6 write the derivation, not only the final formula; in sub-point 7 give the reason the unified cache loses, the structural hazard on its single port. Writing SDRAM for the caches in sub-point 8 repeats the notes' slip of p. 297.

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

MEMORY HIERARCHY. Let's consider a processor where 25% of the instructions are loads/stores, with two alternative L1 organisations of the same total size: (a) separate instruction and data caches (Harvard architecture) with Miss RateI$ = 1% and Miss RateD$ = 6%; (b) a unified cache with Aggregate Miss Rate = 1.8% and only one port, so a data access has 1 more stall cycle. Hit Time = 1 clock cycle and Miss Penalty = 50 clock cycles in both cases.

1. How much are the percentages of instruction accesses and of data accesses? 2. How much is the miss rate of the Harvard organisation over all accesses? 3. How much is the AMAT of each organisation? 4. Which organisation is better, and why?

Show model answer

1. Per instruction: 1 fetch + 0.25 data accesses = 1.25 accesses. %Instr = 1 / 1.25 = 0.80 (80%); %Data = 0.25 / 1.25 = 0.20 (20%).

2. MRHarvard = 0.8 × 0.01 + 0.2 × 0.06 = 0.008 + 0.012 = 0.020 (2.0%), higher than the unified 1.8%.

3. AMATHarvard = 0.8 × (1 + 0.01 × 50) + 0.2 × (1 + 0.06 × 50) = 0.8 × 1.5 + 0.2 × 4 = 1.2 + 0.8 = 2.0 cycles. AMATunified = 0.8 × (1 + 0.018 × 50) + 0.2 × (1 + 1 + 0.018 × 50) = 0.8 × 1.9 + 0.2 × 2.9 = 1.52 + 0.58 = 2.1 cycles.

4. The Harvard organisation is better, 2.0 against 2.1 cycles, although its miss rate is higher (2.0% against 1.8%). The unified cache saves (0.020 − 0.018) × 50 = 0.1 cycles per access on misses but loses 0.2 × 1 = 0.2 cycles per access in structural-hazard stalls on its single port. The comparison is decided by AMAT, not by the miss rate.

The same structure as the notes' Example 6 with rounder numbers. The steps to get right: the shares derived per instruction (80% and 20%, not 75% and 25%), every percentage converted to a fraction, and the "why" of point 4: the structural hazard and its cost per access.

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

A cache has a Hit Time of 1 clock cycle and a Miss Penalty of 100 clock cycles. Which Miss Rate gives an AMAT of 3 clock cycles? Write it as a decimal fraction, not as a percentage.

Show model answer

AMAT = Hit Time + Miss Rate × Miss Penalty ⇒ 3 = 1 + Miss Rate × 100 ⇒ Miss Rate = 2 / 100 = 0.02 (2%).

The answer "2" is the percentage written as a bare number: the "unit is %" trap of p. 321 in reverse. Check it: 1 + 0.02 × 100 = 3.

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Single answer.) Let's consider a cache with Hit Time = 1 clock cycle, Miss Rate = 5% and Miss Penalty = 100 clock cycles. How much is the AMAT?

  • Answer 1: 5.95 clock cycles 0.95 × 1 + 0.05 × 100 forgets the hit time that the misses pay too.
  • Answer 2: 6 clock cycles 1 + 0.05 × 100: every access pays the hit time, the misses add the penalty on average.
  • Answer 3: 101 clock cycles That is the cost of one miss, hit time plus miss penalty, not the average.
  • Answer 4: 501 clock cycles The miss rate was entered as 5 instead of 0.05.
Show model answer

Answer 2: AMAT = 1 + 0.05 × 100 = 6 clock cycles.

The notes' Example 3. Each distractor is one classic slip: the dropped hit time, the cost of one miss, the percentage used as a number.

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

(Multiple answers.) Which of the following statements about locality and caches are correct?

  • Answer 1: Temporal locality is exploited by keeping recently accessed blocks in the cache and replacing the least recently used one The reused word is still there when it is needed again; LRU protects the blocks in use.
  • Answer 2: Spatial locality is exploited by transferring multi-word blocks between memory and cache One miss brings the neighbours, whose accesses then hit.
  • Answer 3: A larger block size helps a program that reads each element of a large array once, in random order The neighbours are read only much later, long after their block has been evicted: each block serves one useful word, and a larger block only makes each miss longer.
  • Answer 4: The instructions of a loop body show both temporal and spatial locality They are fetched again in every iteration (temporal) and lie at consecutive addresses (spatial).
  • Answer 5: Without any locality, a cache still reduces the average memory access time Every access misses: AMAT = hit time + miss penalty, more than accessing memory directly.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 4.

The locality probe shows four of the five: answers 1 and 2 are its first program (capacity, then block size), answers 3 and 5 its third. Answer 4 is the instruction stream of the six-instruction loop in section 2: fetched again in every iteration, from consecutive addresses.

Lesson 22 · The memory wall and the illusion of a fast, big memory · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

(True/False.) For the same total size, a unified L1 cache always has a higher AMAT than separate instruction and data caches.

  • True In the notes' Example 6 the split cache wins only because the unified one has a single port; without that stall the unified cache, with its lower miss rate, has AMAT 1.995 against 2.049.
  • False The result depends on the miss rates, the share of data accesses, the miss penalty and the number of ports: compute both AMATs.
Show model answer

False. A unified cache usually has the lower miss rate for the same size; it loses only when the structural-hazard stalls on its single port cost more than its miss-rate advantage saves (Example 6: 0.25 cycles against about 0.05 per access). With two ports, or a large enough miss penalty (above about 233 cycles with the numbers of Example 6), it wins.

An "always" in a true/false question is a hint to look for the counterexample. Here it is one line of arithmetic.

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used in cache memories: (a) direct-mapped block placement; (b) fully associative block placement; (c) set-associative block placement; (d) block identification by tag comparison; (e) LRU replacement; (f) write-back with dirty bit; (g) write-through with write buffer; (h) write allocate and no write allocate. (Five lines each.)

Show model answer

(a) Each memory block can be placed in exactly one cache line, line = block address mod #blocks: the index bits select the line and a single tag comparison decides hit or miss. Benefit: the simplest and fastest organisation — one comparator, no replacement decision, and the data can be read while the tag is being checked. Cost: conflict misses — two blocks with the same index evict each other at every access (blocks 12 and 20 in an 8-line cache), even while other lines are empty.

(b) A block may be placed in any line: there is no index, the whole block address is the tag, and the address tag is compared with the tags of all lines in parallel. Benefit: no conflict misses, and full freedom for the replacement policy. Cost: one comparator per line plus the logic that selects the matching line — expensive in area and energy, and slower, so it is used only for small caches and buffers.

(c) The cache is divided into sets of n lines (ways); a memory block maps to exactly one set, set = block address mod #sets, and may occupy any of its n ways. Benefit: far fewer conflict misses than direct-mapped (two blocks with the same index can coexist) at a much lower cost than fully associative, since only the n tags of one set are compared. Cost: n comparators and an n-way multiplexer, so a slightly longer hit time and more energy.

(d) The address is split into tag | index | offset: offset = log2(block size), index = log2(#sets), tag = the remaining high-order bits. The index selects the set, the n stored tags of that set are compared with the address tag in parallel, and a way hits if its tag is equal and its valid bit is 1; the offset then selects the word. Benefit: one parallel lookup; only the tag is stored, since index and offset are implied by the position; the valid bit prevents false hits at cold start.

(e) On a miss into a full set, the block whose last use (read or write, hit or fill) is oldest is evicted; the order of use is updated on every access. Benefit: it exploits temporal locality — recently used blocks are likely to be used again — so it usually misses less than FIFO or random. Cost: state per set grows as log2(n!) bits and must be updated on every hit, so highly associative caches use pseudo-LRU or random.

(f) A write updates only the cache line and sets its dirty bit; main memory is updated with the whole block only when a dirty block is evicted. Benefit: many writes to the same block cost one memory write, less memory traffic and writes at cache speed. Cost: a dirty bit per line, memory stale until eviction, and a miss that evicts a dirty block must write it back as well.

(g) Every write updates the cache (on a hit) and main memory; a small FIFO write buffer holds the pending writes so the processor continues while they drain to memory, stalling only if the buffer is full. Benefit: memory is always up to date, simple control, no write-back on eviction. Cost: one memory transaction per store, so high traffic; without the buffer every store would stall for a memory write.

(h) On a write miss, write allocate fetches the block into the cache and then writes it (as a read miss would); no write allocate writes the word directly to memory and leaves the cache unchanged. Write allocate is paired with write-back: once allocated, further writes to the block hit and generate no traffic. No write allocate is paired with write-through: every write goes to memory anyway, so fetching the block would only add traffic; it also keeps data that is written once and not read soon from evicting useful blocks.

Eight sub-points for 8 points, five lines each: the form of the June 2026 open question. Every answer needs three things: the mechanism in one sentence (with the formula where there is one: mod #blocks, mod #sets, log2), what it buys, what it costs. Do not write "LRU evicts the oldest block" in (e): that describes FIFO. In (h) give the pairing and its reason, not the pairing alone.

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following memory hierarchy addressed at word-level (32-bit): main memory size = 4 Giga word; 4-way set-associative cache with cache size = 1 Mega word and block size = 256 word. Please indicate the structure of the 32-bit memory address: (SINGLE ANSWER)

  • Answer 1: |12-bit tag | 12-bit index | 8-bit offset| 12 index bits would mean 4096 sets: that is the same cache direct-mapped, one line per set.
  • Answer 2: |24-bit tag | 8-bit offset| No index at all means a single set: fully associative, not 4-way.
  • Answer 3: |14-bit tag | 10-bit index | 8-bit offset| Offset log2 256 = 8; 2^20 / 2^8 = 4096 lines; 4096 / 4 = 1024 sets → 10 index bits; 32 − 10 − 8 = 14.
  • Answer 4: |13-bit tag | 11-bit index | 8-bit offset| 11 index bits = 2048 sets of 2 ways: the 2-way version of the cache.
Show model answer

Answer 3. Offset = log2(256) = 8 bits; number of blocks = 2^20 / 2^8 = 2^12; number of sets = 2^12 / 4 = 2^10, so index = 10 bits; tag = 32 − 10 − 8 = 14 bits.

Quiz 2 of the 15 July 2024 paper, in its own words. The three distractors are the same cache at 1, 2 and 4096 ways — so if you only remember that "more ways move bits from the index to the tag", you can still pick the right one.

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a 32-block main memory with a 4-way set associative 8-block cache based on a write allocate with write-back protocol with LRU. The addresses are expressed as: Memory Addresses [0, 1, 2, … 31]10; Cache Addresses [a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h], and Cache Tags are expressed as binary numbers. How many sets? (Answer 1: 2; Answer 2: 4; Answer 3: 8; Answer 4: 16.) At cold start the cache is empty, then there is the following sequence of memory accesses: Write 16, Write 14, Write 07, Read 14, Write 16, Read 12, Write 18, Read 06, Write 04, Write 30. Please complete the table (Hit/Miss Type, Cache Tag, Set, Cache Address, Dirty bit, Write in memory). How much is the miss rate?

Show model answer

Sets = 8 / 4 = 2 (Answer 1). The 5-bit block address = 4-bit tag | 1-bit set: set = address mod 2, tag = address div 2. Set 0 = lines a–d, set 1 = lines e–h; a free line is taken in alphabetical order, otherwise the LRU line of the set. "Yes (14)" means: the evicted block 14 is dirty and is written back to memory. The last column is scratch work, not a column of the paper.

1Write[16]10Cold-start Miss[1000]2[0]10a1No16
2Write[14]10Cold-start Miss[0111]2[0]10b1No16 14
3Write[07]10Cold-start Miss[0011]2[1]10e1No7
4Read[14]10Hit[0111]2[0]10b1No16 14

The paper's own exercise (15 July 2024, Exercise 3); this table is a computed model answer, as the official solution is not in the course material. Rows 8–10 are where it is decided: the LRU victim (14, not 16 — FIFO would pick 16), the dirty bit 0 after the read fill of row 8, and "Write in memory" Yes only for the dirty victims. Every miss here is a first reference to its block, hence Cold-start by the 3C definition of the notes (p. 319). Rows 8–10 also evict from a full set while set 1 still has free lines, which the notes' wording for a conflict miss ("even if the cache is not full") could seem to fit; no official solution settles the label, so write the reason next to the type ("replaces LRU block 14, dirty") and either reading is covered.

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Same 32-block main memory and the same sequence of memory accesses (Write 16, Write 14, Write 07, Read 14, Write 16, Read 12, Write 18, Read 06, Write 04, Write 30), but now a 2-way set associative 8-block cache, again write allocate with write-back and LRU; Cache Addresses [a … h], tags in binary. How many sets? Complete the table and compute the miss rate. Which blocks are written back to memory?

Show model answer

Sets = 8 / 2 = 4: set = address mod 4 (the two last bits), tag = address div 4 (3 bits). Set 0 = a, b; set 1 = c, d; set 2 = e, f; set 3 = g, h.

Type of memory accessMemory AddressHit/ Miss TypeCache TagSetCache AddressDirty bitWrite in memoryscratch: the set after it, LRU first
1Write[16]10Cold-start Miss[100]2[0]10a1No16
2Write[14]10Cold-start Miss[011]2[2]10e1No14
3Write[07]10Cold-start Miss[001]2[3]10g1No7
4Read[14]10Hit[011]2[2]10e1No14

Miss rate = 8 / 10 = 80 %. Written back: blocks 14, 16 and 18.

A variant built on the paper's numbers to check that you do not copy the 4-way table: with four sets of two ways, sets 0 and 2 fill up sooner, and one more dirty block (18) is written back. Set 1 is never used, because no address of the sequence is 1 mod 4 — which is legal; do not "move" a block into a free line of another set. Recompute both columns from the address every time: the tag now has 3 digits.

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

A byte-addressed processor with 32-bit addresses has a 64 KB 4-way set-associative data cache with 64-byte blocks. How many bits does the tag have?

Show model answer

Offset = log2(64) = 6 bits; blocks = 65 536 / 64 = 1024; sets = 1024 / 4 = 256, so index = 8 bits; tag = 32 − 8 − 6 = 18 bits. Check: 32 − log2(64 KB / 4) = 32 − 14 = 18.

The two usual slips: forgetting to divide the lines by the ways (index 10, tag 16) and taking the block size in words when the machine is byte-addressed. A common follow-up asks for the storage the cache really needs: every line keeps V, D and its tag besides the data, 1024 × (1 + 1 + 18 + 512) = 544 768 bits; the 20 480 bits of V, D and tags add about 4 % to the 64 KB of data (the LRU state of the sets comes on top).

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which statements about a write-back cache are correct? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • Answer 1: Every store updates main memory immediately. That is write-through; write-back updates only the cache line.
  • Answer 2: A block is written to main memory only when it is evicted while dirty. The dirty bit records that the cached copy is newer; the eviction writes it back.
  • Answer 3: It generates less memory traffic than write-through when the same block is written many times. Many stores to one block become a single block write at eviction.
  • Answer 4: Main memory always holds the most recent value. Memory is stale while a dirty copy sits in the cache.
  • Answer 5: It is usually combined with write allocate. Allocation brings the block in, so later writes to it hit in the cache.
Show model answer

Answers 2, 3 and 5. Answers 1 and 4 describe write-through.

A multiple-answer quiz in the June 2026 format. Answer 4 is the common trap: in a write-back cache it is the cache, not memory, that holds the newest copy of a dirty block.

Lesson 23 · Anatomy of a cache: the four questions and the exam cache table · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

True or false: with LRU replacement, the block evicted from a full set is the one that was brought into the set first. (True/False)

  • True "Brought in first" is FIFO. LRU looks at the last use, which a later hit can renew.
  • False LRU evicts the block whose most recent access is the oldest; in July 2024, access 8, that is block 14, while block 16 arrived first.
Show model answer

False. LRU evicts the least recently used block; the block that arrived first is the FIFO victim. Before access 8 of the July 2024 sequence, set 0 holds 16 (in at 1, last used 5) and 14 (in at 2, last used 4): FIFO evicts 16, LRU evicts 14.

True/False is one of the quiz forms of the papers (June 2025, Quiz 3), and a near-definition like this one is its natural target. Back the answer with a two-block counter-example; it takes one line.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

PERFORMANCE EVALUATION (part 2). Evaluate the impact of the memory hierarchy on another CPU3 for which all instructions require 8 clock cycles in the following cases:

1. How much is the CPI3 when considering an ideal cache (100% hit)? CPI3_ideal_cache = …

2. How much is the CPI3 when considering a real cache with a miss rate of 50% and assuming 3 memory accesses on average for each instruction and miss penalty of 3 clock cycles? CPI3_real_cache = … How much is the impact on performance of the real cache with respect to the ideal cache (100% hit)?

3. How much is the CPI3 in the case of no cache (100% miss)? CPI3_no_cache = … How much is the impact on performance with respect to the real cache (50% hit)?

Show model answer

CPI = CPI_exec + memory accesses per instruction × miss rate × miss penalty, with CPI_exec = 8 clock cycles.

1. CPI3_ideal_cache = 8 + 3 × 0 × 3 = 8.

2. Stall cycles per instruction = 3 × 0.5 × 3 = 4.5; CPI3_real_cache = 8 + 4.5 = 12.5. Same IC and same clock, so the ratio of the CPU times is the ratio of the CPIs: 12.5 / 8 = 1.5625. With the real cache CPU3 takes 56.25% more time; the ideal cache would be 1.56 times (56.25%) faster.

3. Stall cycles per instruction = 3 × 1 × 3 = 9; CPI3_no_cache = 8 + 9 = 17. 17 / 12.5 = 1.36: without a cache CPU3 takes 36% more time than with the real cache (the real cache is 1.36 times faster); against the ideal cache, 17 / 8 = 2.125.

Exercise 3 of the 10 June 2025 paper, part 2, in its own words; part 1 is in lesson 3. Write the formula once, compute the stall term on its own line (3 × 0.5 × 3 = 4.5) and give the impact as a ratio of times, which here is the CPI ratio because IC and the clock do not change.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

MEMORY HIERARCHY. Let's consider a computer with an L1 and L2 cache memory hierarchy. In 1000 memory references there are 40 misses in L1 and 20 misses in L2. Hit Time L1 = 1 clock cycle; Hit Time L2 = 10 clock cycles; Miss Penalty L2 = 200 clock cycles; 1.5 memory accesses on average for each instruction; CPI = 1.0 with an ideal cache.

1. How much are the L1 miss rate, the L2 local miss rate and the L2 global miss rate? 2. How much is the AMAT? 3. How much are the memory stall cycles per instruction and the CPI? 4. How much is the CPI without the L2 cache (L1 misses served by main memory in 200 clock cycles), and how much is the speed-up due to the L2 cache?

Show model answer

1. Miss Rate L1 = 40 / 1000 = 0.04 (4%); local Miss Rate L2 = 20 / 40 = 0.5 (50%); global Miss Rate L2 = 0.04 × 0.5 = 20 / 1000 = 0.02 (2%).

2. AMAT = 1 + 0.04 × (10 + 0.5 × 200) = 1 + 0.04 × 110 = 5.4 clock cycles.

3. Stall cycles per instruction = 1.5 × (0.04 × 10 + 0.02 × 200) = 1.5 × (0.4 + 4) = 6.6 (check: 1.5 × (5.4 − 1) = 6.6). CPI = 1.0 + 6.6 = 7.6.

4. Without L2: stall cycles per instruction = 1.5 × 0.04 × 200 = 12; CPI = 13 (AMAT = 1 + 0.04 × 200 = 9). Speed-up due to L2 = 13 / 7.6 = 1.71.

The notes' Example 7 (p. 261) with the latencies of Hennessy & Patterson's version of the same example; the no-L2 part is added here. The whole exercise is about which rate goes where: the local 50% inside the bracket of the AMAT, the global 2% in the misses per instruction that reach memory, and no L1 hit time among the stall cycles.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Single answer.) Let us consider a computer with L1 and L2 caches with the following parameters: Processor Clock Frequency = 1 GHz; Hit Time L1 = 1 clock cycle; Hit Rate L1 = 95%; Hit Time L2 = 5 clock cycles; Hit Rate L2 = 90%; Miss Penalty L2 = 15 clock cycles. How much is the Global Miss Rate for Last Level Cache? Motivate your answer.

  • Answer 1: 5% That is the L1 miss rate, 1 − 0.95: the share that reaches L2, not the share that reaches memory.
  • Answer 2: 0.5% L2 is the last level: 0.05 × 0.10 = 0.005. Global rate = L1 miss rate × local L2 miss rate.
  • Answer 3: 10% That is the local miss rate of L2, measured against the 5% of accesses that reach it.
  • Answer 4: 7.5% The average of 5% and 10%: miss rates along a path multiply, they are not averaged.
Show model answer

Answer 2. Miss Rate L1 = 1 − 0.95 = 0.05. The L2 hit rate of 90% is local, so the L2 misses 0.10 of the accesses that reach it. Global miss rate of the last-level cache (L2) = 0.05 × 0.10 = 0.005 = 0.5%: one access in 200 goes to main memory. The clock and the hit times are not needed.

Question 3 of the 8 January 2026 paper as the notes reproduce it (p. 503); the notes' answer is right. The motivation needs one fact: the 90% is a local hit rate, so the L2 misses 10% of the 5% that reach it.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

With the parameters of the previous question, how much is the AMAT, in clock cycles?

Show model answer

AMAT = Hit Time L1 + Miss Rate L1 × (Hit Time L2 + Miss Rate L2 × Miss Penalty L2) = 1 + 0.05 × (5 + 0.10 × 15) = 1 + 0.05 × 6.5 = 1.325 clock cycles (1.325 ns at 1 GHz).

Not asked in the paper, but the same numbers make it the natural next question. The expanded form gives the same: 1 + 0.25 + 0.075. The slip to avoid is the global rate inside the bracket, 1 + 0.05 × (5 + 0.005 × 15) = 1.254.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Multiple answers.) Which of the following cache improvements are effective in reducing the miss penalty?

  • Answer 1: Adopting higher associativity in designing the cache to reducing the impact of collisions when the correct block must be loaded in the cache. It removes conflict misses: it lowers the miss rate, while each miss still costs the same.
  • Answer 2: Avoiding address translation during indexing of the cache to minimize the management time of miss penalties. Indexing with untranslated bits lets the TLB and the cache work in parallel on every access: a hit-time technique, whatever the wording of the option says.
  • Answer 3: Combining a small and fast level-1 cache with a slower but larger level-2 cache to capture many accesses that would go to the main memory. An L1 miss now costs Hit Time L2 + Miss Rate L2 × Miss Penalty L2 instead of a trip to memory.
  • Answer 4: Giving priority to read misses over writes, exploiting techniques to serve the reads before the writes have been completed. A read miss no longer waits for buffered writes, or a dirty victim's write-back, to reach memory first: the processor waits less on that miss.
Show model answer

Answers 3 and 4. Answer 1 reduces the miss rate (fewer conflict misses). Answer 2 reduces the hit time: the cache is indexed while the TLB translates. Answer 3 makes an L1 miss cost an L2 access instead of a memory access. Answer 4 serves a read miss before the pending writes complete (after checking the write buffer for the same address), so the read waits less.

Question 4 of the 8 January 2026 paper as the notes reproduce it (pp. 503–504). The notes' solution marks 2 and 3: see the red pen in the last section. The options copy, almost word for word, the titles of Hennessy & Patterson's basic cache optimizations, where each title also names the term it reduces.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(Multiple answers.) Which of the following statements about the classification of cache misses (the 3 C's model) are correct?

  • Answer 1: Compulsory misses occur even in a cache of infinite size. They are the first references to each block: no cache holds a block it has never fetched.
  • Answer 2: A fully associative cache has no conflict misses. Conflict misses are defined against a fully associative cache of the same size: its own count is zero.
  • Answer 3: Increasing the associativity at the same cache size reduces the capacity misses. Capacity misses are those of a fully associative cache of that size: more ways cannot remove them, only a bigger cache can.
  • Answer 4: A miss that replaces a valid block in a full set is a conflict miss. The first reference to a block is a cold-start miss whatever it evicts; a conflict miss is a re-reference that a fully associative cache would have hit.
  • Answer 5: Larger blocks can reduce the compulsory misses. One fetch brings the neighbours, so fewer first touches miss (p. 319), at the price of a larger miss penalty.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 5.

Built in the paper's multiple-answer form. The definitions are Hennessy & Patterson's; the remedies are the notes' Table 28 (p. 320). Answer 4 is the trap of the exam's cache table, where every first touch is a Cold-start Miss.

Lesson 24 · Misses, penalties and the design space: the second level, the 3 C’s, from misses to seconds · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following concepts and techniques of the memory hierarchy: 1. Second-level cache (L2); 2. Local and global miss rate; 3. AMAT of a two-level cache hierarchy; 4. Memory stall cycles and CPU time; 5. Compulsory misses; 6. Capacity misses; 7. Conflict misses; 8. The three strategies to improve cache performance.

Show model answer

1. A larger, slower cache between L1 and main memory (hundreds of KB to MBs, SRAM, usually unified, on chip). It catches L1 misses before they reach DRAM, so the L1 miss penalty becomes Hit Time L2 + Miss Rate L2 × Miss Penalty L2 instead of the memory latency. Benefit: L1 stays small and fast while its misses become cheap (Example 8: AMAT 6 → 2.5 cycles). It does not change the L1 miss rate; it pays off only if its hit time plus its own misses cost less than the trip to memory.

2. Local miss rate = misses of a level / accesses to that level; global miss rate = misses of a level / all CPU accesses; for L2, local = L2 misses / L1 misses and global = Miss Rate L1 × local Miss Rate L2. Example: 1000 accesses, 40 L1 misses, 20 L2 misses → 4%, 50% local, 2% global. The global rate measures the accesses that reach memory and is comparable across hierarchies; the local L2 rate is high because L1 has already served the easy accesses.

3. Nest the one-level formula: AMAT = Hit Time L1 + Miss Rate L1 × Miss Penalty L1, with Miss Penalty L1 = Hit Time L2 + Miss Rate L2 × Miss Penalty L2 (local rate). Expanded: every access pays Hit Time L1, the L1 misses pay Hit Time L2, the misses in both pay Miss Penalty L2. Example: 1 + 0.04 × (10 + 0.5 × 200) = 5.4 cycles, against 9 without L2.

4. CPU time = IC × (CPI_exec + memory stall cycles per instruction) × T_CLK; stall cycles per instruction = memory accesses per instruction × miss rate × miss penalty (misses per instruction × penalty). With L1 and L2: MAPI × (Miss Rate L1 × Hit Time L2 + Miss Rate L1 × Miss Rate L2 × Miss Penalty L2) = MAPI × (AMAT − Hit Time L1). Example: CPU3, 8 + 3 × 0.5 × 3 = 12.5. Benefit: it predicts the effect of the memory system on execution time, and shows that a faster core loses a larger share of its time to the same misses.

5. The first access to a block (cold start); they occur even in an infinite cache. Reduced by larger blocks (one miss brings the neighbours) and by prefetching; larger blocks raise the miss penalty.

6. The working set does not fit, so blocks are evicted and needed again: the misses of a fully associative cache of the same size, minus the compulsory ones. Reduced by a larger cache, at the cost of hit time, area and power.

7. Blocks that map to the same set evict each other although other lines are free: the misses of the real cache minus those of a fully associative cache of the same size. Reduced by higher associativity (more comparators, longer hit time) or a victim cache. Example: blocks 12 and 20 in a direct-mapped cache of 8 blocks, 100% miss rate, against 33% fully associative.

8. From AMAT = Hit Time + Miss Rate × Miss Penalty: reduce the miss rate (larger caches and blocks, higher associativity, prefetching, compiler optimizations), reduce the miss penalty (multilevel caches, priority to read misses over writes, early restart and critical word first, non-blocking caches), reduce the hit time (small and simple L1, avoiding address translation with virtually indexed, physically tagged caches, pipelined writes). Most techniques move two terms (bigger cache: miss rate down, hit time up; bigger block: miss rate down, miss penalty up), so each choice is a trade-off judged on AMAT.

The 2026 paper's form: eight sub-points, about five lines each. A strong sub-point has a definition, the mechanism or formula, a number and the benefit with its cost. Sub-point 2 needs both denominators named; sub-point 4 needs the multiplication by the memory accesses per instruction; sub-points 5–7 are strongest with the comparison caches of the definitions.

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

(In the form of the June 2026 Question 1.) Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following cache optimization techniques: 1. Larger block size; 2. Higher associativity; 3. Victim cache; 4. Way prediction; 5. Pseudo-associativity; 6. Hardware prefetching with stream buffers; 7. Software (compiler-controlled) prefetching; 8. Loop interchange and loop blocking.

Show model answer

1. Larger block size: a miss brings in the neighbouring words too, exploiting spatial locality, so compulsory misses decrease. For a fixed cache size there are fewer blocks, so beyond an optimum conflict and capacity misses grow (U-shaped miss rate), and the miss penalty grows with the block (latency + transfer). The AMAT-optimal block is at or below the miss-rate-optimal one; common sizes are 16–64 bytes.

2. Higher associativity: a block can be placed in any of the n ways of its set, so blocks with the same index can coexist: conflict misses decrease, compulsory and capacity misses do not change. Rules of thumb: a direct-mapped cache of size N misses about as often as a 2-way cache of size N/2; 8-way is practically fully associative. Cost: the way-select multiplexer must wait for the n tag comparisons, so the hit time (clock cycle) and the power grow; for large caches AMAT can increase.

3. Victim cache: a small (4–16 entries) fully associative buffer between the cache and the next level, holding the blocks recently evicted. On a miss it is checked; on a hit there the block is swapped back into the cache. It removes many conflict misses of a direct-mapped cache (a few blocks that keep evicting each other) without changing the hit time of the main cache; low cost. Seen from the main cache, it makes those misses cheap (miss-penalty view).

4. Way prediction: a small table indexed by the set index stores, for each set, the way that hit last (log2 n bits). Only the predicted way is read and its data is sent out at once, as in a direct-mapped cache; one tag comparison confirms it. On a mismatch the other ways are checked (+1–2 cycles); the table is updated on every wrong prediction, misses included. Benefit: hit time and energy close to direct-mapped with the miss rate of a set-associative cache (accuracy 80–95 %). It does not reduce the miss rate.

5. Pseudo-associativity: a direct-mapped cache where each block has a second possible line (e.g. the index with its MSB flipped). Check the primary line (1-cycle hit), then the secondary (slow hit, +1 cycle), then miss. Two blocks with the same index can now both stay, one in each line (the pair of lines acts as one 2-way set), so it removes many conflict misses with little hardware; cost: two different hit times, harder to pipeline; blocks are swapped so that the most recently used one is in the primary line.

6. Hardware prefetching: on a miss on block X the hardware fetches X into the cache and X+1, X+2, … into a stream buffer next to it (instruction stream buffer for sequential code, data stream buffers for sequential or strided accesses). If the next access finds the block in the buffer, it is moved into the cache and the following one is prefetched. It removes compulsory (and some capacity) misses by overlapping memory accesses with execution; the separate buffer avoids polluting the cache with wrong guesses. Cost: memory bandwidth, useless prefetches.

7. Software prefetching: the compiler inserts prefetch instructions ahead of the use (e.g. prefetch A[i+8] while using A[i]), at a distance of about miss latency / cycles per iteration. Cache prefetch (into the cache, not a register) is the common form; the prefetch must be non-faulting and the cache non-blocking, so that execution continues while the block arrives. Benefits: removes compulsory/capacity misses of predictable patterns the hardware may not detect, tunable by profiling. Costs: extra instructions (issue slots), bandwidth; too close is useless, too far may be evicted.

8. Loop interchange swaps nested loops so that the inner loop walks memory sequentially (row-major C arrays: inner loop on the last index), so each block fetched serves all its words before eviction (e.g. 16 times fewer misses with 4-byte words and 64-byte blocks). Loop blocking (tiling) splits a computation that reuses data, such as matrix multiplication, into B × B tiles that fit in the cache, so that each tile is reused before eviction: words moved go from 2N³ + N² to 2N³/B + N². Both reduce capacity (and conflict) misses at no hardware cost; they must respect the data dependences.

A complete sub-point has three parts: the mechanism in one or two sentences, the kind of miss (or the term of AMAT) it attacks, and its cost. Two classic traps: crediting way prediction with fewer misses, and saying that larger blocks reduce capacity misses. Short of time, write the AMAT term and the miss category first — they carry the answer.

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

A 64 KB cache has a hit time of 1 clock cycle. Its measured miss rate is 2.04 % with 16-byte blocks, 1.35 % with 32-byte blocks, 1.06 % with 64-byte blocks, 1.02 % with 128-byte blocks and 1.15 % with 256-byte blocks. On a miss, main memory delivers the first 16 bytes after 80 clock cycles and 16 more bytes every 2 clock cycles. Which block size (in bytes) minimizes the average memory access time?

Show model answer

Miss penalty = 80 + 2 × (B/16) clock cycles; AMAT = 1 + Miss rate × Miss penalty.

Block sizeMiss rateMiss penaltyAMAT
16 B2.04 %821 + 0.0204 × 82 = 2.67
32 B1.35 %841 + 0.0135 × 84 = 2.13
64 B1.06 %881 + 0.0106 × 88 = 1.93
128 B1.02 %961 + 0.0102 × 96 = 1.98
256 B1.15 %1121 + 0.0115 × 112 = 2.29

The minimum AMAT is 1.93 clock cycles with 64-byte blocks, although the lowest miss rate is at 128 bytes: from 64 to 128 bytes the miss rate drops only from 1.06 % to 1.02 % while the miss penalty grows from 88 to 96 cycles.

The tempting wrong answer is 128, the miss-rate optimum. Write the penalty formula first and the table second: a slip then stays in one row, where it is easy to spot.

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a 32-block main memory with a direct-mapped 8-block cache based on a write allocate with write-back protocol. The addresses are expressed as: Memory Addresses: [0, 1, 2, … 31]10; Cache Addresses: [a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h] and Cache Tags are expressed as binary numbers. At cold start the cache is empty, then there is the following sequence of memory accesses: Read [03]10, Read [11]10, Write [03]10, Read [11]10, Write [05]10, Read [03]10, Read [13]10, Read [05]10.

(a) Please complete the table (Type of memory access, Memory Address, Hit/Miss Type, Cache Tag, Set, Cache Address, Dirty bit, Write in memory). How much is the miss rate? (b) Repeat with a 2-way set associative 8-block cache with LRU. (c) Which misses of (a) disappear in (b), and why? (d) How many accesses of (a) must still fetch the block from main memory if a 2-entry fully associative victim cache is added to the direct-mapped cache?

Show model answer

(a) Direct-mapped: 8 sets, Set = address mod 8, Tag = address div 8 (2 bits); set k is cache address a + k (set 3 = d, set 5 = f).

#Type of memory accessMemory AddressHit/Miss TypeCache TagSetCache AddressDirty bitWrite in memory
1Read[03]10Cold-start Miss[00]2[3]10d0No
2Read[11]10Cold-start Miss[01]2[3]10d0No
3Write[03]10Conflict Miss[00]2[3]10d1No
4Read[11]10Conflict Miss[01]2[3]10d0Yes (write-back of block 3)
5Write[05]10Cold-start Miss[00]2[5]10f1No
6Read[03]10Conflict Miss[00]2[3]10d0No
7Read[13]10Cold-start Miss[01]2[5]10f0Yes (write-back of block 5)
8Read[05]10Conflict Miss[00]2[5]10f0No

Miss rate = 8/8 = 100 %.

(b) 2-way: 4 sets, Set = address mod 4, Tag = address div 4 (3 bits); set 0 = a, b; set 1 = c, d; set 2 = e, f; set 3 = g, h.

#Type of memory accessMemory AddressHit/Miss TypeCache TagSetCache AddressDirty bitWrite in memory
1Read[03]10Cold-start Miss[000]2[3]10g0No
2Read[11]10Cold-start Miss[010]2[3]10h0No
3Write[03]10Hit[000]2[3]10g1No
4Read[11]10Hit[010]2[3]10h0No
5Write[05]10Cold-start Miss[001]2[1]10c1No
6Read[03]10Hit[000]2[3]10g1No
7Read[13]10Cold-start Miss[011]2[1]10d0No
8Read[05]10Hit[001]2[1]10c1No

Miss rate = 4/8 = 50 %.

(c) The four conflict misses of (a), rows 3, 4, 6 and 8, become hits. Blocks 3 and 11 share a set in both caches (and so do 5 and 13), but the 2-way set holds both, while the direct-mapped line holds one at a time. The four cold-start misses remain: associativity does not remove compulsory misses. The two write-backs of (a) disappear too, because no dirty block is evicted.

(d) 4: only the cold-start misses (accesses 1, 2, 5, 7). Each conflict miss of (a) finds its block in the victim cache, where the previous access to the same line put it (access 6 finds block 3, evicted by access 4), and swaps it back into the line.

Label the misses as the July 2024 paper does ("Cold-start Miss" for a first reference) and say "Conflict Miss" when a block comes back after being evicted although the cache had free room: in a fully associative cache of 8 blocks these four accesses would hit. Dirty bits and write-backs are where most slips happen: memory is written only when a dirty block is evicted, never on the write itself.

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

A 32 KB 8-way set associative cache with 64-byte blocks uses way prediction. How many bits does the way predictor table need?

Show model answer

Number of sets = 32 768 / (64 × 8) = 64. Each entry names one of 8 ways: log2 8 = 3 bits. Table size = 64 × 3 = 192 bits (24 bytes).

The table has one entry per set, not per block: the tempting wrong answers are 512 × 3 = 1536 (one entry per block) and 64 × 8 = 512 (one bit per way).

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) Which of the following techniques reduce conflict misses?

  • Answer 1: Increasing the associativity of the cache Blocks with the same index get several places in the set.
  • Answer 2: Adding a small fully associative victim cache The block evicted by a collision waits in the victim cache and is swapped back.
  • Answer 3: Pseudo-associativity A direct-mapped cache gets a second line for each index, so two colliding blocks can both stay.
  • Answer 4: Increasing the block size for the same cache size It reduces compulsory misses, and with fewer blocks in the cache it increases conflict misses.
  • Answer 5: Way prediction It never changes what the cache holds; it reduces the hit time and the power of hits.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 3.

The quizzes of the papers build their wrong options from neighbouring techniques; the January 2026 paper, solved in the notes' Exams chapter, asked the same question about the miss penalty (lesson 24).
