Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) A 4-way set associative L1 cache adds a way predictor. Which statement is correct?

  • Answer 1: It reduces the miss rate, because the most likely way is checked first The order of the checks does not change what is in the cache: the same accesses miss.
  • Answer 2: It reduces the hit time and the energy of most hits, while the miss rate is unchanged Right: a correctly predicted hit reads one way and does not wait for the tag comparison; a wrong guess costs an extra cycle.
  • Answer 3: It removes the tag comparison from the access The predicted way's tag is still compared — to confirm the hit, in parallel with sending the data.
  • Answer 4: The predictor is updated only on hits After a miss the entry points to the way that received the new block (Example 11, access 3).
Show model answer

Answer 2.

Answer 4 is the sentence printed on p. 331 of the notes, which their own Example 11 contradicts one page later: after a miss the entry is rewritten too.

Lesson 25 · Fewer misses: size, blocks, ways, victims, prediction, prefetching and the compiler · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

(True/False, motivate your answer.) "Loop interchange, loop fusion and loop blocking reduce the compulsory misses of a program."

  • True They reorder the accesses but touch the same blocks, so every block still has its first, compulsory miss.
  • False Right: they remove repeat misses to blocks evicted and fetched again — capacity and conflict misses.
Show model answer

False. A compulsory miss is the first access to a block. These transformations change only the order of the accesses (and the loop structure), not the set of blocks the program touches, so the number of first accesses is unchanged. They reduce capacity (and conflict) misses: a block is used completely (interchange) or reused (fusion, blocking) before it is evicted.

The notes' table on p. 341 says the opposite. In the papers a motivation earns its own extra point: it must say what a compulsory miss is and why reordering the accesses cannot change their number.

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used to reduce the cache miss penalty: 1. Giving priority to read misses over writes; 2. Sub-block placement; 3. Early restart; 4. Critical word first; 5. Non-blocking caches (hit under miss, miss under miss); 6. Second-level caches; 7. Inclusive and exclusive multilevel caches; 8. Merging write buffer. (Five lines each.)

Show model answer

1. A read miss soon stalls the processor, because the following instructions need the data; a write can wait in the write buffer. So when a read miss competes with pending writes, the read is served first. In a write-through cache the read miss first compares its address with those in the write buffer: on a match it takes the data from the buffer, because memory still holds the old value (a RAW hazard through memory); with no match it goes to memory ahead of the buffered writes. In a write-back cache a dirty victim is copied into the write buffer, the new block is fetched at once and the victim is written back afterwards. Benefit: the read-miss penalty no longer includes writes nobody waits for. Cost: comparators on the buffer; a full buffer still stalls.

2. Each block is divided into sub-blocks, each with its own valid bit, under a single tag. On a miss only the requested sub-block is fetched; the others stay invalid until needed, and a hit requires both the tag match and the sub-block's valid bit. Benefit: the tag storage of a large block with the transfer time of a small one, so a lower miss penalty and less bandwidth, useful with large blocks or weak spatial locality. Cost: extra valid bits and control; less spatial locality exploited, so neighbouring words may miss later.

3. The block is fetched in normal order, but the requested word is sent to the processor as soon as it arrives and the processor resumes while the rest of the block is filled in the background. With a latency L before the first word, one word per cycle, n words per block and the requested word at position k (from 0), the stall falls from L + n to L + k + 1 cycles. Benefit: no change to the memory; the gain is larger when the word is early in the block.

4. Critical word first (wrapped fetch) asks the memory for the requested word first and then for the rest of the block, wrapping around (W2, W3, W0, W1). The stall is L + 1 cycles whatever the position of the word. Benefit: the largest gain when the word is late in the block. Cost: wrap-around fetch logic and a memory that supports it. Both 3 and 4 pay mainly with large blocks, where the transfer is a large part of the penalty, and both gain less when the processor soon needs another word of the block.

5. A blocking cache stalls the processor on a miss even if the next accesses would hit. A non-blocking (lockup-free) cache keeps serving accesses while misses are outstanding, each tracked in a Miss Status Holding Register (MSHR). Hit under miss: hits proceed while one miss is pending. Miss under miss: several misses are serviced in parallel (memory-level parallelism), with several MSHRs and a memory that accepts several requests. Benefit: the miss penalty is overlapped with useful work and with other misses, so the effective miss penalty drops; each miss itself is not shorter. It needs a processor with independent work, such as an out-of-order core. Cost: MSHRs, complexity, memory ordering.

6. An L2, larger and slower than L1 but much faster than main memory, catches most L1 misses: AMAT = L1 Hit Time + L1 Miss Rate × (L2 Hit Time + L2 Miss Rate × L2 Miss Penalty), with the local L2 miss rate; the bracket is the new L1 miss penalty. Example: 0.05 × (10 + 0.10 × 100) adds 1 cycle per access instead of 0.05 × 100 = 5. Design: much larger (hundreds of KB to MBs), more associative (8 to 16 ways), usually write-back, its slower hit time acceptable because it is not on the processor's critical cycle. Cost: every L1 miss pays the L2 lookup; area and power.

7. Inclusive: L2 holds a copy of every block in L1, so a coherence check in a multiprocessor can rely on L2 alone; the price is duplicated capacity. Exclusive: a block is either in L1 or in L2, never in both, so the total capacity is the sum of the two. Non-inclusive non-exclusive (NINE): no strict relation. In multicores a private L2 per core reduces contention and a shared L3 helps sharing and coherence.

8. In a write-through cache every store goes to memory through the write buffer; without merging, stores to adjacent addresses each become a memory transaction. A merging write buffer keeps block-sized entries with the aligned block address, a data field and a byte-valid mask; a store to a block already in the buffer is written into its entry instead of taking a new one. Benefit: fewer and larger transactions, less bus contention, so read misses wait less for the bus and the buffer fills less often. Cost: matching logic and masks; a partial entry needs a byte-enabled write or a read-merge-write; a full buffer still stalls.

The form is the June 2026 question: eight sub-points, about five lines each. Give three things in each: the mechanism, the term of AMAT it attacks, and its cost. Sub-points 3 and 4 are best answered with the three penalties; sub-point 6 with the formula from the A4 sheet; sub-point 5 with the sentence "it overlaps the miss, it does not shorten it".

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) Which of the following cache improvements are effective in reducing the miss penalty?

  • Answer 1: Adopting higher associativity in designing the cache to reducing the impact of collisions when the correct block must be loaded in the cache. Higher associativity removes conflict misses: it lowers the miss rate. A miss that still happens costs the same.
  • Answer 2: Avoiding address translation during indexing of the cache to minimize the management time of miss penalties. This takes the TLB off the path of every hit: it lowers the hit time. The notes' solution ticks it; it is wrong.
  • Answer 3: Combining a small and fast level-1 cache with a slower but larger level-2 cache to capture many accesses that would go to the main memory. An L1 miss is served by L2 most of the time: the L1 miss penalty becomes L2 Hit Time + L2 Miss Rate × L2 Miss Penalty.
  • Answer 4: Giving priority to read misses over writes, exploiting techniques to serve the reads before the writes have been completed. The read miss no longer waits for buffered writes or for a dirty write-back: each read miss is serviced sooner. The notes' solution rejects it; it is correct.
Show model answer

Answers 3 and 4. Answer 1 reduces the miss rate (conflict misses); Answer 2 reduces the hit time; Answer 3 replaces most trips to main memory with an L2 access; Answer 4 lets a read miss start its fetch without waiting for pending writes.

This is the quiz of the 8 January 2026 paper as the notes transcribe it (pp. 503–504), with the options verbatim; the notes' worked solution picks 2 and 3 (red pen in section 6). If the paper asks you to motivate the answer, one line per option with the term of AMAT is enough.

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) A cache has blocks of 8 words. On a miss, main memory needs an access latency of 30 clock cycles before the transfer starts, then transfers one word per clock cycle. The CPU requests word 5 (words are numbered from 0). How many stall cycles does the CPU pay with (i) a full-block fetch, (ii) early restart, (iii) critical word first?

  • Answer 1: 38, 36, 31 30 + 8 = 38; word 5 is the sixth to arrive, 30 + 5 + 1 = 36; sent first, 30 + 1 = 31.
  • Answer 2: 37, 35, 30 This counts the first word as arriving at the end of the latency, which the text excludes: the transfer starts after the 30 cycles.
  • Answer 3: 38, 35, 31 Early restart waits for words 0 to 5, six words, not five: 30 + 6 = 36.
  • Answer 4: 38, 36, 36 Critical word first sends word 5 first, so its position no longer matters.
Show model answer

Answer 1. Full block: L + n = 30 + 8 = 38. Early restart: L + k + 1 = 30 + 5 + 1 = 36. Critical word first: L + 1 = 31.

The whole question is in the off-by-one. Read how the text defines the latency, write \(t(p) = L + p + 1\) for the word sent \(p\)-th, and the three numbers follow.

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

(True/False) In a non-blocking cache with miss-under-miss support, each individual miss is serviced in fewer clock cycles than in a blocking cache.

  • True Main memory is just as slow: every miss still costs the full miss penalty. What changes is that the misses overlap each other and the hits.
  • False Right: the cache overlaps misses, it does not shorten them. The effective penalty seen by the processor drops; the penalty of each miss does not.
Show model answer

False. A non-blocking cache keeps serving hits and starts further misses while one is outstanding (tracked in MSHRs). Each miss takes as long as before; the processor simply does other work, so the stall it sees, the effective miss penalty, is smaller.

The distinction between the miss penalty and the effective miss penalty is the point. The widget of section 4 shows it: the orange bars keep their length in every mode.

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

A cache has blocks of 16 words. On a miss, main memory needs an access latency of 40 clock cycles before the transfer starts, then transfers one word per clock cycle, in order from word 0. With early restart, how many clock cycles does the CPU stall when it requests word 9 (words numbered from 0)?

Show model answer
\[ L + k + 1 = 40 + 9 + 1 = 50 \text{ clock cycles} \]

For comparison: full-block fetch 40 + 16 = 56, critical word first 40 + 1 = 41.

Early restart does not change the order: words 0 to 9 must arrive, ten transfer cycles after the 40 of latency.

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let us consider a computer with L1 and L2 caches with the following parameters: Hit Time L1 = 1 clock cycle; Hit Rate L1 = 95%; Hit Time L2 = 10 clock cycles; Hit Rate L2 = 90%; Miss Penalty L2 = 100 clock cycles.

1. How much is the miss penalty of L1? 2. How much is the AMAT? 3. How much is the Global Miss Rate for the Last Level Cache? 4. How much would the AMAT be without L2, if the L1 misses were served by main memory in 100 clock cycles? 5. How much is the speed-up of the average memory access due to L2?

Show model answer

Miss Rate L1 = 1 − 0.95 = 0.05; Miss Rate L2 (local) = 1 − 0.90 = 0.10.

1. The L1 miss penalty, in clock cycles:

\[ MP_{L1} = 10 + 0.10 \times 100 = 20 \]

2. The AMAT, in clock cycles:

\[ \text{AMAT} = 1 + 0.05 \times 20 = 2 \]

3. The global miss rate of L2, the last-level cache:

\[ 0.05 \times 0.10 = 0.005 = 0.5\% \]

4. Without L2, in clock cycles:

\[ \text{AMAT} = 1 + 0.05 \times 100 = 6 \]

5. Speed-up of the average access: 6 / 2 = 3.

These are the notes' Example 16 (p. 351) with an L1 hit time of 1 cycle, written in the form of the January 2026 quiz. The trap is item 1: the notes call the 10 cycles the L1 miss penalty, but they are the L2 hit time (red pen in section 5). Turn every hit rate into a miss rate before you substitute.

Lesson 26 · Cheaper misses: reads first, the critical word, caches that do not block, the second level · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

A write-through cache has a merging write buffer of 4 entries; each entry holds a 32-byte block with a byte-valid mask. With R1 = 0x1000 and R2 = 0x2000, the CPU executes the following stores (8 bytes each) before the bus becomes free:

SD  F2, 0(R1)
SD  F4, 8(R1)
SD  F6, 0(R2)
SD  F8, 16(R1)
SD  F10, 24(R1)
SD  F12, 8(R2)

Complete a table with the columns Store | Address | Block base | Action | Valid bytes of the entry after the store. How many write transactions reach memory with and without merging, and of which kind?

Show model answer
StoreAddressBlock baseActionValid bytes after
SD F2, 0(R1)0x10000x1000allocate E0E0: 0–7
SD F4, 8(R1)0x10080x1000merge into E0E0: 0–15
SD F6, 0(R2)0x20000x2000allocate E1E1: 0–7
SD F8, 16(R1)0x10100x1000merge into E0E0: 0–23
SD F10, 24(R1)0x10180x1000merge into E0E0: 0–31 (full)
SD F12, 8(R2)0x20080x2000merge into E1E1: 0–15 (partial)

With merging: 2 entries and 2 transactions, one full 32-byte write for E0 and one partial 16-byte write for E1 (with byte enables, or a read-merge-write if the bus has none). Without merging: 6 transactions, one per store; the 4-entry buffer is full after the fourth store, so the fifth and the sixth store stall the CPU until an entry has been written to memory.

The block base is the address with its 5 low bits cleared (32-byte blocks). Three points to get right: the alignment, the merge into an existing entry even when another block came in between (the third store), and the difference between a full and a partial flush. The widget of section 2 replays this stream ("two blocks, interleaved").

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used in the memory hierarchy of modern processors: 1. Paged virtual memory; 2. Per-process page tables; 3. Translation lookaside buffer (TLB); 4. Handling of TLB misses and page faults; 5. Virtually indexed, physically tagged (VIPT) L1 caches; 6. Indexing the cache with page-offset bits only; 7. Higher associativity in a VIPT L1 cache; 8. Physically indexed, physically tagged (PIPT) L2 and L3 caches.

Show model answer

1. The virtual and the physical address spaces are divided into pages of one fixed size (for example 4 KB). Each process sees a large, private, contiguous virtual space; the OS maps its pages onto physical frames anywhere in RAM. A virtual address is VPN | page offset; translation replaces the VPN with the PFN and leaves the offset unchanged. Benefits: programs larger than RAM (main memory acts as a cache for the disk), protection and isolation between processes, relocation, sharing of frames; fixed-size pages avoid external fragmentation. Cost: a translation on every access.

2. Each process has a page table in memory, indexed by the VPN; each entry holds the PFN, a valid bit, a dirty bit, a use bit and protection bits. The same virtual address in two processes maps to different frames (isolation), two VPNs may point to one frame (sharing), and the hardware enforces the permissions. Because a page fault costs millions of cycles, placement is fully associative (any frame), replacement approximates LRU with the use bit, and the write policy is always write-back with the dirty bit. Cost: memory for the tables, hence multi-level tables.

3. A small, fast, fully or highly associative cache of recent translations: tag = VPN, data = PFN and the flags. It is looked up on every access; on a hit the physical address is available in about one cycle, with the protection check. Benefit: without it every access would need an extra memory access to read the page table. Cost: it must stay tiny (tens of entries) to answer within the cycle.

4. On a TLB miss the page table is walked in memory, by a hardware walker or a software handler, in tens of cycles; if the entry is valid it is inserted into the TLB and the access completes, with no OS involvement for a hardware walker. If the entry is invalid, the page is not in RAM: a page-fault exception transfers control to the OS, which chooses a frame (writing back a dirty victim), reads the page from the disk, updates the entry and restarts the faulting instruction; this costs millions of cycles and requires precise exceptions. A TLB miss is not a page fault.

5. The cache index is taken from the virtual address and the tag from the physical address. The set is read while the TLB translates the VPN; the PFN is then compared with the tags of the set. Benefit: the TLB latency leaves the hit time, max(TLB, set read) + compare instead of TLB + cache, for example 1 cycle instead of 2 (notes, p. 360); since most accesses are hits, the AMAT drops. The TLB is still read on every access, for the tag and the permissions. Cost: the size restriction of point 7.

6. Translation never changes the page offset, so index and block-offset bits taken from it are the same in the virtual and the physical address and can be used before the TLB answers (the page offset trick). It also solves synonyms, two virtual addresses mapped to one physical address (shared memory, mapped files): all aliases select the same set and, with physical tags, match the same line, so only one copy exists and no stale value can be read. A VIVT cache could keep two copies with different virtual tags.

7. All index bits must lie in the page offset: index bits + block-offset bits ≤ page-offset bits, that is cache size / associativity ≤ page size. With 4 KB pages and 64-byte blocks a 32 KB 4-way cache (8 KB per way, 7 index bits) takes one index bit from the VPN; 8 ways make it safe (4 KB per way), and 48 KB needs 12 ways. A larger VIPT L1 therefore needs more ways. Cost: more comparators, a wider way multiplexer, more energy and a longer hit time, which way prediction mitigates. Alternatives: page colouring, hardware alias detection, larger pages.

8. L2 and L3 use the physical address for both index and tag. They are accessed only after an L1 miss, when the TLB has already translated the address, so translation adds nothing to their access and they have no synonym problem and no page-size limit: they can be large (MBs) and highly associative. Their longer hit time is acceptable because it is paid only on L1 misses.

The form is the June 2026 question: eight sub-points, about five lines each. For each technique the examiner looks for the mechanism, what it buys and what it costs. Sub-points 4 and 7 carry the usual traps: a TLB miss is not a page fault, and the law limits one way, not the whole cache.

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

A processor has 32-bit virtual addresses, 4 KB pages and an L1 data cache with 32-byte blocks. 1. The L1 is 8 KB, 2-way set-associative: give the structure of the address as seen by the cache (tag | index | offset, with bit positions) and say whether the cache can be virtually indexed and physically tagged without synonym problems. 2. The designers want 64 KB, keeping 2 ways: how many index bits come from the virtual page number, and in how many sets could an alias of a block be found? 3. What is the minimum associativity of a 64 KB VIPT L1? 4. Give two other ways to make the 64 KB 2-way cache safe.

Show model answer

Page offset = log₂ 4096 = 12 bits, [11:0]. Block offset = log₂ 32 = 5 bits, [4:0].

1. Sets = 8 KB / (32 B × 2) = 128, so the index has 7 bits, [11:5], and the tag 32 − 7 − 5 = 20 bits, [31:12]: |20-bit tag | 7-bit index | 5-bit offset|. Index + offset = 7 + 5 = 12 bits = the page offset; equivalently 8 KB / 2 = 4 KB ≤ 4 KB. The cache can be VIPT, and its tag is exactly the PFN.

2. Sets = 64 KB / (32 B × 2) = 1024, so the index has 10 bits, [14:5]. Bits 14, 13 and 12 lie above the page offset: k = 10 + 5 − 12 = 3 bits come from the VPN, and an alias may be in any of 2³ = 8 sets.

3. Minimum associativity = 64 KB / 4 KB = 16 ways: 128 sets, index [11:5] again.

4. Pages of 32 KB (15 offset bits contain index [14:5]); page colouring by the OS, so that all aliases of a frame agree on virtual-address bits 14 to 12; hardware that checks the 8 candidate sets on a miss and removes a duplicate (as the AMD Opteron did).

Part 1 is the address-structure quiz of July 2024 (lesson 23) with one more question: do index and offset fit in the page offset? The examiner checks the count k = i + o − p and the \(2^k\) candidate sets, and that the fixes act on C / A or on P, never on the block size.

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Pages are 4 KB. The L1 data cache is 4 KB, direct-mapped, with 64-byte blocks, write-back and write-allocate. The operating system maps the virtual addresses VA1 = 0x4000_1234 and VA2 = 0x8000_1234 to the same physical address PA = 0x1000_1234, where memory holds 0. The CPU performs: (1) write 7 via VA1; (2) read via VA2; (3) write 9 via VA2; (4) read via VA1. Give the set that all four accesses use, then complete the table Type of memory access | Hit/ Miss Type | Cache Tag | Dirty bit | Write in memory | Value returned, first for a virtually indexed, virtually tagged cache, then for a virtually indexed, physically tagged one. Does either design return a wrong value?

Show model answer

64 sets: index = bits [11:6] of the page offset 0x234 = 001000 = set 8 for all three addresses. Virtual tags: 0x40001 (VA1) and 0x80001 (VA2); physical tag: 0x10001.

VIVT:

In the VIVT table, "Yes (7)" means that the dirty line of VA1, holding 7, is written back to PA 0x1000_1234 before VA2's block is fetched; that fetch then reads the 7.

Neither design returns a wrong value. The aliases share set 8, and a direct-mapped set holds one block: the VIVT cache never has two copies, it pays 3 misses out of 4 and 2 write-backs instead (ping-pong). The VIPT cache has 1 miss and 3 hits. With a second way (8 KB 2-way) the VIVT cache would keep both copies and both reads would return stale values.

This is the notes' own Example 20 configuration, run to the end: it is the counterexample to their "Set 10 / Set 42" story (red pen in section 4). The examiner checks the set from the page offset, the tags as page numbers (0x40001, not 0x4000), and the write-backs. A VIVT cache must also translate the victim's address to write it back to PA 0x1000_1234; the table assumes it does.

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

A physically indexed L1 cache waits for the TLB: TLB lookup 1 clock cycle, then the cache hit 1 clock cycle. A VIPT version overlaps the TLB lookup completely with the indexing, so its hit takes 1 clock cycle. In both designs the miss rate is 5 % and the miss penalty 20 clock cycles. How much is the speed-up of the average memory access time of the VIPT design over the serial one?

Show model answer
\[ \text{AMAT}_{\text{serial}} = 2 + 0.05 \times 20 = 3 \] \[ \text{AMAT}_{\text{VIPT}} = 1 + 0.05 \times 20 = 2 \]

Speed-up = 3 / 2 = 1.5.

The miss term is the same 1 cycle in both designs: only the hit time changes. The widget of section 3 shows that the gap stays one cycle at any miss rate.

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) An L1 data cache of 64 KB with 64-byte blocks must be virtually indexed and physically tagged on a machine with 4 KB pages. What is the minimum associativity such that no index bit comes from the virtual page number?

  • Answer 1: 4-way 16 KB per way: 8 index bits [13:6], two of them from the VPN.
  • Answer 2: 8-way 8 KB per way: 7 index bits [12:6], bit 12 still from the VPN.
  • Answer 3: 16-way 64 KB / 16 = 4 KB per way = one page: 64 sets, index [11:6].
  • Answer 4: 32-way Safe, with 2 KB per way, but not the minimum: 16 ways already fit.
Show model answer

Answer 3. Minimum associativity = cache size / page size = 64 KB / 4 KB = 16. The block size does not enter: it moves bits between index and block offset, not out of the page offset.

The quick route is the law, C / A ≤ P; the slow route counts sets and bits. Both give 16. Answer 2 is the near miss: 8 ways halve the overflow, but bit 12 is still a VPN bit.

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) In a virtually indexed, physically tagged cache, the tag stored with each block is:

  • Answer 1: the virtual page number of the address that brought the block into the cache That is a virtually tagged (VIVT) cache, the design with synonyms.
  • Answer 2: bits of the physical address, from the physical page number supplied by the TLB The tag is physical: every alias of the block matches it.
  • Answer 3: the page offset of the address The page offset supplies the index and the block offset; it is never compared.
  • Answer 4: no tag, because the index identifies the block Many blocks share an index; without a tag a hit could not be recognised.
Show model answer

Answer 2. The index comes from the page-offset bits of the virtual address, the tag from the physical address: the PFN delivered by the TLB is compared with the tags of the selected set.

Read the name backwards: physically tagged. The distractors are the other two designs and the index.

Lesson 27 · Virtual memory in one lesson, and VIPT: start the cache before the TLB answers · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

(True/False) A TLB miss means that the page is not in main memory, so the operating system must load it from the disk.

  • True That describes a page fault. After a TLB miss the page is usually in memory, and the walk only refills the TLB.
  • False A TLB miss is a miss in the cache of translations: the page table is walked in tens of cycles. Only an invalid entry, a page fault, involves the disk.
Show model answer

False. On a TLB miss the page table in memory is walked and the translation inserted into the TLB, in tens of cycles. Only if the page-table entry is invalid does a page fault occur, and the OS loads the page from the disk, in millions of cycles.

The distinction between the two misses is the whole point. If asked to motivate, give the cost of each.

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used to improve the performance of a cache, and say which term of the average memory access time each one reduces: 1. Small and simple first-level caches; 2. Way prediction; 3. Avoiding address translation during indexing of the cache; 4. Pipelined writes; 5. Small sub-blocks for write-through caches; 6. Victim cache; 7. Giving priority to read misses over writes; 8. Second-level caches.

Show model answer
  1. Hit time. The L1 is accessed on every cycle, so its access time bounds the clock period or adds pipeline stages. A small array has short wires, a simple index decoder and a small tag array; low associativity needs few comparators and, when direct-mapped, no way-select multiplexer, so the data can be sent on while the tag is compared. Benefit: minimum hit time and lower energy per access. Cost: a higher miss rate, worth it only if (MR_small − MR_big) × MP < HT_big − HT_small, i.e. when a fast L2 keeps the miss penalty small.
  2. Hit time. A small table remembers, for each set, the way that hit last; only that way is read and its data is sent at once, as in a direct-mapped cache, while one tag comparison confirms the hit. A wrong guess costs an extra cycle to check the other ways, and the table is updated. Benefit: the hit time and energy of a direct-mapped cache with the miss rate of a set-associative one. It does not reduce the miss rate.
  3. Hit time. The cache is indexed with bits of the virtual address that lie in the page offset, identical in the physical address, while the TLB translates in parallel; the physical tag is compared at the end (virtually indexed, physically tagged). Benefit: the TLB leaves the critical path of every hit. Constraint: index and block-offset bits must fit in the page offset, so cache size / associativity ≤ page size (for example 32 KB, 8-way, with 4 KB pages).
  4. Hit time. A write hit is slower than a read hit, because the tag must be checked before the data array is written: a wrong read can be discarded, a wrong write cannot. The write is split into stages (address decode, tag check, data write) and a new write enters every cycle; the data write of one store overlaps the tag check of the next, the data waiting in a delayed-write buffer. Benefit: one write per cycle instead of one every k cycles; the latency of a write does not change. Loads must check the buffer for a pending write to the same address.
  5. Hit time. Each one-word sub-block has its own valid bit, so the cache writes the word and the tag in parallel with the tag check. Match: the write was right (set the word's valid bit if needed). Mismatch: the overwritten word belonged to another block that memory already holds (write-through), so the line keeps the new tag with only this word valid. Benefit: writes never wait for the tag check, and a write miss fetches nothing. Usable only with write-through: with write-back a dirty block could be destroyed.
  6. Miss rate. A small fully associative buffer (4–16 entries) between the cache and the next level keeps the blocks recently evicted; on a miss it is searched, and a hit there swaps the block back into the cache. It removes many conflict misses of a direct-mapped cache without changing its hit time. Seen from the L1 alone, a victim-cache hit is a cheap miss.
  7. Miss penalty. A read miss stalls the processor, a buffered write does not; so read misses are served before the writes waiting in the write buffer, after checking the buffer for the same address (otherwise a RAW hazard through memory). In a write-back cache a dirty victim is moved to the write buffer and written after the new block has been fetched. Benefit: the read-miss penalty no longer includes pending writes.
  8. Miss penalty. An L2, larger and slower than the L1 but much faster than main memory, serves most L1 misses: AMAT = L1 Hit Time + L1 Miss Rate × (L2 Hit Time + L2 Miss Rate × L2 Miss Penalty), with the local L2 miss rate. The L1 miss rate is unchanged; the L1 miss penalty falls, and the global miss rate (L1 Miss Rate × L2 Miss Rate) is the fraction of accesses that reaches memory. It also makes a small, fast L1 affordable.

Eight sub-points in the form of June 2026, about five lines each. A complete sub-point names the mechanism, the term of AMAT and the cost. Two traps: putting "avoiding address translation" under the miss penalty (the January 2026 quiz of lesson 26), and crediting an L2 with a lower L1 miss rate. For sub-point 4, start with why a write hit is slow; for sub-point 5, with the three cases.

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let us consider two designs for the first-level cache of a processor. Design S: 16 KB direct-mapped cache with Hit Time = 1 clock cycle and Miss Rate = 6%. Design B: 64 KB 4-way set associative cache with Hit Time = 2 clock cycles and Miss Rate = 3%. Both are backed by the same L2 cache with Hit Time L2 = 10 clock cycles, Miss Rate L2 (local) = 20% and Miss Penalty L2 = 100 clock cycles.

1. How much is the miss penalty of L1? 2. How much is the AMAT of each design? 3. For which L1 miss penalty would the two designs have the same AMAT? 4. How much would the AMAT of each design be without the L2, if the L1 misses were served by main memory in 100 clock cycles? Which design would you choose in each case?

Show model answer

1. Miss Penalty L1 = Hit Time L2 + Miss Rate L2 × Miss Penalty L2, in clock cycles:

\[ MP_{L1} = 10 + 0.20 \times 100 = 30 \]

2. AMAT = Hit Time + Miss Rate × Miss Penalty, in clock cycles:

\[ \text{AMAT}_S = 1 + 0.06 \times 30 = 2.8 \] \[ \text{AMAT}_B = 2 + 0.03 \times 30 = 2.9 \]

Design S is faster by 0.1 clock cycles per access.

3. Equal AMAT when the extra misses of S cost exactly the cycle it saves on every hit:

\[ 1 + 0.06\, MP = 2 + 0.03\, MP \] \[ MP = \frac{2 - 1}{0.06 - 0.03} = 33.3 \]

S is faster for an L1 miss penalty below about 33 clock cycles.

4. Without the L2 the miss penalty is 100 clock cycles:

\[ \text{AMAT}_S = 1 + 0.06 \times 100 = 7.0 \] \[ \text{AMAT}_B = 2 + 0.03 \times 100 = 5.0 \]

With the L2 choose S (2.8 against 2.9); without it choose B (5.0 against 7.0).

Work in this order: first the L1 miss penalty, from the two-level formula of the example A4 sheet, then the two AMATs. The trap is to use 100 cycles as the L1 miss penalty in part 2. Note how close 30 is to the break-even 33.3: a slightly slower L2 would flip the choice.

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

The write hits of a cache go through a write path of four one-cycle stages (address decode, tag check, data array update, write buffer update). With pipelined writes, how many clock cycles do 10 back-to-back store hits need, from the first decode to the last stage of the tenth store?

Show model answer
\[ k + N - 1 = 4 + 10 - 1 = 13 \]

Without pipelining \(kN\) = 40 clock cycles. The throughput becomes one store per cycle instead of one every 4; each store still takes 4 cycles.

The pipeline fill counts once: the first store needs all four cycles, each of the other nine finishes one cycle later. The same arithmetic as N instructions in a k-stage pipeline (lesson 3).

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) Which of the following cache improvements are effective in reducing the hit time?

  • Answer 1: Adopting higher associativity to reduce conflict misses It lowers the miss rate and lengthens the hit: the way-select multiplexer waits for more comparisons.
  • Answer 2: Avoiding address translation during indexing of the cache Right: the cache is indexed while the TLB translates, so translation leaves the path of every hit (lesson 27).
  • Answer 3: Small and simple first-level caches Right: short wires, few comparators, no way-select multiplexer.
  • Answer 4: Critical word first It shortens the miss penalty: the requested word arrives first.
  • Answer 5: Pipelined writes Right: a write hit no longer holds the cache for its tag check and its data write in a row.
Show model answer

Answers 2, 3 and 5. Answer 1 reduces the miss rate (and increases the hit time); Answer 4 reduces the miss penalty.

The options mix the hit-time list of the notes (p. 369) with one miss-rate and one miss-penalty technique, the way the January 2026 quiz built its wrong answers. Sort each option by the term it changes and the answer follows.

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) Which of the following changes increase the hit time of a first-level cache?

  • Answer 1: Increasing the block size, keeping the cache size No: at a fixed cache size the tag, the comparisons and the data array stay the same; the price of larger blocks is the miss penalty.
  • Answer 2: Increasing the associativity Right: more tags to compare, and the way-select multiplexer waits for them.
  • Answer 3: Increasing the cache size Right: a bigger array has longer wires and a bigger decoder.
  • Answer 4: Adding a victim cache No: it is searched only after a miss, so the hit path does not change.
  • Answer 5: Adding a second-level cache No: the L1 hit is untouched; the L2 lookup is part of the L1 miss penalty.
Show model answer

Answers 2 and 3. A larger block raises the miss penalty, a victim cache and an L2 act only after an L1 miss.

Answers 1 and 5 are the cells a and f of Table 29 that this lesson corrects. If the paper asks you to motivate, give one line per option: the term it really changes.

Lesson 28 · Hit time is the critical path: small L1, pipelined writes, and the nineteen-technique scorecard · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

(True/False) Pipelining the cache writes reduces the latency of each individual write.

  • True Each write still goes through all its stages; only the next write no longer waits for it.
  • False Right: pipelining raises the number of writes per cycle, not the speed of one write.
Show model answer

False. With pipelined writes a new write can start every cycle, so the throughput rises (to one write per cycle in Example 22 of the notes), but each write still needs its address decode, tag check and data write: "latency still 3, but overlapped". Its data may even wait longer in the delayed-write buffer when loads are using the data array.

Motivate a true/false with the mechanism: throughput against latency, the same distinction as for an instruction pipeline.

Module 7 · Threads, vectors and GPUs

25 questions · Notes, chapters 7–9 · multithreading, SIMD and vector machines, VMIPS and convoys, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs

Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI

Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

QUESTION 2: MULTITHREADING (5 points). Let's consider the fine-grained and simultaneous multithreading techniques used to manage thread-level parallelism by hardware processors. Answer to the following questions:

  • Explain the main concepts for each technique.
  • For each technique, make an example with up to 4 threads (T1, T2, T3, T4) on a quad-issue processor.
  • Let's consider a quad-issue SMT processor that can manage up to 4 threads: How much is the ideal CPI? How much is the ideal per-thread CPI?
  • What are the main benefits for each technique?
  • What are the main drawbacks for each technique?
Show model answer

Concepts. Fine-grained MT: the core holds the context (PC, registers) of several threads and switches thread every clock cycle, round robin, skipping a thread that is stalled; in each cycle all the issue slots belong to one thread. SMT: on a dynamically scheduled (out-of-order, renamed) superscalar, the issue slots of one cycle are filled with ready instructions of several threads; each thread has its own PC and renaming table, instructions carry a thread tag in the reservation stations and the ROB, and functional units and caches are shared.

Example, fine-grained (one thread per cycle; T4 is stalled at C7 and skipped):

CycleIssue 1Issue 2Issue 3Issue 4
C0T1T1T1–
C1T2T2T2–
C2T3T3T3–
C3T4T4T4–
C4T1T1––
C5T2T2––
C6T3–––
C7T1T1T1T1
C8T2T2T2–
C9T3T3––

Example, SMT (several threads per cycle; T4 is stalled from C3 to C6 and the others take its slots):

CycleIssue 1Issue 2Issue 3Issue 4
C0T1T2T3T4
C1T2T3T4T1
C2T3T4T1T2
C3T1T1T2T3
C4T1T1T1T2
C5T2T2T2T1
C6T3T3T1–
C7T4T4T3T3
C8T3T4T4T4
C9T3T3T3T4

Ideal CPI = 1 / issue width = 1/4 = 0.25. Ideal per-thread CPI = threads / issue width = 4/4 = 1: the four slots are shared by four threads, one instruction per cycle each.

Benefits. Fine-grained: hides short and long stalls (RAW, mispredictions, misses), because a stalled thread just loses its turn; no switch cost; simple fair rotation. SMT: the highest throughput, because it fills both the empty cycles (vertical waste) and the empty slots inside a cycle (horizontal waste); it combines ILP and TLP at a small hardware cost on an out-of-order core.

Drawbacks. Fine-grained: each thread runs slower when the others are ready (one cycle in N); the slots a thread cannot fill in its cycle stay empty; a thread selection every cycle. SMT: the threads compete for caches, functional units and branch predictors, so per-thread performance is lower and unpredictable; complex fetch, issue and commit logic; a larger register file.

This is the July 2024 question, a two-column table with five rows; the CPI row is asked for SMT only. What it checks: the rule "one thread per cycle" against "several threads per cycle", said in the concepts and visible in the drawings; the two numbers with their reason; benefits and drawbacks named in terms of waste. The two grids are the widget's exam example, quad-issue with four threads: fine-grained fills 26 of 40 slots, SMT 39. Any grid that respects the two rules is a valid example.

Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

QUESTION 2: MULTICORE PROCESSORS (5 points). High-performance multicore processors today use a combination of several computer architecture techniques. Let's consider a single-chip dual-core shared-memory architecture where each core is a dual-issue superscalar processor with multi-threading up to 2 threads per core.

  • Explain the preferred technique used to manage the multiple threads for each core.
  • Make an example of scheduling up to 4 threads per chip where T1 and T2 are executed on Core1 and T3 and T4 on Core2 (table: Cycle C0–C9 × Core 1: Issue 1, Issue 2 × Core 2: Issue 1, Issue 2).
  • Let's consider our dual-issue SMT processor that can manage up to 4 concurrent threads (2-thread per core): How much is the ideal per core CPI? How much is the ideal dual-core CPI? How much is the ideal per-thread CPI?
  • Explain the preferred technique used in each superscalar processor to manage the instruction scheduling in each thread.
  • Explain the preferred technique used to manage the cache coherency problem in the dual core chip.
Show model answer

Threads in each core: simultaneous multithreading (Intel Hyper-Threading style). Both hardware threads of a core are active at once and, in the same cycle, their ready instructions fill the two issue slots of the shared out-of-order engine; per thread a PC and a renaming table, thread tags in the reservation stations and the ROB; caches and functional units shared.

CycleCore 1Core 2
Issue 1Issue 2Issue 1Issue 2
C0T1T2T3T4
C1T2T1T4T3
C2T1T2T3T4
C3T2T1T3–
C4T1T2––
C5T2T1––
C6T1T2T3T3
C7T2T1T4T3
C8T1–T3T4
C9T1–T4T3

Ideal CPIs: per core = 1/2 = 0.5; dual-core = 1/(2 × 2) = 0.25, since the chip completes four instructions per cycle; per thread = 2/2 = 1, since each thread shares its core's two slots with one other thread.

Scheduling inside each thread: dynamic scheduling — Tomasulo's algorithm with reservation stations and register renaming, out-of-order execution, and a reorder buffer for speculation with in-order commit, together with dynamic branch prediction.

Coherence: each core has private caches holding copies of shared blocks; a snooping write-invalidate, write-back protocol (MESI) on the shared bus keeps them coherent: a write to a shared block first invalidates the other copies.

The June 2025 question. The schedule is the widget's exam example split over two cores. At C4 and C5 both threads of core 2 are stalled: its slots stay empty, because core 1's threads cannot issue on core 2 — the thread-to-core assignment is part of the question. Any schedule in which each thread stays on its core and either thread of a core may take both of its slots is a valid example. For the last row snooping is the natural choice: two cores on one chip share a bus, while directories serve machines with many nodes (lessons 37–38).

Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Question 1: Instruction-Level and Thread-Level Parallelism. Modern processors exploit Instruction Level Parallelism and Thread Level Parallelism. Answer to the following questions:

  • Explain the main concepts for each approach (Instruction Level Parallelism ILP, Thread Level Parallelism TLP).
  • Which type of technique can be applied in superscalar processors to combine both ILP and TLP?
  • Explain what type of instruction scheduling is used in superscalar processors to combine both ILP and TLP?
  • What are the main hardware modifications required for a generic superscalar processor to support both ILP and TLP?
Show model answer

ILP executes independent instructions of one instruction stream in parallel: pipelining, multiple issue (superscalar or VLIW), dynamic scheduling with out-of-order execution, register renaming against WAR and WAW hazards, branch prediction and speculation. It is limited by true dependences, control dependences and memory latency, is extracted mostly by the hardware (by the compiler in a VLIW) and speeds up one thread. TLP executes independent instruction streams — threads or processes provided by the programmer or the OS — in parallel: multithreading in one core (coarse-grained, fine-grained, SMT), multicores and shared-memory multiprocessors (coherent caches, synchronisation with locks and barriers), and clusters with private memories and message passing. It raises the throughput of a multithreaded workload.

Combining both: simultaneous multithreading, which fills the issue slots of a superscalar in the same cycle with instructions of several threads.

Scheduling: dynamic scheduling — the hardware reorders the instructions at run time (Tomasulo with renaming and a reorder buffer), instead of static scheduling by the compiler as in simple in-order superscalars and VLIWs. One instruction window holds the instructions of all active threads; every cycle the ready instructions, from whichever thread, are issued to the free functional units. Renaming gives each in-flight instruction a unique physical destination, so threads cannot collide on register names.

Hardware: a PC per thread; a renaming table per thread over a larger shared physical register file that holds every thread's architectural registers; a thread tag on every instruction in the reservation stations, the ROB and the load/store queues; per-thread commit and exception squash; a fetch policy that selects the thread each cycle (round robin, ICOUNT); issue logic that picks ready instructions across threads; buffers shared or partitioned between threads; caches, functional units and predictors shared.

The question of the 11 February 2025 paper as the notes transcribe it (p. 574). Their worked answer (pp. 574–577) is long and has three slips, corrected in the red pens of sections 4 and 5: clusters do not share memory, SMT threads raise no coherence problem, and the buffers need not all grow. The compact hardware list of Hennessy and Patterson — a renaming table per thread, separate PCs, commit from several threads — is a good first line, then expand.

Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

QUESTION 1: SUPERSCALAR PROCESSORS (8 points). Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by modern superscalar processors (Tomasulo-style): 1. Dynamic Instruction Scheduling; 2. Dynamic branch prediction and speculative execution; 3. Multiple-issue pipeline; 4. Reservation stations; 5. Dynamic Register Renaming; 6. Out-of-Order Execution; 7. Reorder Buffer; 8. Simultaneous multi-threading.

Here: sub-point 8 only, in five lines.

Show model answer

8. Simultaneous multi-threading. Several hardware threads are active at once in one out-of-order superscalar core: each keeps its own PC and renaming table over the shared physical registers, and a thread tag in the reservation stations and the ROB keeps results and commits apart. Every cycle the issue logic sends the ready instructions of any thread to the free functional units, so one cycle mixes threads. Benefit: the other threads fill the slots that one thread's ILP leaves empty (vertical and horizontal waste) — higher throughput and unit utilisation for little hardware (Hyper-Threading: under 5 % of the die, up to 30 % more throughput). Cost: shared caches and units, so single threads run slower and less predictably.

The June 2026 paper asks eight techniques in five lines each, one point apiece; SMT is sub-point 8. A good answer names four things: several threads issue in the same cycle (the line that separates SMT from fine-grained multithreading), on an out-of-order core with per-thread renaming and thread tags, filling the slots ILP leaves empty, at the price of contention. The other seven sub-points belong to lessons 5 to 18.

Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) Let's consider a quad-issue SMT processor that can manage up to 8 simultaneous threads. What are the values of the ideal CPI and the ideal per-thread CPI?

  • Answer 1: Ideal CPI = 1 & Ideal per-thread CPI = 0.125 1 is the ideal of a single-issue pipeline, and 0.125 = 1/8 divides by the threads, as if sharing made a thread faster than the core.
  • Answer 2: Ideal CPI = 0.5 & Ideal per-thread CPI = 2 The per-thread value is right, but 0.5 is the ideal of a dual-issue core; four slots give 1/4.
  • Answer 3: Ideal CPI = 0.25 & Ideal per-thread CPI = 2 1/w = 1/4 = 0.25; eight threads share four slots, half an instruction per cycle each: 8/4 = 2.
  • Answer 4: Ideal CPI = 0.5 & Ideal per-thread CPI = 4 Both values belong to a dual-issue core with 8 threads: 1/2 and 8/2.
  • Answer 5: Ideal CPI = 0.25 & Ideal per-thread CPI = 4 The core value is right; 4 would need sixteen threads on four slots.
Show model answer

Answer 3. Ideal CPI = 1 / issue width = 1/4 = 0.25; ideal per-thread CPI = threads / issue width = 8/4 = 2.

The June 2025 quiz, with its options verbatim. All three papers build the five options from the same few numbers permuted, so the only defence is the two formulas: 1/w for the core, n/w per thread. The CPI widget of section 4 drills the other two papers' versions.
