Lesson 29 · Multithreading: fine-grained, coarse-grained, SMT, and the exam’s per-thread CPI · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

(True/False) In fine-grained multithreading, instructions from different threads can be issued in the same clock cycle.

  • True That is the definition of simultaneous multithreading. Fine-grained MT changes thread between cycles; inside a cycle all the issued instructions belong to one thread.
  • False Right: one thread per cycle, a different one every cycle. That is exactly why fine-grained MT leaves horizontal waste.
Show model answer

False. Fine-grained multithreading switches thread every clock cycle, so consecutive cycles may belong to different threads, but within one cycle only one thread issues; issuing from several threads in the same cycle is simultaneous multithreading.

The classic confusion in the July 2024 two-column answer. If the drawing of the fine-grained column shows two threads in one row, the example contradicts the concept.

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following topics of SIMD and vector architectures: 1. Data-level parallelism; 2. Flynn's taxonomy and the class of a superscalar processor; 3. SIMD execution with a single program counter; 4. Array processors vs pipelined vector processors; 5. Vector register file; 6. Deeply pipelined vector functional units; 7. Interleaved memory banks; 8. Little or no data cache.

Show model answer

1. Data-level parallelism. The same operation applied independently to many data items, as in Z[i] = X[i] + Y[i]: no element waits for another. It is a property of the program, exploited by SIMD and vector hardware with one instruction per data operation instead of one per element; the fetch, decode and issue cost per element is divided by the vector length, so it costs far less energy than extracting the same parallelism as ILP.

2. Flynn's taxonomy and the superscalar. Flynn classifies machines by the number of instruction streams and data streams. A superscalar fetches several different instructions per cycle from one program counter: one instruction stream, each instruction on its own operands, so it is SISD and exploits ILP. The benefit of the classification is to separate ILP (more instructions per cycle from one stream), DLP (one instruction on many data, SIMD) and TLP (many streams, MIMD): Flynn counts streams, not width.

3. SIMD with a single PC. One control unit fetches each instruction once and broadcasts it to many processing elements, which execute it in lockstep on their own data. Benefits: one fetch and decode per operation instead of per PE (energy), compact code, no synchronisation between PEs. Drawback: divergent control flow must be handled by masking off elements.

4. Array vs vector processors. An array processor has N processing elements working side by side; an operation with latency L keeps them frozen, so it takes L × ⌈n/N⌉ cycles and the latency is exposed on every round, and the programmer thinks in PEs and local memories. A vector processor pipelines its units: the latency is paid once and then one result per cycle, L + n cycles (6 + 64 = 70), and the programmer thinks in vector registers. On long vectors one L-stage pipelined unit does the work of about L unpipelined ones.

5. Vector register file. Registers holding whole vectors (VMIPS: 8 registers of 64 × 64-bit elements, 16 read and 8 write ports through a crossbar), used register-to-register: each element is loaded once and reused by several instructions, and the pipelines are fed from registers without stalling on memory. It is compiler-controlled, unlike a cache: no tags, no misses.

6. Deeply pipelined functional units. Each unit accepts a new element every cycle. Element independence means no hazard checks between elements, so the pipelines can be deep and fast, and the start-up latency is amortised over the whole vector: about one result per cycle per unit. Cost: every instruction pays its start-up, so short vectors are inefficient.

7. Interleaved memory banks. Consecutive words are placed in consecutive banks; each bank is busy for several cycles after an access, but together they deliver one word per cycle to the vector load/store unit (Cray-1: 16 banks, 4-cycle bank busy time). Benefit: the bandwidth needed by streaming loads and stores; cost: many banks, and conflicts when a stride keeps hitting the same bank.

8. Little or no data cache. Vector accesses are predictable — the instruction states which words it needs — and streaming kernels use each element once, so a cache would add latency and area for little reuse. Bandwidth comes from the banks, latency is amortised by pipelining and buffered in the vector registers; a small scalar cache may serve the scalar side, which the vector side bypasses.

The 22 June 2026 paper asks its 8-point question in exactly this form, there on Tomasulo-style techniques, with five lines per item. For each item write the mechanism in one sentence, then the benefit, then a cost or a condition. Items 2 and 4 carry the two traps of the topic: "superscalar = SIMD" and the notes' "latency × vector length" for arrays.

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let's consider Flynn's taxonomy. In which class is an out-of-order superscalar processor that issues up to 4 instructions per clock cycle from a single thread? (Single answer.)

  • SISD One program counter, one instruction stream: the four instructions of a cycle are different instructions of the same stream. Parallelism across instructions is ILP, not a second stream.
  • SIMD SIMD is one instruction applied to many data elements; the superscalar issues several different instructions, each on its own operands.
  • MISD MISD would need several instruction streams on a single data stream; it is essentially hypothetical.
  • MIMD MIMD needs several instruction streams, one PC per thread or core; a single core running one thread has one.
Show model answer

Answer 1: SISD. Flynn's classification counts instruction and data streams, not issue width: a superscalar fetches all its instructions from one program counter.

The notes file it under "Practical clarification" (p. 388) because it is the classic confusion. A good motivation line: "streams, not width; superscalar = ILP within one stream".

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: in a vector processor with fully pipelined functional units, the latency of a functional unit is paid once for each element of the vector.

  • True That describes a single unpipelined unit (the array with N = 1) or a scalar loop: L × n. In a pipelined unit the elements overlap, L + n cycles.
  • False The latency (start-up) is paid once per vector instruction; after it, one element completes every cycle: 6 + 64 = 70 cycles for 64 elements with a 6-cycle adder.
Show model answer

False. The latency is paid once per vector instruction (the start-up); then the pipeline delivers one result per clock cycle, so n elements take about L + n cycles, not L × n.

The True/False form of the June 2025 paper's GPU quiz, on the idea behind every vector timing in lesson 31. If asked to motivate, give both counts with numbers.

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Which features allow a classic (Cray-style) vector processor to keep its functional units busy with little or no data cache? (Multiple answers.)

  • Interleaved memory banks that together deliver one word per clock cycle Each bank is busy for several cycles, but consecutive words sit in different banks: bandwidth without a cache.
  • Vector registers loaded and reused under compiler control They buffer whole vectors next to the units; the compiler decides what is loaded, so nothing misses.
  • Pipelined load/store and arithmetic units that pay the latency once per vector Memory latency is paid once per vector load, then one word per cycle: it is amortised, not hidden by a cache.
  • A large branch target buffer that predicts the next memory address A BTB predicts branch targets, not data addresses; a vector loop has no per-element branches to predict anyway.
  • The high temporal reuse of the vector elements Streaming kernels use each element once; it is the lack of reuse that makes a cache useless here.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 3. Vector accesses are predictable and stream each element once: interleaved banks supply the bandwidth, pipelined units pay the latency once per vector, and the vector registers act as a compiler-managed buffer.

The last option is the trap: caches live on reuse, and the reason vector machines drop them is that streams have none. Choosing it means confusing locality with data-level parallelism — the slip of the notes on p. 387.

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a vector processor whose FP add unit is fully pipelined with a start-up latency of 6 clock cycles. How many clock cycles does ADDVV.D V3, V1, V2 take on vectors of 64 elements, counting the start-up and then one result per cycle?

Show model answer
\[ T = \text{start-up} + n = 6 + 64 = 70 \ \text{cycles} \]

For comparison: one unpipelined adder would need 6 × 64 = 384 cycles of computation; an array of 64 unpipelined PEs about 6, with 64 adders instead of one.

The papers' convoy quizzes ignore the start-up (cycles = convoys × vector length, lesson 31); a question about one instruction with a given start-up counts it. Read which of the two the question wants.

Lesson 30 · Why Cray threw away the cache: Flynn, SIMD and the vector idea · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let's consider DAXPY on 64 elements. The scalar MIPS code has 2 set-up instructions and a loop body of 9 instructions executed once per element; the VMIPS code has 6 instructions. By what factor does vectorisation reduce the number of executed instructions?

Show model answer
\[ IC_{\text{MIPS}} = 2 + 9 \times 64 = 578 \] \[ \frac{IC_{\text{MIPS}}}{IC_{\text{VMIPS}}} = \frac{578}{6} = 96.3 \]

Write the instruction count before dividing: 9 × 64 = 576 forgets the set-up. If the question asks where the difference goes, 572 = 315 element instructions + 192 pointer and bound updates + 64 branches + 1 set-up.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider a vector processor architecture, as VMIPS shown in the figure (main memory, vector load/store unit, vector registers, scalar registers, and the FP add/subtract, FP multiply, FP divide, integer and logical vector units).

Present the main concepts. Present the main advantages of vector execution with respect to scalar execution, detailing the features of the vector architecture that provide the advantage.

Show model answer

Main concepts. A vector processor is a SIMD machine: one instruction operates on whole vectors held in vector registers. VMIPS is the scalar MIPS pipeline plus a vector unit: (1) a vector register file — 8 registers of 64 elements of 64 bits, with many read and write ports (16 and 8) through a crossbar — a compiler-controlled buffer between memory and the functional units; (2) deeply pipelined vector functional units (FP add/subtract, FP multiply, FP divide, integer, logical) that deliver one result per cycle after a start-up latency; (3) a vector load/store unit that streams one word per cycle from interleaved memory banks, with unit-stride, strided (LVWS) and indexed (gather/scatter) accesses; (4) scalar registers for addresses, strides and the scalar operands of instructions such as MULVS.D; (5) the vector-length register VLR (0 ≤ VLR ≤ MVL), used with strip mining for loops of any length, and the vector mask register for loops with an IF. Execution time is counted in convoys — instructions that run together, one per unit, dependent ones only with chaining — and chimes of about VL cycles: T ≈ convoys × VL.

Advantages and the feature that provides each. (a) Far fewer instructions fetched, decoded and issued: one vector instruction does the work of a whole loop (DAXPY on 64 elements: 6 instructions instead of about 578) — vector registers and vector instructions; less instruction bandwidth and energy. (b) Almost no loop overhead: index updates and loop branches disappear, one branch per strip of up to MVL elements instead of one per element, hence far fewer control hazards and mispredictions — vector-length semantics, VLR and strip mining. (c) Deep pipelines without stalls: the elements of a vector are independent, so no hazard checks inside an instruction, and dependences between vector instructions are checked once per vector, not once per element — pipelined vector units. (d) Overlap of dependent operations and of several units: chaining forwards element by element and several units run in one convoy (DAXPY in 3 chimes, 192 cycles, instead of 4 chimes, 256) — multiple units plus chaining. (e) Memory latency paid once per vector and a sustained bandwidth of one word per cycle: known access patterns let the load/store unit stream from interleaved banks, with little or no data cache — vector load/store unit plus banks. (f) Scalability without changing the code: L lanes process L elements per cycle, ⌈VL/L⌉ cycles per chime, same instructions. Limits: the gain needs data-level parallelism; masked elements still cost cycles; bad strides cause bank conflicts.

This is Question 2 of the February 2025 paper (notes, p. 578); lesson 30 redraws its VMIPS figure. The question has two halves: answer both. For the concepts, name the parts of the figure and what each does; for the advantages, tie each one to the feature that delivers it — "fewer instructions, because one vector instruction encodes a whole loop" — with a number where you have one (6 against 578 instructions, 192 against 256 cycles). One line on the limits shows that you know where vectors stop helping.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by vector processors: (1) Chaining; (2) Multiple lanes; (3) Vector length register and strip mining; (4) Vector mask registers; (5) Interleaved memory banks and strided accesses.

Show model answer

(1) Chaining: the result of a vector unit is forwarded element by element to the next unit, so a dependent vector instruction starts as soon as the first element of its operand is produced, instead of waiting until the whole vector is written back. Benefit: dependent instructions run overlapped in the same convoy, one chime instead of two per dependence (DAXPY: 4 convoys, 256 cycles → 3 convoys, 192 cycles). It is the vector analogue of forwarding; it does not remove structural hazards — one instruction per unit per convoy.

(2) Multiple lanes: each vector unit and the vector register file are split into L lanes; element i of every register lives in lane i mod L, so element-wise operations never cross lanes. Benefit: L elements per cycle, a chime of ⌈VL/L⌉ cycles (64 elements on 4 lanes: 16 cycles), with no change to the ISA or the code. Cost: register-file ports and memory bandwidth grow with L.

(3) VLR and strip mining: MVL is the hardware length of a vector register; the vector length register (0 ≤ VLR ≤ MVL) tells each instruction how many elements to process. Strip mining is the compiler transformation that cuts a loop of n iterations into ⌈n/MVL⌉ strips, the short one with VLR = n mod MVL (260 elements on MVL 64: 4 × 64 + 4). Benefit: one vector loop for any n, no access past the end of the arrays. Cost: a scalar strip loop with its overhead.

(4) Vector mask registers: one bit per element, set by a vector compare; instructions executed under the mask update only the elements whose bit is 1. Benefit: loops with an IF stay vectorizable (predication at vector level), no scalar fallback, any instruction can be masked. Cost: masked elements still take their cycle, so efficiency equals the fraction of active elements.

(5) Interleaved banks: consecutive words go to consecutive banks (bank = (address / word size) mod #banks), so a unit-stride access finds a free bank every cycle and the load/store unit sustains one word per cycle; strided loads (LVWS) and gather/scatter through index vectors handle matrix columns and sparse data. A stride visits #banks / gcd(stride, #banks) banks; if that is less than the bank busy time, accesses stall (8 banks, busy time 6, latency 12, 64 elements: stride 128 hits one bank and takes 391 cycles instead of 76). Fixes: pad rows (129 words), interchange the loops, choose a column-major layout.

The June 2026 paper asked eight such sub-points in one 8-point question, about five lines each; the 2024 and 2025 papers had 5-point open questions. A complete sub-point says what the mechanism does, what it buys — with a number — and what it costs.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following code executed by a Vector Processor with: Vector Register File composed of 32 vectors of 16 elements per 64 bits/element; Scalar FP Register File composed of 32 registers of 64 bits; One Load/Store Vector Unit with operation chaining and memory bandwidth 64 bits; One ADD/SUB Vector Unit with operation chaining; One MUL/DIV Vector Unit with operation chaining.

L.V      V1, R1       # Load vector from mem. addr. R1 into V1
L.V      V3, R2       # Load vector from mem. addr. R2 into V3
ADDVS.D  V2, V1, S1   # FP add vector V1 to scalar S1
MULVS.D  V2, V2, S2   # FP multiply vector V2 to scalar S2
ADDVS.D  V3, V3, S3   # FP add vector V3 to scalar S3
S.V      V2, R1       # Store vector V2 into memory addr. R1
S.V      V3, R2       # Store vector V3 into memory addr. R2

How many convoys? How many clock cycles to execute the code? Motivate your answer by completing the timing scheme (0, 1, … 15 for the first convoy).

  • 2 convoys; 32 clock cycles Four memory instructions share one load/store unit: two convoys would need two of them in each.
  • 3 convoys; 48 clock cycles A load phase, an arithmetic phase and a store phase: that puts two loads and two stores into single convoys, and both ADDVS.D on the one adder.
  • 4 convoys; 64 clock cycles The load/store unit forces four convoys and chaining packs the arithmetic into the first two: 4 × 16 = 64.
  • 5 convoys; 80 clock cycles The count without chaining: ADDVS.D V2 can no longer join L.V V1, nor MULVS.D join ADDVS.D.
Show model answer

Answer 3: 4 convoys; 64 clock cycles. Four memory instructions on one load/store unit need at least four convoys, and four are enough. A convoy lasts 16 cycles: 16 elements, one 64-bit element per cycle (bandwidth 64 bits), start-up ignored.

The same question was Question 6 of July 2024, with the options 3/48, 2/32 and 4/64. The motivation point is the scheme: four strips of 16 cycles. Say why the count cannot be lower — four memory instructions, one load/store unit — and why chaining matters: without it, 5 convoys and 80 cycles. Swapping the second and third convoy (S.V V2 before L.V V3) is equally correct, and so is the scheme packed strictly in listing order: L.V V1 alone in the first strip, then L.V V3 + ADDVS.D V2 + MULVS.D V2, then ADDVS.D V3 + S.V V2, then S.V V3 — the same 4 convoys.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following code executed by a Vector Processor with: Vector Register File composed of 32 vectors of 8 elements per 64 bits/element; Scalar FP Register File composed of 32 registers of 64 bits; One Load/Store Vector Unit with operation chaining and memory bandwidth 64 bits; One ADD/SUB Vector Unit with operation chaining; One MUL/DIV Vector Unit with operation chaining.

L.V      V1, RA
L.V      V2, RB
L.V      V3, RC
MULVS.D  V1, V1, F1
MULVS.D  V2, V2, F2
MULVS.D  V3, V3, F3
ADDVV.D  V2, V2, V1
ADDVV.D  V3, V2, V3
S.V      V3, RD

How many convoys? How many clock cycles to execute the code? Motivate your answer by completing the table (Load/Store Vector Unit, Mul/Div Vector Unit, Add/Sub Vector Unit; 1^ convoy: L.V V1, RA).

  • 2 convoys; 16 clock cycles Four memory instructions on one load/store unit need at least four convoys.
  • 3 convoys; 24 clock cycles Three loads and a store cannot fit in three convoys on one load/store unit.
  • 4 convoys; 32 clock cycles Each multiply chains on its load and each add on its multiply; the store needs a fourth convoy: 4 × 8 = 32.
  • 5 convoys; 40 clock cycles No reading of the rules gives five: chaining with the packing of move 3 reaches the floor of four, and both the count without chaining and the strict listing-order packing give six.
  • 6 convoys; 48 clock cycles The count without chaining — and also the count if you pack strictly in listing order, so that MULVS.D V1 waits behind L.V V2 and L.V V3. With chaining and the packing of move 3, four convoys suffice.
Show model answer

Answer 3: 4 convoys; 4 × 8 = 32 clock cycles (8 elements, one 64-bit element per cycle).

The single load/store unit serialises the three loads and the store; with chaining each multiply joins the convoy of its load, and each add the convoy of its multiply, its other operand being complete (V1 after the 1^ convoy, V2 after the 2^).

The paper fills in L.V V1, RA and leaves six rows: two stay empty here. MULVS.D V1 joins the first convoy although two loads stand before it in the listing, because it does not depend on them — the packing of move 3. Answer 5, 6 convoys and 48 cycles, is the count without chaining and also the count of a packing that keeps strict listing order. No official key exists for this paper: under the table, write the one-line assumption that independent instructions are grouped regardless of listing order.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following code: for (i=0; i<260; i++) Y[i] = X[i] + Y[i]; Which code transformation can be applied to be executed by the VMIPS processor with a Vector Register File composed of 8 registers of 64 elements and 64 bits/element? Motivate your answer.

  • Trace scheduling A VLIW compiler technique that schedules scalar instructions across branches; this loop body has no branch, and its problem is the vector length.
  • Software pipelining It overlaps iterations of a scalar loop on a statically scheduled machine; it does not make 260 elements fit into 64-element registers.
  • Strip mining 260 > 64, so the loop is cut into strips of at most 64 elements, with the vector length register set for each strip.
  • Apply a mask register A mask handles a conditional statement inside a vector loop; there is none here.
Show model answer

Strip mining. MVL = 64 < n = 260, so the loop is split into ⌈260/64⌉ = 5 strips: four strips of 64 elements and one of 260 mod 64 = 4 elements (first, in Hennessy and Patterson's code, or last), setting VLR before each strip. Each strip executes L.V V1, RX; L.V V2, RY; ADDVV.D V3, V1, V2; S.V V3, RY. A mask register is for an IF inside the loop, which is absent; trace scheduling and software pipelining are VLIW compiler techniques for scheduling scalar instructions.

Question 5 of July 2024; June 2026 asked it again with 16 registers of 32 elements (260 = 8 × 32 + 4). The motivation point is the arithmetic, plus one sentence on why each distractor does not fit.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following code executed by a Vector Processor with: Vector Register File composed of 32 vectors of 16 elements per 32 bits/element; Scalar FP Register File composed of 32 registers of 32 bits; One Load/Store Vector Unit with memory bandwidth 32 bits; One Add/Sub Vector Unit; One Mul/Div Vector Unit. None of the three units has operation chaining.

L.V      V1, RX
ADDVS.D  V1, V1, F0
MULV.D   V2, V1, V1
DIVVS.D  V2, V2, F0
L.V      V3, RY
ADDVV.D  V3, V2, V3
S.V      V2, RZ
S.V      V3, RW

How many clock cycles does the code take?

Show model answer

Without chaining every true dependence ends a convoy, and the chain L.V V1 → ADDVS.D V1 → MULV.D V2 → DIVVS.D V2 → ADDVV.D V3 → S.V V3 has six instructions: 6 convoys. A convoy lasts 16 cycles (16 elements, one 32-bit element per cycle): 6 × 16 = 96 clock cycles.

With chaining, as in the January 2026 paper: 4 convoys, 64 cycles — L.V V1 + ADDVS.D + MULV.D; L.V V3 + DIVVS.D + ADDVV.D; S.V V2; S.V V3.

The code is the January 2026 quiz (notes, p. 501) with chaining removed. Without chaining the floor from the load/store unit (4) is not the answer: the chain of six dependent instructions is longer. Note that the elements are 32 bits here and the bandwidth 32 bits: still one element per cycle.

Lesson 31 · VMIPS and the art of counting convoys: chaining, lanes, lengths, masks and banks · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

A vector processor has MVL = 64, 4 lanes, one Load/Store Vector Unit that moves 4 elements per cycle, one MUL/DIV and one ADD/SUB Vector Unit, all with operation chaining. The DAXPY loop Y[i] = a * X[i] + Y[i] runs on n = 1000 elements with strip mining; each strip executes L.V, MULVS.D, L.V, ADDVV.D, S.V. Ignoring start-up and the scalar loop overhead, how many clock cycles does the loop take?

Show model answer

Each strip: 3 convoys (L.V + MULVS.D; L.V + ADDVV.D; S.V). Strips: ⌈1000/64⌉ = 16 — 15 of 64 elements and one of 1000 mod 64 = 40. With 4 lanes a chime takes ⌈VL/4⌉ cycles: 16 for a full strip, 10 for the short one.

\[ T = 15 \times 3 \times 16 + 3 \times 10 \] \[ T = 720 + 30 = 750 \]

750 clock cycles, 0.75 per element.

Two traps: forgetting the short strip (15 × 48 = 720) and charging it a full chime (16 × 48 = 768). Lanes divide the chime, strips multiply the convoys.

Lesson 32 · SIMD today: packed extensions, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs

Lesson 32 · SIMD today: packed extensions, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs · question 1 of 5 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used by data-parallel architectures:

  1. SIMD ISA extensions (e.g. SSE/AVX)
  2. Vector length register (VLR) with strip mining
  3. Vector mask registers
  4. Multithreaded SIMD execution in GP-GPUs (warps)
  5. Separate host and device memories in the GP-GPU programming model
  6. Address coalescing in GP-GPUs
  7. Tensor cores
  8. Systolic arrays in Neural Processing Units
Show model answer

  1. A scalar ISA extended with instructions that apply one operation to the elements packed in a wide register (128, 256 or 512 bits: 2, 4 or 8 doubles). Benefits: several data operations per fetched and decoded instruction; cheap to add as extra datapaths in the existing pipeline; little extra state, fed by the caches; available in every CPU, so compilers and libraries use it for media and ML kernels. Limits: the width is fixed in the opcode, so a wider register needs new instructions and recompiled code, leftovers need a scalar remainder loop, and strided/sparse access and masks came late (gather with AVX2, scatter and masks with AVX-512).

  2. VLR holds the number of elements, 0 ≤ VLR ≤ MVL, that every vector instruction processes; MVL is the hardware length of a vector register. Strip mining (a compiler transformation) splits a loop of n iterations into ⌈n/MVL⌉ strips: full strips with VLR = MVL and one strip of n mod MVL. Benefits: one loop handles any n with no remainder loop, nothing beyond the arrays is touched, and the same binary uses more lanes or a longer MVL if the loop takes its strip length from the hardware (ARM SVE, RISC-V V).

  3. A mask register holds one bit per element; a vector compare sets it, and the following vector instructions update only the elements whose bit is 1, leaving the others unchanged (predication). Benefits: loops with if statements stay vectorised, with no branch per element, and masked elements raise no exceptions. Cost: masked elements still use their slot, so an if-else executes both bodies. GPUs do the same per thread (branch divergence).

  4. A GP-GPU streaming multiprocessor is a multithreaded SIMD processor. Threads are grouped into warps of 32; one instruction is fetched per warp and executed in lockstep on the SIMD lanes for all 32 threads, each thread with private registers (SIMT). Many warps are resident; every cycle the warp scheduler issues from a ready one, and since all their registers stay in a large register file, switching costs nothing. Benefits: DRAM latency of hundreds of cycles is hidden by the other warps instead of by caches or out-of-order logic; the programmer writes sequential code for one element; the same kernel scales over any number of SMs.

  5. The CPU (host) and the GPU (device) have separate memory address spaces, physically two DRAMs linked by PCIe/NVLink. The host allocates device memory (cudaMalloc), copies the inputs (cudaMemcpy), launches the kernel and copies the results back. Benefit: the device memory (GDDR/HBM) is built for bandwidth and serves the kernel at full speed. Cost: every transfer crosses the link, so a kernel must do enough work per byte copied; unified memory only automates the migration.

  6. Each thread of a warp computes its own address; the coalescing unit merges the 32 addresses of a warp's load or store into as few wide memory transactions as possible. Consecutive threads touching consecutive words cost one or two transactions — the run-time equivalent of a unit-stride L.V — while column or scattered accesses cost up to 32. Benefit: memory bandwidth is used efficiently without the program stating the stride; it rewards unit-stride data layouts.

  7. A tensor core is a functional unit in each SM that computes D = A × B + C on small matrices as one operation (Volta: 4 × 4, A and B in FP16, accumulation in FP16 or FP32, 64 FMAs per clock), used through a warp-level matrix instruction. Benefits: one instruction for 64 FMAs; each input reused 4 times inside the unit, so less register traffic per flop; reduced precision halves data movement — up to 125 TFLOPS on a V100. Only matrix-shaped, high-intensity work (dense linear algebra, deep learning) gains.

  8. A systolic array is a grid of multiply-accumulate cells in which data moves between neighbours in lockstep, so each operand fetched from memory is used by a whole row or column. In the TPU (256 × 256 8-bit MACs, weight-stationary) the weights stay in the cells, activations flow right and partial sums flow down into accumulators: one activation read feeds 256 MACs. Benefits: very high throughput (92 TOPS) at low energy per operation, simple control, software-managed on-chip memory instead of caches. Limits: only dense matrix work, and a fill/drain latency per stream.

The June 2026 paper asked exactly this form for superscalar techniques: eight sub-points, five lines each. For every point write the mechanism (what the hardware or the compiler does), the benefit, and one cost. Points 2–3 are the notes' own material (lesson 31); points 4–8 are the background of this lesson, which the topic list ("Intro to GP-GPUs; Intro to Tensor Cores", "Intro to Neural Processing Units") makes examinable but the notes do not cover.

Lesson 32 · SIMD today: packed extensions, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs · question 2 of 5 · open in the lesson

Compare a vector processor (VMIPS-like) and the streaming multiprocessor of a GP-GPU. For each one describe: the main concepts; how the length of a loop is handled; how a conditional statement inside the loop is handled; how memory latency is hidden; the main benefits; the main drawbacks.

Show model answer
Vector processor (VMIPS)GP-GPU streaming multiprocessor
Main conceptsSIMD with one PC; vector registers of MVL = 64 elements; deeply pipelined vector FUs in lanes with chaining; one vector load/store unit; a scalar control processor. One instruction = a whole vector.Multithreaded SIMD: threads grouped in warps of 32; one instruction per warp, executed in lockstep for its 32 threads, each with private registers (SIMT); dozens of resident warps, a warp scheduler issues from a ready one each cycle; a host CPU and a separate device memory.
Loop lengthVLR (≤ MVL) and strip mining: ⌈n/MVL⌉ strips.A grid of n threads in blocks; the tail is masked off by if (i < n); no VLR.
ConditionalsA vector compare sets the mask register; the instructions execute under the mask.Branch divergence: the warp executes both paths, each under a per-thread mask.
Memory latencyPaid once per vector, then one element per cycle from interleaved banks; strided and gather/scatter loads; little or no data cache.Hidden by switching among resident warps, whose registers stay in the register file; coalescing merges a warp's accesses; small caches save bandwidth.

The forms of July 2024 (fine-grained vs simultaneous multithreading) and June 2025 (a dual-core chip) were tables with blank lines: one precise line per cell, naming the mechanism and not only the term. The loop-length, conditional and latency rows are the mapping of this lesson — VLR against a masked tail, mask register against divergence, latency amortised by long vectors against latency hidden by warps.

Lesson 32 · SIMD today: packed extensions, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs · question 3 of 5 · open in the lesson

In the GP-GPU programming model, the CPU (host) and GPU (device) have separate memory address spaces. (True/False)

  • True The host allocates device memory, copies the data in with cudaMemcpy, launches the kernel and copies the results back: two memories, two address spaces.
  • False Unified memory only migrates pages between the two memories automatically; the basic model and the hardware keep two separate memories.
Show model answer

True. The device has its own DRAM; pointers to device memory are valid only on the device, and data moves explicitly (cudaMalloc, cudaMemcpy host to device, kernel launch, cudaMemcpy device to host).

Question 3 of the quizzes of 10 June 2025, word for word — the only GPU question of the three papers. The wrong answer usually comes from unified memory, which automates the copies but does not merge the memories.

Lesson 32 · SIMD today: packed extensions, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs · question 4 of 5 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the loop for (i=0; i<260; i++) Y[i] = X[i] + Y[i]; compiled for a processor with the AVX-512 SIMD extension (512-bit registers, 64-bit elements), without masked instructions: the leftover elements are handled by a scalar remainder loop. How many SIMD add instructions and how many scalar add instructions are executed? (SINGLE ANSWER)

  • Answer 1: 32 SIMD adds and 4 scalar adds 8 doubles per register: 260 = 32 × 8 + 4.
  • Answer 2: 33 SIMD adds and 0 scalar adds That needs a masked last add, which the question excludes.
  • Answer 3: 65 SIMD adds and 0 scalar adds That is AVX, with 4 doubles per 256-bit register: 260 = 65 × 4.
  • Answer 4: 5 SIMD adds and 0 scalar adds That is the number of strips on VMIPS with MVL = 64, a vector machine, not packed SIMD.
  • Answer 5: 8 SIMD adds and 4 scalar adds 8 is the width of the register, not the number of trips of the loop.
Show model answer

Answer 1. A 512-bit register holds 8 doubles, and 260 = 32 × 8 + 4: the SIMD loop runs 32 times, and the 4 leftover elements go to the scalar remainder loop. With the k-mask registers of AVX-512 the 4 leftovers would take one masked add (33 instructions); on a vector machine the loop is strip-mined with VLR instead (5 strips with MVL = 64).

The same loop as the strip-mining quiz of the papers, on a different machine. The distractors are the counts of the neighbouring machines — 65 for AVX, 33 for the masked version, 5 for the VMIPS strips — in the style the papers build their options.

Lesson 32 · SIMD today: packed extensions, GPUs, tensor cores and NPUs · question 5 of 5 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about the streaming multiprocessor of a GP-GPU are true? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • A warp executes one instruction for its 32 threads in lockstep, each thread with its own registers. SIMT: a warp instruction is a vector instruction of length 32 with per-lane registers.
  • The latency of a DRAM access is hidden mainly by switching to another ready warp. Fine-grained multithreading at no switch cost: the registers of all resident warps stay in the register file.
  • The tail of a loop is handled with a vector length register, as in VMIPS. There is no VLR: the threads with i ≥ n are masked off by the if of the kernel.
  • When the threads of a warp take different paths of an if, both paths are executed, each under a mask. Branch divergence: the vector mask of lesson 31, managed per thread by the hardware.
  • A tensor core performs one fused multiply-add per lane per clock. A tensor core computes D = A × B + C on 4 × 4 matrices: 64 FMAs per clock as one operation.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 4. A warp is one instruction stream for 32 threads with private registers; latency is hidden by switching warps; divergent paths run one after the other under masks. A GPU has no VLR (the tail is masked), and a tensor core is a matrix unit, not a wider FMA.

The two false options are the traps: carrying the VLR over from the vector machine, and imagining the tensor core as a faster ordinary lane.

Module 8 · Multiprocessors and cache coherence

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following:

  1. Multiprocessor (tightly coupled, MIMD)
  2. Multicore and manycore processors
  3. Symmetric multiprocessors (SMP)
  4. Large-scale distributed shared-memory multiprocessors
  5. Shared-memory and message-passing communication
  6. Single-bus interconnection
  7. Interconnection networks
  8. Bisection bandwidth
Show model answer

1. A multiprocessor is a computer system with tightly coupled processors coordinated by a single operating system, typically offering a shared address space. Each processor runs its own instruction stream on its own data (MIMD), so the machine exploits thread-level parallelism. Benefits: high throughput on many threads or processes, performance that grows with the number of processors, tolerance of some faults through redundancy, one design from embedded systems to servers. Unlike a cluster, it has one OS and one address space.

2. A multicore puts several cores on the same chip; beyond about 32 cores it is called a manycore. A multicore chip is a small SMP on a chip: private L1/L2 caches, usually a shared last-level cache, uniform access to memory, snooping coherence. Benefit: it turns the transistor budget into throughput through TLP — the industry's answer to the ILP and frequency (power) walls — instead of a wider or faster single core.

3. In an SMP (centralized shared memory) all processors share one physical memory with the same access time for everyone (UMA), usually through a bus; cache coherence is kept by snooping. Benefits: simple, symmetric view for the programmer and the OS, cheap for a few processors. Limit: the shared bus and memory saturate, so SMPs typically stay at or below about 32 processors.

4. In a distributed shared-memory (DSM) multiprocessor the memory is physically distributed among the nodes (processor + caches + local memory module) connected by an interconnection network, but forms one shared address space. Access time is non-uniform (NUMA): local is fast, remote is slower. Benefits: it scales beyond about 32 processors because memory bandwidth grows with the nodes and there is no single memory bottleneck, and it is still programmed with loads and stores. Costs: data placement matters, coherence needs directories, the network is complex.

5. With shared memory, processors communicate implicitly by loads and stores to a common address space: natural to program, but it needs coherence hardware and explicit synchronization (locks, barriers). With message passing, each processor has a private address space and communicates explicitly with send and receive: simpler hardware, explicit control of data movement, harder programming. Both models are Turing complete and each can implement the other, so the choice affects performance, programmability and cost, not what can be computed.

6. All processors, the memory and the I/O share one bus; every memory access (cache miss) is a bus transaction, one at a time. Benefits: cheap, simple, and a broadcast medium — every cache sees every transaction, which snooping coherence needs. Drawback: saturation. If each processor alone uses a fraction d of the bus, throughput grows linearly and then flattens near 1/d processors (commercial systems maxed out at about 36); beyond that, adding a processor only lengthens the queue and slows every access.

7. An interconnection network connects the nodes with many point-to-point links and switches (ring, 2-D mesh, torus, hypercube, crossbar, fat tree), so many transfers proceed in parallel and the bandwidth grows with the system. Benefits: it removes the shared bus from the critical path and scales to hundreds or thousands of processors. Costs: switches and links are expensive, and multiple hops add latency. Networks are compared by total and bisection bandwidth, diameter and node degree.

8. Bisection bandwidth is the minimum, over all cuts that split the network into two halves with equal numbers of nodes, of the total bandwidth of the links crossing the cut. It is the worst case, when half of the nodes communicate with the other half, and it bounds global traffic, while the total bandwidth (M × b, the sum of all links) is the best case for local traffic. Examples: bus b, ring 2b whatever P, 2-D mesh √P·b, hypercube (P/2)·b.

The 2026 paper asks its 8-point question in this form, "Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following …", with five lines for each sub-point; multiprocessors and multicores are an item of the course's topic list. What earns the points: the defining words (tightly coupled, single OS, shared address space, MIMD; UMA and NUMA; implicit versus explicit communication; one transaction at a time; best case versus worst case) and a cost next to every benefit. Sub-point 6 is stronger with the 1/d argument than with the bare number 36.

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider a multiprocessor with P = 16 nodes whose interconnection links each have a bandwidth b = 1 GB/s. Complete the following table, then explain which topology you would choose if the 16 processors communicate all-to-all, and why.

TopologyLinksTotal BWBisection BWDiameterPorts per node
Single bus
Ring
2-D mesh (4 × 4)
Hypercube (N = 4)
Fully connected
Show model answer
TopologyNumber of linksTotal bandwidthBisection bandwidthDiameterPorts per node
Single bus1b = 1 GB/sb = 1 GB/s1 (shared)1
RingP = 16P·b = 16 GB/s2b = 2 GB/sP/2 = 82
2-D mesh (4 × 4)2N(N − 1) = 2424 GB/sN·b = 4 GB/s2(N − 1) = 62 to 4
Hypercube (N = 4)NP/2 = 3232 GB/s(P/2)·b = 8 GB/sN = 44
Fully connectedP(P − 1)/2 = 120120 GB/s(P/2)²·b = 64 GB/s115

All-to-all traffic crosses the machine, so it is bounded by the bisection bandwidth, not by the total. The fully connected network has the largest bisection (64 GB/s) and diameter 1, but needs 120 links and 15 ports per node. The hypercube gives 8 GB/s of bisection, so every node can send across the cut at its full link speed (8 GB/s for 8 senders), with only 32 links, 4 ports per node and a diameter of 4: the usual engineering choice at this size. The mesh is cheaper but has half the bisection and diameter 6; the ring and the bus would be throttled to 2 and 1 GB/s.

An exercise in the style of the papers' tables. The two traps: the hypercube bisection (8, not the 6 of the notes' eq. 98) and confusing the total with the bisection when the question is about global traffic. Write the formula before the number in each cell — a correct formula with a slip in the arithmetic is still worth most of the cell.

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

A 32-processor distributed shared-memory multiprocessor runs at a clock frequency of 4 GHz. Its base CPI, when every memory reference is satisfied locally, is 0.5. A reference to remote memory takes 100 ns and stalls the processor. If 0.2% of the instructions make a remote reference, what is the CPI of the machine?

Show model answer
\[ T_{CLK} = \frac{1}{4\ \text{GHz}} = 0.25\ \text{ns} \] \[ \text{remote cost} = \frac{100}{0.25} = 400 \text{ cycles} \] \[ CPI = 0.5 + 0.002 \times 400 = 1.3 \]

Without remote references the machine would be 1.3 / 0.5 = 2.6 times faster.

The example comes from Hennessy & Patterson. The marks are in converting the 100 ns into clock cycles (400, not 100 or 0.4) and in weighting the stall by the fraction of instructions that pay it: 0.2% is 0.002, not 0.2.

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

You want a speedup of 80 on a multiprocessor with 100 processors. According to Amdahl's law, what is the largest fraction of the original execution time that may remain sequential? Give the answer in percent.

Show model answer
\[ 80 = \frac{1}{(1-F) + F/100} \] \[ 1 - 0.99\,F = 0.0125 \] \[ F = 0.99747, \quad 1 - F \approx 0.25\% \]

The formula sheet's Amdahl's law with S = P. The answer asked for is the sequential fraction 1 − F, not F: answering 99.75% is the classic lost point. The form F = (1 − 1/Speedup)/(1 − 1/S) from lesson 3 gives the same number in one line: 0.9875 / 0.99.

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

Which of the following statements about the bisection bandwidth of an interconnection network are true? (Multiple answers)

  • It is the sum of the bandwidths of all the links of the network. That is the total network bandwidth, M × b: the best case, not the worst.
  • It is the minimum bandwidth across any cut that divides the nodes into two equal halves. This is the definition: the narrowest balanced cut decides.
  • For a ring it is 2b regardless of the number of nodes. The cheapest balanced cut, into two arcs, crosses the loop exactly twice, whatever P.
  • For a single bus it equals the total network bandwidth. The bus is one link: total and bisection are both b.
  • For a √P × √P mesh it grows linearly with P. It grows as √P: the cut is one column of N = √P links.
Show model answer

Answers 2, 3 and 4. Answer 1 describes the total bandwidth; the mesh bisection is N·b = √P·b, which grows as the square root of P.

The family of statements mixes the two metrics on purpose. Check each against the ring and the bus, the two cases you can draw in a second.

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

A hypercube interconnects P = 64 nodes with links of bandwidth b = 1 GB/s. What is its bisection bandwidth? (Single answer)

  • 10 GB/s That is 2(N − 1)·b with N = 6, the slip of the notes' eq. (98). The cut severs one link per pair of twins.
  • 12 GB/s That is 2N·b, a formula with no derivation behind it. Count instead: the cut along the top address bit severs one link per pair of twins, 32 links.
  • 32 GB/s (P/2)·b = 2^(N−1)·b: cutting along the top address bit severs the 32 links that join the two 5-cubes.
  • 64 GB/s That would be one link per node crossing; each crossing link joins two nodes, so it is P/2, not P.
  • 192 GB/s That is the total bandwidth, N·P/2·b = 6 · 32.
Show model answer

Answer 3: N = log₂64 = 6, bisection = 2^(N−1)·b = (P/2)·b = 32 GB/s; the total is N·P/2·b = 192 GB/s.

The distractors are the notes' wrong formula (10), the total (192) and a double count (64). The quickest route is the doubling picture: a 6-cube is two 5-cubes joined by 32 links.

Lesson 33 · One machine, many brains: multiprocessors, buses and topologies · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

In a single-bus multiprocessor, each processor running alone keeps the bus busy 4% of the time. True or false: however many processors are attached, the machine cannot deliver more than the throughput of 25 such processors running alone.

  • True The bus can be busy at most 100% of the time, so it carries the traffic of at most 1 / 0.04 = 25 processors; past that, extra processors only queue.
  • False Adding processors adds demand, not bus capacity: the throughput flattens at 1/d = 25 processors' worth.
Show model answer

True. Throughput ≤ bus capacity / demand per processor = 1 / 0.04 = 25 processors' worth; near that point the bus is saturated and every further processor only lengthens the queue for the bus.

The ceiling is capacity divided by demand, the same argument that gives the notes' ≈ 36. Halve the traffic per processor (bigger caches) and the ceiling doubles.

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) What type of multiprocessor architecture does the following picture represent?

  • Answer 1: Centralized Memory; Single Logically Shared Address Space There is no central memory: every node has its own module, MM0 … MM3.
  • Answer 2: Centralized Memory; Message Passing Architectures Wrong on both axes: the memory is in the nodes, and the labels say that every processor addresses every module.
  • Answer 3: Distributed Memory; Single Logically Shared Address Space One memory per node (distributed), each addressable by P0–P3 (one shared address space): a DSM, also called NUMA.
  • Answer 4: Distributed Memory; Message Passing Architectures The memory is distributed, but with message passing each module would answer to its own processor only; here every module lists P0, P1, P2, P3.
Show model answer

Answer 3. Each node contains its own processor, private cache and memory module, so the memory is physically distributed and there is no single central memory: Answers 1 and 2 are wrong. Each memory module is annotated with P0, P1, P2, P3: every processor can access every module with ordinary loads and stores, so the memories form a single logically shared address space and Answer 4 is wrong. This is a Distributed Shared Memory (DSM) architecture, also known as NUMA.

This is the quiz of the 8 January 2026 paper as the notes reproduce it (p. 505), options verbatim; the notes' answer, 3, is correct. Read the picture in two moves: count the memories for the column, read the labels for the row. If the paper asks you to motivate, one sentence per axis is the whole answer.

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following organizations and models used in multiprocessors: 1. Shared address space; 2. Private address spaces (message passing); 3. Centralized shared-memory multiprocessors (UMA / SMP); 4. Distributed shared-memory multiprocessors (NUMA / DSM); 5. Home node of a block in a DSM; 6. Small-scale multicores; 7. Distributed-memory clusters; 8. Data-parallel (SPMD) programming model. (Five lines each.)

Show model answer

1. All processors see one logical memory: an address names the same location for every processor, any processor can load or store any address, and communication is implicit (one processor stores, another loads). Programmed with threads (Pthreads, OpenMP). Benefits: easy to program, handles irregular and dynamic sharing, low latency for small items, caches reduce remote traffic automatically. Costs: complex hardware (cache coherence), synchronization with locks and barriers, little control of data placement.

2. Each processor has its own address space: the same address names different locations on different processors, no load or store crosses processors, and data moves by explicit send/receive (MPI_Send / MPI_Recv). Benefits: simple hardware with no coherence protocol, the programmer sees when and how much data moves, every send/receive synchronizes, explicit placement, fault isolation. Costs: harder programming, high overhead for fine-grain sharing, data decomposed by hand.

3. One centralized physical memory shared by all processors, with the same latency and bandwidth whichever processor accesses it (symmetric). Processors with private caches, possibly a shared L3, reach it through a bus or crossbar; coherence by snooping. Benefits: the simplest programming (no data placement), sequential code ports easily, a uniform view for the OS. Limit: the single memory and interconnect saturate under contention, about 32 processors in classic designs.

4. Memory physically distributed: each node is processor(s) + caches + a local memory module, and the nodes are joined by a scalable interconnection network. The union of the local memories is one global address space, so programs still use loads and stores. The access time depends on whether the address is in the local module (fast) or in a remote node (slower). Benefits: scales to hundreds of cores, aggregate memory bandwidth grows with the nodes. Costs: performance depends on data placement; a scalable network and directory-based coherence are needed.

5. The home node of a block is the node whose main memory holds it, fixed by the physical address (if every node owns 4 KiB, N2 owns 0x2000–0x2FFF and the block of 0x2200 is homed in N2). In a directory protocol it also keeps the block's directory entry (state and sharer bits), stored with the block in its memory, so every request for the block goes through it. The local node is the node that issues the request. Benefit: the data and its coherence information are found from the address alone, without broadcasts.

6. A multicore chip with a few cores (up to about 8, sometimes 16) is an SMP on a chip: identical cores, uniform access to one main memory, one shared-memory machine for the OS. Private L1 and L2 caches per core hold the working sets and reduce contention; a shared L3 cuts DRAM traffic and is where shared data meets; coherence by snooping on the shared bus. Beyond about 8 cores the L3 is split into banks and the bus becomes a network-on-chip (mesh, ring, crossbar), so several requests are served in parallel.

7. A set of independent computers, each with its own processors, private memory, local disk and OS instance, connected by a network (Ethernet, InfiniBand); no shared address space, communication by explicit message passing with MPI. Benefits: scales to tens of thousands of nodes, memory grows linearly with the nodes, commodity hardware is cheap, one node can fail without crashing the system. Costs: harder programming (partition the data, orchestrate the messages), network latency much higher and bandwidth lower than local DRAM, synchronization at every send/receive.

8. All processors run the same program, each on its own portion of the data (Single Program, Multiple Data): adding two 1000-element vectors, Proc 0 takes indices 0–249, …, Proc 3 750–999. Execution proceeds in bulk-synchronous phases: local computation, global communication or a barrier, next phase (Valiant's BSP model). Benefits: one simple program, high scalability for large and evenly split data, efficient on GPUs and vector units. Costs: load imbalance and stragglers stall everyone at the barrier; it suits regular array computations, not trees or graphs.

The form is the June 2026 question: eight sub-points, about five lines each. Give three things in each: what it is, one concrete detail (a number, an example, an API) and the benefit with its cost. Sub-points 3 and 4 are best written as a pair, uniform against non-uniform latency; sub-point 5 is the July 2024 question "What is the definition of home node?".

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models · question 3 of 6 · open in the lesson

Consider a distributed shared memory system with 4 nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3); each node has a processor Pi, a cache Ci and a main memory MMi, and the nodes are connected by an interconnection network. Each node owns 4 KiB of the physical addresses: N0 from 0x0000 to 0x0FFF, N1 from 0x1000 to 0x1FFF, N2 from 0x2000 to 0x2FFF, N3 from 0x3000 to 0x3FFF. Processor P0 executes LD F2, 0(R1) with R1 = 0x2200 and misses in C0; no other cache holds a copy of the block.

1. What is the definition of home node? Which node is the home node of this access? 2. Which node is the local node? 3. Describe the path of the request and of the data. 4. What changes, and what does not change, if R1 = 0x0200?

Show model answer

1. The home node of a block is the node whose main memory holds the block; it is fixed by the physical address, and in a directory protocol it also keeps the block's directory entry, so every request for the block goes through it. 0x2200 lies in 0x2000–0x2FFF: the home node is N2.

2. The local node is the node whose processor issues the request: N0.

3. C0 misses; the request leaves N0 and the interconnection network routes it, by address, to N2; MM2 supplies the block (offset 0x200 in its range); the data travels back to N0, is written into C0, and the load completes into F2. It is a remote access: two network crossings and a longer latency than a local access.

4. With 0x0200 the home is N0 itself: home and local coincide, and the miss is served by MM0 without using the network, with the shorter local latency. The program does not change — the same instruction in the same address space; only the latency changes.

Sub-question 1 is the July 2024 question word for word. In the directory exercises a third role appears, the remote node: a node that holds a cached copy of the block (lesson 37). Here no other cache holds a copy, so there is none; do not call N2 "remote" in that exercise vocabulary.

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let us consider a distributed shared-memory multiprocessor with 4 nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3) and a clock frequency of 1 GHz. With all memory references hitting in the caches, the CPI is 1. 0.5% of the instructions miss in the cache, and the processor stalls until the miss is served: 80 ns when the home node of the block is the local node, 240 ns when it is another node. Case A: the data of the program are interleaved evenly over the four nodes. Case B: the operating system places the data so that 75% of the misses find their block in the local node. How much is the speed up of case B over case A in terms of CPI?

Show model answer

With \(T_{CLK} = 1\) ns, a local miss costs 80 clock cycles and a remote miss 240. Case A: a quarter of the blocks are homed in the local node, so the average miss penalty and the CPI are

\[ 0.25 \times 80 + 0.75 \times 240 = 200 \] \[ CPI_A = 1 + 0.005 \times 200 = 2 \]

Case B:

\[ 0.75 \times 80 + 0.25 \times 240 = 120 \] \[ CPI_B = 1 + 0.005 \times 120 = 1.6 \]

Speed up in terms of CPI:

\[ \frac{CPI_A}{CPI_B} = \frac{2}{1.6} = 1.25 \]

The same program on the same machine runs 25% faster only because of where its data are placed.

Not a past-paper exercise: it is the formula sheet's CPI with stalls (CPI = 1 + stall cycles per instruction) with the average miss latency of section 3 as the penalty, asked the way the 2025 performance exercise and the 2026 VLIW exercise ("How much is the speed up in terms of CPI?") ask. The two traps: 0.5% is 0.005, and with four nodes an even spread makes only a quarter of the misses local, not half. Lesson 33 works Hennessy & Patterson's version: 0.2% of the instructions make a remote reference of 400 cycles, and a CPI of 0.5 becomes 1.3.

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) Which of the following statements about a distributed shared-memory (DSM, cc-NUMA) multiprocessor are true?

  • Answer 1: Any processor can access any memory location with ordinary load and store instructions. The single global address space: the hardware routes the access to the home node.
  • Answer 2: Processors on different nodes must exchange explicit messages (send/receive) to share data. That is a cluster. In a DSM a load or a store suffices.
  • Answer 3: The access time depends on whether the address belongs to the local memory module or to a remote node. Non-uniform memory access: local inside the node, remote across the network.
  • Answer 4: Each node runs its own instance of the operating system. A DSM is one machine with one OS; an OS instance per node is the mark of a cluster.
  • Answer 5: Cache coherence is typically maintained by a directory stored alongside each memory block. Snooping a network does not scale; the directory notifies only the caches that hold a copy (p. 435).
Show model answer

Answers 1, 3 and 5. A DSM has physically distributed memory, a single logically shared address space reached by loads and stores, non-uniform latency, and directory-based coherence. Answers 2 and 4 describe a distributed-memory cluster.

A multiple-answer quiz as in June 2026. The two wrong options are the cluster's features; sort each option by asking "load/store or send/receive?".

Lesson 34 · Where does the memory live? UMA, NUMA, DSM, clusters and programming models · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

(True/False) In a distributed-memory cluster programmed with MPI, a process can read a variable of a process running on another node with an ordinary load instruction.

  • True Each node has its own private address space; the memory of another node is out of reach of loads and stores.
  • False Right: the variable must be requested and sent as a message (MPI_Send / MPI_Recv).
Show model answer

False. In a cluster every node has its own private memory and address space, and the memory of one node cannot be accessed directly by another node via loads and stores; data moves only through explicit messages, with MPI_Send and MPI_Recv.

The true/false form of June 2025. The same statement is true for a DSM; the word "cluster" decides.

Lesson 35 · The day the caches disagreed: coherence, snooping and the MSI machine

Lesson 35 · The day the caches disagreed: coherence, snooping and the MSI machine · question 1 of 6 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following techniques used to keep the private caches of a shared-memory multiprocessor coherent:

1. Caching of shared data (migration and replication); 2. Cache coherence; 3. Memory consistency; 4. Write-invalidate protocol; 5. Write-update protocol; 6. Snooping protocol; 7. Duplicate snoop tags; 8. MSI protocol.

Show model answer

1. A private cache keeps a copy of a block next to the core. For shared data it gives migration — the block moves to the cache of the core using it, so accesses no longer go to memory or over the interconnect — and replication — a read-shared block gets a copy in every reader's cache, so readers do not contend for one copy. Benefit: lower latency, less bus and memory traffic. Cost: several copies of one block, hence the coherence problem.

2. A memory system is coherent if (1) a processor that writes X and then reads X, with no other write in between, reads its own value; (2) a read of X by P2 after a write by P1 returns P1's value if enough time has passed and no other write intervened; (3) writes to the same location are serialized: all processors see them in the same order. It concerns one location at a time.

3. Consistency defines when a write becomes visible to the other processors and in which order loads and stores to different addresses may be observed. Example: P0 writes A = 1 then B = 2, P1 reads B then A: sequential consistency forbids reading B = 2 and A = 0, relaxed models allow it. A coherent memory need not be sequentially consistent.

4. Before writing a shared block, the writer broadcasts an invalidate; all other copies become Invalid and the writer gets exclusive ownership. Further writes are local and silent until another processor requests the block; that processor misses and gets the data from the owner or from memory. Benefit: traffic only on the first write — the choice of almost all modern multiprocessors, with write-back caches.

5. Every write broadcasts the new value to all caches holding the block (and usually to memory), which update their copies. Benefit: readers never miss and never see stale data. Cost: one bus transaction per write, also for repeated writes to one word or to the words of one block, so it wastes bandwidth; it pays off only for producer–consumer sharing.

6. The caches share a bus, which is a broadcast medium: every cache controller monitors every transaction and reacts if it holds the block (invalidates it or supplies the data). No central directory; one transaction informs all caches; the bus order serializes the writes. Suitable for small symmetric (UMA) multiprocessors; it does not scale, because broadcast traffic, electrical load and serialization grow with the number of processors.

7. A second copy of the cache's tag array — tag and coherence state, no data — used only by the snooper. Bus transactions are checked there while the processor keeps using the main tags, so snooping does not stall the pipeline: it removes the structural hazard on the tag array. Cost: area, and both copies must be updated together.

8. Each block is Modified (only copy, dirty), Shared (clean, possibly in other caches) or Invalid. A read miss puts a Read Miss on the bus (→ S), a write miss a Write Miss (→ M), a write hit in S an Invalidate (→ M); read hits and write hits in M are silent. A snooped Read Miss turns M into S with a write-back; a snooped Write Miss turns S or M into I (M supplies the block and writes it back first); a snooped Invalidate turns S into I. A replaced Modified block is written back, a replaced Shared block leaves silently.

This is the format of the 8-point question of the 22 June 2026 paper, with about five lines per sub-point. Each line should name the mechanism and its benefit; points 4–6 together answer the snooping half of Question 1 of the January 2026 paper as the notes reproduce it (pp. 495–497). The usual slip is in point 2: "every processor sees a write immediately" — the definition says "after enough time", and the timing belongs to consistency.

Lesson 35 · The day the caches disagreed: coherence, snooping and the MSI machine · question 2 of 6 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a single-chip dual-core shared-memory architecture where each core is a dual-issue superscalar processor with multi-threading up to 2 threads per core. Explain the preferred technique used to manage the cache coherency problem in the dual core chip.

Show model answer

A snooping, write-invalidate protocol with write-back caches (MSI, in practice MESI). The private caches of the two cores sit on a shared on-chip bus that acts as a broadcast medium: each cache controller monitors every transaction, with duplicate snoop tags, and keeps a coherence state per block. Before a core writes a block that the other core may hold, it broadcasts an invalidate, so the other copy becomes Invalid and later writes stay local. A read miss on a block that the other cache holds Modified is served by that cache, which writes the block back and goes to Shared. With two cores the broadcast is cheap and the bus serializes the writes, so no directory is needed — directories are for many nodes with distributed memory.

This is the last row of Question 2 (5 points in all) of the 10 June 2025 paper. A good answer names snooping, write-invalidate and write-back, and gives the reason: two cores on one chip share a broadcast medium. Naming MESI, the refinement of lesson 36, adds precision; a directory answers a different machine.

The wording is that of the MESI tables of the papers (Exercise 3 of January 2026 as the notes reproduce it, Quiz 7 of June 2026) with MSI in place of MESI. The four write-backs cover all three ways of leaving M. Under MESI (lesson 36) only cycle 0 would change: Excl (0).

Lesson 35 · The day the caches disagreed: coherence, snooping and the MSI machine · question 4 of 6 · open in the lesson

Processors P0 and P1 both hold block X in their write-back caches. P0 then writes X ten times in a row, while P1 does not access it. How many coherence transactions appear on the bus under a write-invalidate protocol and under a write-update protocol?

  • 10 and 10 Invalidate pays only for the first write: afterwards P0 owns the block and the other nine writes are silent hits.
  • 1 and 10 One Invalidate, then nine silent hits in M; update must broadcast each of the ten new values because P1 still holds a copy.
  • 10 and 1 The other way round: update cannot batch writes, every new value must reach P1's copy.
  • 0 and 0 P1 holds a copy, so at least the first write must invalidate or update it; otherwise P1 could read a stale value.
Show model answer

Answer: 1 and 10. Write-invalidate sends one invalidation for the first write; the block is then exclusively owned and the remaining writes are local. Write-update broadcasts every write.

This is the first of Hennessy & Patterson's three characteristics, in numbers, and the slope of the traffic meter. A "motivate your answer" line would be: invalidation is per block and per ownership, update per write.

Lesson 35 · The day the caches disagreed: coherence, snooping and the MSI machine · question 5 of 6 · open in the lesson

How does an MSI write-invalidate write-back protocol manage a Write Hit on a cache block which is Shared in another cache? (Multiple answers.)

  • The status of the cache block becomes Modified S → M: after the write this cache holds the only, dirty copy.
  • The cache block is retrieved from memory It is a hit: the data is already in the cache, nothing is fetched.
  • The cache block is retrieved from another cache Same reason: a write hit needs permission, not data.
  • An invalidate is broadcasted on the bus to the other copies of the block The other sharer must drop its copy: that is the S → M arrow, PrWr / Invalidate.
  • The cache block is overwritten in the processor's cache Write-back: the new value goes into the cache line only, and memory becomes stale.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 4 and 5: an invalidate is broadcast, the block is written in the cache and becomes Modified; nothing is retrieved because the block is already present.

Quiz 2 of the 22 June 2026 paper asks exactly this for MESI, and in MESI the answer is the same. Its Quiz 1 — a write hit on an Exclusive block — needs the E state of lesson 36.

Lesson 35 · The day the caches disagreed: coherence, snooping and the MSI machine · question 6 of 6 · open in the lesson

True or false: in an MSI snooping protocol with write-back caches, after a cache that holds a block in Modified state answers another processor's read miss, main memory holds an up-to-date copy of the block.

  • True The owner supplies the block and memory takes it from the bus: M → S with a write-back. Both copies are Shared, that is clean.
  • False Memory could stay stale only if a dirty block could be shared, which needs the Owned state of MOESI. In MSI a Shared copy is clean by definition.
Show model answer

True. On the snooped read miss the Modified copy is written back while it is supplied; both caches end in Shared and memory is up to date.

Row 3 of the table in Quiz 7 of the 22 June 2026 paper — one of its correct rows — prints exactly this: P3 reads block 0 while P1 holds it Modified, and "Memory at bl. 0 up to date?" becomes Yes. The notes argue the opposite on p. 455; see the red pen in section 5.

Lesson 36 · The E in MESI, and the exam table · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider the following access patterns on a 4-processor system with a direct-mapped, write-back cache with one cache block per processor and a two-cache block memory. Show model answer
CycleAfter OperationP0 cache block stateP1 cache block stateP2 cache block stateP3 cache block stateMemory at bl. 0 up to date?Memory at bl. 1 up to date?
1P0: Read Bl. 0Excl (0)InvalidInvalidInvalidYesYes
2P1: Read Bl. 0Shared (0)Shared (0)InvalidInvalidYesYes
3P1: Write Bl. 0InvalidMod (0)InvalidInvalidNoYes
4P2: Read Bl. 0InvalidShared (0)Shared (0)InvalidYesYes
5P2: Write Bl. 1InvalidShared (0)Mod (1)InvalidYesNo
6P1: Write Bl. 0InvalidMod (0)Mod (1)InvalidNoNo
7P1: Read Bl. 1InvalidShared (1)Shared (1)InvalidYesYes
8P3: Write Bl. 1InvalidInvalidInvalidMod (1)YesNo

Row 3: write hit in Shared, an Invalidate takes P0 to Invalid. Row 4: P1 writes block 0 back on P2's Read Miss. Row 5: P2's clean Shared (0) leaves silently, so P1 is left as the only holder of block 0 — still Shared. Row 6: P1 therefore broadcasts an Invalidate that nobody needs before going to Mod (0). Row 7: two write-backs in one row — P1 replaces its dirty block 0 (memory at bl. 0 back to Yes), and P2 writes block 1 back on P1's Read Miss. Row 8: the Write Miss invalidates both Shared (1) copies.

A drill in the exact format of the papers, built to contain the two cases their sequences avoid: a lone Shared copy that must still invalidate (row 6) and the replacement of a dirty block (row 7). The exam-table trainer of section 4 produces more drills of this kind (its "random drill").

Lesson 36 · The E in MESI, and the exam table · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

(June 2026, Question 1) How does a MESI write-invalidate write-back protocol manage a Write Hit on an Exclusive cache block? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • Answer 1: The status of the cache block becomes Modified Right: after any write the line is dirty, so E becomes M.
  • Answer 2: The cache block is retrieved from memory It is a hit: the block is already in the cache, and clean.
  • Answer 3: The cache block is retrieved from another cache No other cache holds it — that is what Exclusive means.
  • Answer 4: An invalidate is broadcasted on the bus to the other copies of the block There are no other copies: E → M is silent, and that is the whole point of the E state.
  • Answer 5: The cache block is overwritten in the processor's cache Right: with write-back the write updates the local copy only.
Show model answer

Answers 1 and 5. The block is present and clean and no other cache holds it, so the write is performed in the local cache and the state changes from Exclusive to Modified with no bus transaction: nothing is retrieved and nothing is invalidated.

The question tests the one advantage of MESI over MSI. Ticking Answer 4 is the classic mistake: it describes a write hit on a Shared block, which is the next question of the same paper.

Lesson 36 · The E in MESI, and the exam table · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

(June 2026, Question 2) How does a MESI write-invalidate write-back protocol manage a Write Hit on a cache block which is Shared in another cache? (MULTIPLE ANSWERS)

  • Answer 1: The status of the cache block becomes Modified Right: the writer ends with the only, dirty copy.
  • Answer 2: The cache block is retrieved from memory It is a hit: the data is already in the writer's cache.
  • Answer 3: The cache block is retrieved from another cache Same reason: nothing has to be fetched on a hit, only permission is missing.
  • Answer 4: An invalidate is broadcasted on the bus to the other copies of the block Right: the other Shared copies must become Invalid before the write.
  • Answer 5: The cache block is overwritten in the processor's cache Right: the write goes into the local copy (write-back).
Show model answer

Answers 1, 4 and 5. The block is present but not exclusive: the cache broadcasts an invalidate on the bus, the other copies become Invalid, the block is written in the local cache and its state becomes Modified. No data is transferred, because the writer already has the block.

Questions 1 and 2 of the paper differ in one answer, the invalidate. Answers 2 and 3 are the distractors for both: they belong to a miss, not to a hit.
