(June 2026, Question 7) Let's consider the following access patterns on a 4-processor system with a direct-mapped, write-back cache with one cache block per processor and a two-cache block memory. Assume the MESI protocol is used, with write-back caches, write-allocate, and write-invalidate of other caches.

CycleAfter OperationP0 cache block stateP1 cache block stateP2 cache block stateP3 cache block stateMemory at bl. 0 up to date?Memory at bl. 1 up to date?
0P0: write Bl. 1Mod (1)InvalidInvalidInvalidYesNo
1P1: Write Bl. 0Mod (1)Mod (0)InvalidInvalidNoNo
2P2: Write Bl. 1InvalidMod (0)Mod (1)InvalidNoNo
3P3: Read Bl. 0InvalidShared (0)Mod (1)Shared (0)YesNo
4P0: Read Bl. 1Shared (1)Shared (0)Shared (1)Shared (0)YesYes
5P2: Write Bl. 0Shared (1)Shared (0)Mod (0)Shared (0)NoYes

Which is the wrong line? (SINGLE ANSWER) Motivate your answer.

  • Answer 1: Line 1 Correct as printed: P1's write miss finds no other copy of block 0 and takes Mod (0); P0's Mod (1) is another block. Memory: No, No.
  • Answer 2: Line 2 Correct as printed: P0 writes block 1 back and is invalidated, P2 takes Mod (1), and memory at bl. 1 is No again because P2 writes.
  • Answer 3: Line 3 Correct as printed: P1 writes block 0 back on P3's Read Miss and both end Shared (0); memory at bl. 0 is Yes.
  • Answer 4: Line 4 Correct as printed: P2 writes block 1 back on P0's Read Miss, both end Shared (1), memory at bl. 1 is Yes.
  • Answer 5: Line 5 Wrong line: P2's write miss on block 0 must invalidate the Shared (0) copies of P1 and P3.
Show model answer

Answer 5 (Line 5). P2 holds Shared (1), which it replaces silently (clean). Its write on block 0 is a write miss: the write-invalidate protocol places a Write Miss on the bus and every other copy of block 0 must become Invalid, so P1 and P3 cannot remain Shared (0). P2 ends in Mod (0), memory at bl. 0 is not up to date and memory at bl. 1 is. The correct line is:

CycleAfter OperationP0 cache block stateP1 cache block stateP2 cache block stateP3 cache block stateMemory at bl. 0 up to date?Memory at bl. 1 up to date?
5P2: Write Bl. 0Shared (1)InvalidMod (0)InvalidNoYes

The paper leaves an empty row under the motivation lines: that is where the corrected line goes, and writing it is the quickest motivation. Lines 2–4 are the ones that look suspicious and are right — each relies on a write-back that turns the memory column, so check the memory cells with the rule "No exactly while some cache holds the block in Mod".

Lesson 36 · The E in MESI, and the exam table · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(January 2026, Question 1, as transcribed in the notes, p. 495) Let's consider the SNOOPING and the DIRECTORY-BASED protocols used to maintain the cache coherence in modern multiprocessor. Answer the following questions: (a) Explain the main characteristics of each protocol. (b) For which type of multiprocessor architecture is each protocol suitable for? (c) What are the main benefits of each protocol? (d) Explain the two types of Snooping protocol depending on what happens on a write operation. (e) What are the possible coherence states of a block in the home directory and the meaning of the sharer bits?

Show model answer

(a) Snooping: the caches share a broadcast medium, usually a bus. Every coherence request (read miss, write miss, invalidate) is broadcast; every cache controller snoops it, checks its own tags and acts locally — it invalidates its copy, writes a dirty copy back or downgrades it. There is no central record of who holds what, and the bus order serialises the writes. Directory-based: for every memory block a directory entry, kept at the block's home node next to its memory, records the coherence state and which caches hold a copy (the sharer bits). A request goes to the home, which sends point-to-point messages only to the caches involved; nothing is broadcast.

(b) Snooping suits small-scale centralized shared-memory multiprocessors (SMP, UMA) built around one bus, up to a few tens of processors. Directories suit large-scale distributed shared-memory machines (DSM, NUMA), where memory is physically distributed over many nodes joined by a point-to-point interconnection network.

(c) Snooping: simple, with no extra structure; low latency, since one broadcast reaches every cache; write serialization for free from the bus. Directory: scalable, because traffic grows with the number of actual sharers, not with the number of processors; it works with physically distributed memory and any interconnect.

(d) Write-invalidate: before writing, the writer broadcasts an invalidate, every other copy becomes Invalid and the writer holds the only copy, so later writes by the same processor cause no traffic (MSI, MESI). It is the dominant choice because it saves bandwidth. Write-update (write-broadcast): every write broadcasts the new value and every cache holding the block updates its copy; readers never miss, but every write costs bus bandwidth, even when nobody reads the block again.

(e) Uncached: no cache holds the block, memory is up to date. Shared: one or more caches hold clean, read-only copies, memory is up to date. Modified: exactly one cache, the owner, holds the block with write permission, and memory may be stale. The sharer bits are one bit per node (N0 N1 N2 N3, left to right): 1 means that node's cache holds a copy, and in Modified exactly one bit is set, naming the owner. They let the home send invalidations and fetches only to the nodes that hold the block, instead of broadcasting.

What a complete answer contains: the broadcast-versus-point-to-point contrast stated as a mechanism, the matching architectures (SMP / UMA against DSM / NUMA), two benefits for each, the two write policies with their trade-off (bandwidth against read latency), and the three directory states with whether memory is fresh in each. The directory half is developed in lessons 37 and 38. The notes' own answer (pp. 495–497) is sound and uses the papers' name, Modified, for the third directory state. Question 2 of the same paper, on dynamic branch prediction, is drilled in lesson 5.

Lesson 36 · The E in MESI, and the exam table · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of the MESI protocol with respect to MSI: (1) the four states and their meaning; (2) what happens on a read miss; (3) what happens on a write hit in each valid state; (4) when a block is written back to memory; (5) what the new state buys and what it costs in hardware.

Show model answer

(1) Modified: the only copy, dirty — memory is stale. Exclusive: the only copy, clean — it matches memory. Shared: a clean copy that other caches may also hold. Invalid: no valid copy. MSI has only M, S and I, so after a read miss a block always enters S, even when no other cache holds it.

(2) The miss is broadcast as a Read Miss and every other cache checks its tags. If none holds the block, the requester receives it in Exclusive; if one does, in Shared. A holder in Exclusive drops to Shared; a holder in Modified writes the block back, so memory becomes up to date, and drops to Shared.

(3) In Modified the write proceeds locally, with nothing on the bus. In Exclusive the line becomes Modified silently, with no bus transaction, because no other copy exists. In Shared the cache broadcasts an Invalidate, every other copy becomes Invalid and the line becomes Modified — as in MSI.

(4) Only from Modified: when the line is replaced; when another processor's Read Miss for the block is snooped (M → S); when another processor's Write Miss is snooped (M → I). Exclusive, Shared and Invalid lines are never written back.

(5) Benefit: private data — stack data, local variables, many heap objects — is read and then written by one processor. MSI pays a Read Miss plus a useless Invalidate per block and makes the first write wait for the bus; MESI pays only the Read Miss and writes at once, saving bus bandwidth and write latency. Cost: the requester must learn whether another cache holds the block, so the bus needs a shared line that every snooper holding the block asserts during a read miss; the four states still fit in two state bits per line.

The format of the June 2026 open question ("explain the main concepts and benefits"), applied to this lesson. What a complete answer contains: E defined as exclusive and clean; read miss into E only when no other cache holds the block; E → M without bus traffic; the three write-back cases; the benefit stated as bandwidth and latency on private data; the shared line as the cost.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 1 of 7 · open in the lesson

Explain the main concepts and benefits of each one of the following elements of a directory-based cache coherence protocol for a distributed shared memory multiprocessor: 1. The directory and where it is stored; 2. The directory states of a block; 3. The sharer bits; 4. The home node; 5. The local node and the remote nodes; 6. Point-to-point messages instead of a broadcast; 7. The Fetch and Fetch/Invalidate messages; 8. The Data Write-Back message.

Show model answer
  1. A hardware table in the memory controller of each node, with one entry per memory block of that node's memory, recording the coherence state of the block and which caches hold copies. Logically one place per block (its home); physically distributed with the memories, so the lookups are spread over the nodes. Benefit: coherence without a broadcast medium, scalable to large DSM machines. Cost: storage proportional to blocks × nodes.
  2. Uncached: no cache holds the block, memory has the only valid copy. Shared: one or more caches hold read-only copies, memory is up to date. Modified: exactly one cache, the owner, holds a read-write copy, memory is stale. Benefit: from the state alone the home knows whether it can answer from memory (Uncached, Shared) or must get the block from the owner (Modified). The cache-side states are Invalid, Shared, Modified.
  3. One bit per node, N0 N1 N2 N3 from left to right: bit i = 1 if node i's cache holds a valid copy. All zeros in Uncached, one or more ones in Shared, exactly one in Modified (the owner). Benefit: the home sends invalidations or fetches only to the nodes involved instead of broadcasting. Cost: P bits per block, (P + 2)/(8B) of the memory — 12.9 % for 64 nodes and 64-byte blocks.
  4. The node whose main memory holds the block, fixed by the physical address; it stores the block and its directory entry. Every request for the block passes through its directory, which knows the sharers and the owner and serves the requests to that block one at a time, replacing the order of a bus. Benefit: the place to ask is known from the address alone.
  5. The local node is where the request originates: its processor misses on the block or writes a Shared copy. A remote node already holds a cached copy, Shared or Modified; if Modified it has the only fresh value. Roles belong to one transaction: a node can be home and local at once (a request for a block of its own memory) or home and remote (its own cache holds a copy).
  6. Requests go from the local node to the home only; the home sends Invalidate, Fetch or Fetch/Invalidate only to the nodes in the sharer bits; data comes back as Data Value Reply or Data Write-Back. Traffic grows with the number of actual sharers, not with the number of processors, and no cache looks up requests that do not concern it. Cost: one indirection through the home (more hops, more latency) and the directory storage.
  7. Both are sent by the home to the owner of a Modified block and carry only the address. Fetch serves a Read Miss: the owner returns the data and keeps a read-only copy (Modified → Shared). Fetch/Invalidate serves a Write Miss: the owner returns the data and invalidates its copy (Modified → Invalid), and the requester becomes the new owner. Benefit: the fresh copy is recovered from the only place that holds it.
  8. Sent by a remote owner to the home with the address and the dirty data: in reply to a Fetch or a Fetch/Invalidate, or when the owner evicts a Modified block. The home updates its memory and the entry and, for a request, forwards the data to the requester with a Data Value Reply. It is the only message from a remote node to the home: Shared copies are clean and never send data back.

The form of June 2026: eight sub-points, about five lines each. A complete sub-point names the mechanism, gives one concrete detail (a state, a bit pattern, a message name) and states the benefit with its cost. Three slips to avoid: calling the directory "a central table", listing Invalid among the directory states, and forgetting that Data Write-Back is also the reply to a Fetch.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 2 of 7 · open in the lesson

Consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3), each node with processor, cache, main memory and directory (the figure of Fig. 1). Please answer the following questions:

  1. What is the definition of home node?
  2. What is the definition of local node?
  3. What is the definition of remote node?
  4. What are the possible messages sent from the local node to the home node?
  5. What are the possible messages sent from the home node to the local node?
  6. What are the possible messages sent from the home node to the remote node?
  7. What are the possible messages sent from the remote node to the home node?
  8. What are the possible coherence states of a block in the local cache?
  9. What are the possible coherence states of a block in the home directory?
  10. What is the meaning of the sharer bits of a block in the home directory?
Show model answer
  1. Home node: the node whose main memory holds the block (fixed by the physical address) and whose directory keeps the block's state and sharer bits; every request for the block goes through it.
  2. Local node: the node whose processor issues the request; the read miss, the write miss or the write hit on a Shared copy originates in its cache.
  3. Remote node: a node that holds a cached copy of the block, Shared or Modified (the owner).
  4. Local → home: Read Miss (P, A); Write Miss (P, A); Invalidate (A), sent on a write hit to a block the local cache holds in Shared.
  5. Home → local: Data Value Reply (D).
  6. Home → remote: Invalidate (A); Fetch (A) — the owner returns the block and goes to Shared; Fetch/Invalidate (A) — the owner returns the block and invalidates its copy.
  7. Remote → home: Data Write-Back (A, D) — the owner's dirty block, in reply to a Fetch or a Fetch/Invalidate, or on eviction.
  8. States in the local cache: 1) Invalid, 2) Shared, 3) Modified.
  9. States in the home directory: 1) Uncached, 2) Shared, 3) Modified.
  10. Sharer bits: a bit vector with one bit per node (N0 N1 N2 N3); bit i = 1 if node i's cache holds a copy. All 0 in Uncached, one or more 1s in Shared, exactly one 1 — the owner — in Modified. They let the home send messages only to the caches involved instead of broadcasting.

Question 1 of July 2024, in its own order. Three things to get right: the home is fixed by the address and not by who asks; Invalidate is listed in both directions (local → home for a write hit on a Shared copy, home → remote to drop a copy); and the two lists of states are not mixed — Invalid belongs to the caches, Uncached to the directory. Lesson 38 drills the same question next to the walkthroughs.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 3 of 7 · open in the lesson

Let's consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 Nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3) where:

Directory N2 Block B2 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 0110 |

For each of the following accesses, each one applied to this initial entry, answer: Home node? Local node? Remote node(s)? What is the initial message sent from the local node to the home node? Which message(s) are sent from the home node to the remote node(s)? Is(are) there any message(s) sent from the remote node(s) to the home node?

(a) Read Miss on B2 from node N0. (b) Write Miss on B2 from node N3. (c) Write Hit on B2 from node N1.

Answer the same questions starting from Directory N2 Block B2 | State: Modified | Sharer Bits: 1000 | for (d) Read Miss on B2 from node N3 and (e) Write Miss on B2 from node N2.

Show model answer

Home node: N2 for every access, because B2 lives in MM2.

AccessLocal nodeRemote node(s)Local → homeHome → remote(s)Remote → home
(a)N0N1, N2 (not contacted)Read Miss (P0, B2)nonenone
(b)N3N1, N2Write Miss (P3, B2)Invalidate (B2) to N1 and to C2none
(c)N1N2Invalidate (B2)Invalidate (B2) to C2none
(d)N3N0 (owner)Read Miss (P3, B2)Fetch (B2)Data Write-Back (B2, data)
(e)N2N0 (owner)Write Miss (P2, B2)Fetch/Invalidate (B2)Data Write-Back (B2, data)

In (a)–(c) N2 is a remote too, because its own cache C2 holds a copy; its Invalidate in (b) and (c) stays inside the node. In (a) nothing goes to the remotes: in Shared, MM2 is up to date and the home replies by itself. In (c) N1 holds a Shared copy, so it is the local node of a write hit. In (e) home and local coincide: the Write Miss and the reply stay inside N2. Nothing returns from Shared copies, which are clean; only the owner N0 returns the block in (d) and (e). The home answers (a), (b), (d) and (e) with a Data Value Reply; in (c) no data is sent, because N1 already holds the block.

The first half of the papers' directory exercise: who plays which part, and which messages exist. Lesson 38 adds the full order of the messages and the final state and sharer bits. The traps: in (a)–(c) N2 is home and remote at once; (c) starts with an Invalidate, not a Write Miss, and needs no Data Value Reply; in (e) home and local coincide, so the Write Miss and the reply never use the network.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) Let's consider the directory-based protocol for multiprocessors. Which is the message type that can be sent from the remote node to the home node of a block?

  • Answer 1: Data value reply It travels home → local: the home's reply that brings the block to the requester.
  • Answer 2: Data write back Right: the owner returns its dirty block to the home, after a Fetch or a Fetch/Invalidate or on eviction.
  • Answer 3: Invalidate Home → remote (drop your copy) or local → home (write hit on a Shared copy); never remote → home.
  • Answer 4: Fetch/Invalidate A command of the home to the owner: home → remote.
Show model answer

Answer 2. Data write back is the only message a remote node sends to the home: the owner's reply to a Fetch or a Fetch/Invalidate, or the write-back of an evicted Modified block.

Quiz 1 of June 2025 as printed, with its typo "form" read as "from". Sort every option by its direction (Fig. 4) and only one survives.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) In a directory-based protocol, which message types can be sent from the home node to a remote node of a block?

  • Answer 1: Read Miss A request of the local node to the home: local → home.
  • Answer 2: Invalidate Right: the home tells a sharer to drop its copy.
  • Answer 3: Fetch Right: the home asks the owner of a Modified block for the data on a Read Miss.
  • Answer 4: Data Write-Back The opposite direction: remote (owner) → home.
  • Answer 5: Fetch/Invalidate Right: the home asks the owner for the data and makes it invalidate its copy, on a Write Miss.
Show model answer

Answers 2, 3 and 5. Read Miss goes local → home and Data Write-Back remote → home.

The July 2024 question "possible messages sent from the home node to the remote node" turned into a multiple-answer quiz. The trap is Invalidate: it also exists local → home, but it is correct here because it exists home → remote too.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(True/False) In a directory-based protocol, when the state of a block in the home directory is Modified, the copy of the block in the main memory of the home node is up to date.

  • True In Modified the owner has written its copy, and memory has not seen the write.
  • False Right: Modified means one owner with a dirty copy; the memory copy is stale until the owner's Data Write-Back.
Show model answer

False. Memory is up to date in Uncached and Shared; in Modified the only fresh copy is in the owner's cache, which is why the home must send a Fetch or a Fetch/Invalidate before it can reply.

Motivate with the reason the state exists: it tells the home where the valid data is.

Lesson 37 · The librarian: directory-based protocols, roles and messages · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

A distributed shared memory system has 32 nodes. Each node has 2 GB (231 bytes) of main memory, the block size is 64 bytes, and every home directory keeps, for each of its memory blocks, 2 state bits and one sharer bit per node. What percentage of the main memory does the directory occupy?

Show model answer
\[ \frac{2^{31}}{2^{6}} = 2^{25} \ \text{blocks per node} \] \[ 32 + 2 = 34 \ \text{bits per entry} \] \[ \frac{34}{8 \cdot 64} = \frac{34}{512} \approx 6.64\,\% \]

Per node: \(34 \times 2^{25}\) bits = \(136 \times 2^{20}\) bytes (136 MB) of directory for 2 GB of memory. With 256 nodes the same blocks need 258 bits each: 258 / 512 ≈ 50.4 %.

Write the entry size first, then divide by the bits of one block: the memory size cancels, so the 2 GB only matters if the question also asks for the size in bytes. The typical slip is dividing by 64 instead of 8 × 64 = 512 bits.

Lesson 38 · Four nodes, one block: directory walkthroughs and the exam exercise

(22 June 2026, Exercise 3) Consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3). Let's consider: Directory N0 Block B0 | State: SHARED | Sharer Bits: 1001|. Assuming a Write Miss on B0 from N2, please answer to the following questions:

  1. What is the home node?
  2. What is local node?
  3. What is(are) the remote node(s)?
  4. What is the initial message sent from the local node to the home node?
  5. What is the reply message sent from the home node to the local node?
  6. What is the reply message sent from the home node to the remote node(s)?

  8. What is the final coherence state of the block in the home cache?
  9. What is the final coherence state of the block in the local cache?
  10. What are the final coherence state and the sharers bits of the block in the home directory?

QuestionAnswer
1. Home nodeN0: B0 is in MM0 and its entry in D0.
2. Local nodeN2: the node that issues the Write Miss.

4. Initial message, local → homeWrite Miss (P = N2, A = B0), from N2 to N0.

Each box takes one line. The three traps of the form: N0 counted among the remotes, the "no" with its reason (clean copies), and Invalid — not Uncached — for the home cache. You may add that a real machine sends invalidation acknowledgements; the message set of the course has none.

Remote node(s)N1: C1 holds a copy
Sequence of messages1. N2 → N1 Invalidate (B1): the request to invalidate the other copies
2. N1 → C1, inside N1: Invalidate (B1)
No data is sent: N2 already has the block.
B1 in the home directoryState: Modified | Sharer Bits: 0 0 1 0 (owner N2)

3. Write Miss on B1 from node N0

Home nodeN1
Local nodeN0
Remote node(s)N2: the owner, Modified
Sequence of messages1. N0 → N1 Write Miss (N0, B1)
2. N1 → N2 Fetch/Invalidate (B1)
3. N2 → N1 Data Write-Back (B1, data); C2: Modified → Invalid; MM1 updated
4. N1 → N0 Data Value Reply (B1)
B1 in the home directoryState: Modified | Sharer Bits: 1 0 0 0 (owner N0)

Three shapes in one exercise: two messages, 1 + k, four. The classic slips are answering the second access with a Write Miss and a Data Value Reply (the block is already in C2) and forgetting, in the first, that the only copy is the home's own. Writing "N1 (the home's own cache), not contacted" for the first remote leaves no doubt about what you mean.

Given the following sequence of accesses, please answer to the following questions:

Not from a paper: the June 2025 pattern with the home's own copy invalidated in the first access and a Fetch in the second. The second access is the case the notes get wrong (pp. 473 and 477): after the Data Write-Back the memory of N2 is up to date, and both copies are Shared.

Lesson 38 · Four nodes, one block: directory walkthroughs and the exam exercise · question 4 of 7 · open in the lesson

(15 July 2024, Question 1) Consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3). Please answer to the following questions:

  1. What is the definition of home node?
  2. What is the definition of local node?
  3. What is the definition of remote node?
  4. What are the possible messages sent from the local node to the home node?
  5. What are the possible messages sent from the home node to the local node?
  6. What are the possible messages sent from the home node to the remote node?
  7. What are the possible messages sent from the remote node to the home node?
  8. What are the possible coherence states of a block in the local cache?
  9. What are the possible coherence states of a block in the home directory?
  10. What is the meaning of the sharer bits of a block in the home directory?
Show model answer
QuestionAnswer
1. Home nodeThe node whose main memory holds the block, fixed by the physical address; its directory keeps the block's state and sharer bits, and every request for the block goes through it.
2. Local nodeThe node whose processor issues the request: the read or write miss originates in its cache.
3. Remote nodeA node whose cache holds a copy of the block, Shared or Modified. The home may have to invalidate it (a sharer) or fetch the data from it (the owner); the home itself can also be a remote.
4. Local → homeRead Miss (P, A); Write Miss (P, A); Invalidate (A), the request sent on a write hit to a Shared block.
5. Home → localData Value Reply (D): the block, in answer to a Read Miss or a Write Miss.
6. Home → remoteInvalidate (A); Fetch (A): the owner returns the block and goes to Shared; Fetch/Invalidate (A): the owner returns the block and invalidates it.
7. Remote → homeData Write-Back (A, D): the owner's modified block, in reply to a Fetch or a Fetch/Invalidate, or when the owner evicts it.
8. States in the local cache1) Invalid 2) Shared 3) Modified.
9. States in the home directory1) Uncached 2) Shared 3) Modified.
10. Sharer bitsOne bit per node (N0 N1 N2 N3): bit i = 1 means that node i's cache holds a copy. In Uncached all bits are 0; in Shared they mark all the sharers; in Modified exactly one bit is set and it marks the owner. They let the home send Invalidate and Fetch messages only to the nodes concerned, with no broadcast.

Ten short answers. The marks hide in the directions: Invalidate appears twice (local → home and home → remote), and the remote → home list has a single entry, Data Write-Back — the June 2025 quiz asked exactly that. Keep the two lists of states apart: Invalid belongs to caches, Uncached to the directory.

Lesson 38 · Four nodes, one block: directory walkthroughs and the exam exercise · question 5 of 7 · open in the lesson

(15 July 2024, Question 4, SINGLE ANSWER) Let's consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 Nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3) where: Directory N1 Block B1 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 1001. Which is the state of the block B1 in N1 after this sequence: Read Miss B1 from local cache N1; Read Miss B1 from local cache N2; Write Hit B1 from local cache N1;

  • Answer 1: Directory N1 Block B1 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 1111 That is the entry after the two read misses; the write hit by N1 then invalidates the other three copies.
  • Answer 2: Directory N1 Block B1 | State: Modified | Sharer Bits: 0100 1001 → 1101 (N1 reads, local and home at once) → 1111 (N2 reads) → the write hit by N1 invalidates N0, N2 and N3: Modified, owner N1.
  • Answer 3: Directory N1 Block B1 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 0110 A read never removes a sharer: N0 and N3 keep their copies until the write hit, and after it the state is Modified.
  • Answer 4: Directory N1 Block B1 | State: Modified | Sharer Bits: 0110 In Modified exactly one bit is set: the owner's.
Show model answer

Answer 2. Read Miss from N1: N1 is local and home, the block comes from MM1 and N1's bit is set: Shared | 1101. Read Miss from N2: Data Value Reply from MM1: Shared | 1111. Write Hit from N1: N1 holds a Shared copy, so it sends an Invalidate request to the home (itself), which invalidates N0, N2 and N3: Modified | 0100.

Two traps: in the first access local = home, which changes nothing in the rule — N1's bit is set like any reader's, 1001 → 1101; the last is a write hit, so the final state is Modified with one bit, whatever the bits were before.

Lesson 38 · Four nodes, one block: directory walkthroughs and the exam exercise · question 6 of 7 · open in the lesson

(SINGLE ANSWER) Let's consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 Nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3) where: Directory N3 Block B3 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 0110. Which is the state of the block B3 in N3 after this sequence: Write Miss B3 from local cache N0; Read Miss B3 from local cache N2;

  • Answer 1: Directory N3 Block B3 | State: Modified | Sharer Bits: 1000 That is the entry after the write miss alone; the read miss from N2 then fetches the block from N0.
  • Answer 2: Directory N3 Block B3 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 1010 The write miss invalidates N1 and N2 (Modified | 1000); the read miss from N2 fetches from the owner N0, which keeps a Shared copy.
  • Answer 3: Directory N3 Block B3 | State: Shared | Sharer Bits: 1110 N1's copy was invalidated by the write miss and was never read again.
  • Answer 4: Directory N3 Block B3 | State: Modified | Sharer Bits: 0010 A read miss never makes the reader the owner: both copies end Shared.
Show model answer

Answer 2. Write Miss from N0: the home N3 invalidates N1 and N2 and replies to N0: Modified | 1000. Read Miss from N2: the home sends a Fetch to N0, which returns a Data Write-Back and goes to Shared; the home replies to N2: Shared | 1010, memory up to date.

The July 2024 quiz pattern with a read on a Modified block. The wrong options are the entry after one access (Answer 1), a forgotten invalidation (Answer 3) and a reader made owner (Answer 4).

Lesson 38 · Four nodes, one block: directory walkthroughs and the exam exercise · question 7 of 7 · open in the lesson

(MULTIPLE ANSWERS) Let's consider a directory-based protocol for a distributed shared memory system with 4 Nodes (N0, N1, N2, N3) where: Directory N0 Block B0 | State: Modified | Sharer Bits: 0100. A Read Miss on B0 arrives from node N2. Which of the following statements are true?

  • Answer 1: The home node N0 sends a Fetch message to N1. N1 is the owner: the only node with the fresh value.
  • Answer 2: N1 sends a Data Write-Back to N0 and keeps its copy in Shared state. A Fetch downgrades the owner; a Fetch/Invalidate would invalidate it.
  • Answer 3: N1 sends the block directly to N2 with a Data Value Reply. In the protocol of the course the home replies: owner → home Data Write-Back, then home → local Data Value Reply.
  • Answer 4: At the end of the transaction, the copy of B0 in MM0 is up to date. The Data Write-Back wrote the block into MM0; the final state is Shared, so memory is fresh.
  • Answer 5: At the end of the transaction, the directory entry is Modified | 0010. N2 only reads: the entry becomes Shared | 0110.
Show model answer

Answers 1, 2 and 4. The sequence is N2 → N0 Read Miss, N0 → N1 Fetch, N1 → N0 Data Write-Back (N1: Modified → Shared, MM0 updated), N0 → N2 Data Value Reply. Final entry: Shared | 0110, memory up to date.

A multiple-answer quiz in the June 2026 form. Answer 4 is the statement the notes get wrong (pp. 473 and 477); Answer 3 describes a real-machine optimization, not the protocol of the papers.
